# Beyond diffusion: Flow Matching, step by step 
### Learn a *velocity field* that carries noise onto data - from scratch, on a laptop CPU

> **Scope note.** The notebook is named after the talk title *"Beyond Diffusion: Flow Matching for Generative AI"*; I could not watch the video, so this is **not** a transcript or a summary of it. It teaches flow matching from first principles, using the original papers, and **every claim is tested in a cell** (look for `PASS` lines and the table at the end).

**Papers used**
- Lipman et al., *Flow Matching for Generative Modeling* (ICLR 2023, [arXiv 2210.02747](https://arxiv.org/abs/2210.02747)) - the CFM loss, Eq. (9) and (23).
- Liu et al., *Flow Straight and Fast: Rectified Flow* ([arXiv 2209.03003](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.03003)) - straight paths and *reflow*.
- (also relevant: Albergo & Vanden-Eijnden, *Building Normalizing Flows with Stochastic Interpolants*, [arXiv 2209.15571](https://arxiv.org/abs/2209.15571))

| step | what you will do |
|---|---|
| 1 | state the problem: move a noise cloud onto the data |
| 2 | the idea: a velocity field and an ODE (and what changes versus diffusion) |
| 3 | build the straight path and its target velocity - and test it |
| 4 | *why regressing on a per-pair target works*: an exact 1-D check with a closed-form answer |
| 5 | train the 2-D model (a few seconds) |
| 6 | sample with an ODE solver; **how many steps do you need?** |
| 7 | flow vs a diffusion-style path, measured |
| 8 | straight paths and *reflow* -> one-step generation |
| 9 | conditioning and classifier-free guidance |
| 10 | where this appears in this repo (discrete flow matching for text) and in driving policies |

## Step 1 - The problem: turn noise into data

A generative model must produce *new* samples that look like the training data. Flow matching (like diffusion) does it by **transport**: start from something trivially easy to sample, **noise** $x_0 \sim \mathcal N(0, I)$, and *move every point* until the cloud looks like the data $x_1 \sim q$.

We use the classic **two moons** toy set. It is small enough to see every point, yet has the feature that makes generation non-trivial: two separate curved modes.

## Step 2 - The idea: a velocity field and an ODE

Think of every noise point as a particle. We want a rule that says, for a particle at position $x$ at time $t \in [0, 1]$, **which way to move and how fast**:

$$\frac{dx}{dt} = v_\theta(x, t), \qquad x(0) = x_0 \sim \mathcal N(0, I).$$

This is an ordinary differential equation (a *continuous normalizing flow*). If $v_\theta$ is right, integrating it from $t=0$ to $t=1$ drops the noise cloud on the data. **Generation = solving an ODE**; the network only has to output a 2-D arrow.

*How is this different from diffusion?* Diffusion trains a network to undo gradual noising (predict the noise or the score), and the paths it implies are **curved** and need many small steps. Flow matching says: *choose the path yourself* - the simplest one, a straight line - and regress the velocity along it. Lipman et al. state the contrast in their Fig. 2:

![Lipman et al. Fig. 2](../../assets/flow_matching/lipman_fig2_diffusion_vs_ot.png)

**Lipman et al. (2023), Fig. 2 (PDF p. 6):** "Compared to the diffusion path's conditional score function, the OT path's conditional vector field has constant direction in time and is arguably simpler to fit with a parametric model."

*What to look for:* on the right (the straight "OT" path) the arrows keep pointing at the same place for all $t$; on the left (diffusion) the picture changes with time.

## Step 3 - The straight path and its target velocity

Pick **one noise point $x_0$ and one data point $x_1$** and connect them with a straight line, parametrised by time:

$$x_t = (1-t)\,x_0 + t\,x_1, \qquad \frac{dx_t}{dt} = x_1 - x_0 =: u .$$

So the **target velocity along this path is a constant, $u = x_1 - x_0$** - the arrow from noise to data. (Lipman et al. Eq. (22)-(23) with $\sigma_{\min}\to 0$.)

Verifier: the path hits both endpoints, and a finite-difference derivative equals $u$.

Notice the grey lines **cross each other**. That is fine for a pairing, but a *single* velocity field $v(x,t)$ can only give **one** arrow at each point - so where lines cross, the network will learn their **average**. That averaging is exactly the subtle part, and it is what Step 4 checks.

## Step 4 - Why regressing on a per-pair target works (an exact check)

The training loss is the **Conditional Flow Matching** loss (Lipman et al., Eq. (9)), here with the straight path:

$$\boxed{\;\mathcal L(\theta) = \mathbb E_{t\sim U[0,1],\;x_0\sim\mathcal N,\;x_1\sim q}\;\big\|\,v_\theta(x_t,t) - (x_1 - x_0)\,\big\|^2\;}$$

Lipman et al. (PDF p. 5): *"we consider the Conditional Flow Matching (CFM) objective $\mathcal L_{CFM}(\theta)=\mathbb E_{t,q(x_1),p_t(x|x_1)}\|v_t(x)-u_t(x|x_1)\|^2$ (9) ... which surprisingly will result in the same optima as the original objective."*

Why? A squared-error regression is minimised by the **conditional mean**, so the optimum is

$$v^\*(x,t) = \mathbb E\big[\,x_1 - x_0 \;\big|\; x_t = x\,\big]$$

- the *average* of all pair-velocities passing through $(x,t)$. That average is exactly the *marginal* velocity field that transports the whole noise distribution onto the whole data distribution (Lipman et al. Eq. (8)).

**A closed form to test it against.** Take 1-D data with just two values, $x_1\in\{-1,+1\}$ (equal probability), and $x_0\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$. Given $x_t=x$, Bayes gives $P(x_1=+1\mid x)=\sigma\!\big(2tx/(1-t)^2\big)$, so $\mathbb E[x_1\mid x]=\tanh\!\big(tx/(1-t)^2\big)$, and since $x_0=(x-tx_1)/(1-t)$:

$$v^\*(x,t)=\frac{\tanh\!\big(\tfrac{t\,x}{(1-t)^2}\big)-x}{1-t}.$$

Two independent tests: (a) bin Monte-Carlo pair velocities and compare with $v^\*$; (b) train a small network with the loss above and compare it with $v^\*$.

**Read the plot.** The network never saw $v^*$; it only saw noisy per-pair arrows - and it learned their *average*, i.e. the true transport field. That is why such a simple regression is enough, and why training needs **no ODE solver** ("simulation-free").

## Step 5 - Train the 2-D model
Same loss, same network class, now on two moons. Each step: sample noise $x_0$, data $x_1$, time $t$; build $x_t$ and target $u$; regress.

*Why the loss does not reach 0:* the target $x_1-x_0$ depends on the *pair*, but the network only sees the *position*. Crossing lines (Step 3) make the per-pair target unpredictable, so the best possible loss is the irreducible variance around the average. A flat floor is therefore healthy, not a bug.

## Step 6 - Sampling = solving the ODE (and how many steps you need)

To generate: draw $x_0\sim\mathcal N(0,I)$ and integrate $\dot x = v_\theta(x,t)$ from $0$ to $1$.

- **Euler** with $N$ steps: $x \leftarrow x + h\,v_\theta(x,t)$, $h=1/N$ - one network call per step.
- **Heun** (2nd order): average the slope at the start and at the predicted end - two calls per step, but far more accurate.

The only knob is the number of function evaluations (**NFE**). We score with the **sliced Wasserstein distance** (SW) to fresh data (lower is better); the floor is the SW between two *independent* samples of the data itself.

**Read the plots.** One Euler step ($N=1$) puts every point on the *average* arrow, giving a blob in the middle; each doubling of steps bends the cloud closer to the moons; by $N\!\approx\!32$-$64$ it is indistinguishable from real data. The second-order solver reaches the same quality with fewer calls.

### The animation: noise flowing onto the data
Each dot is one noise sample; we advance the ODE and draw the cloud (and 40 trails) at every step.

![noise flowing onto two moons](../../assets/flow_matching/flow_particles.gif)

*What to look for:* the cloud first **pulls in toward the middle** and then **fans out** into the two moons (orange = arrived). The grey trails are visibly *curved* - the flow "rewires" crossing paths (Step 3), which Step 7 measures as path/chord about 1.6 and Step 8 straightens. The blue bar is time.

## Step 7 - Flow vs a diffusion-style path (measured, not assumed)

"Beyond diffusion" does **not** mean a different family: diffusion's forward process is just a *different probability path*. Lipman et al. show the variance-preserving diffusion path is the special case with a trigonometric schedule. Here is a cosine path in the same time convention (t=0 noise, t=1 data):

$$x_t=\sin\!\big(\tfrac{\pi t}{2}\big)\,x_1+\cos\!\big(\tfrac{\pi t}{2}\big)\,x_0,\qquad u_t=\tfrac{\pi}{2}\big(\cos(\tfrac{\pi t}{2})\,x_1-\sin(\tfrac{\pi t}{2})\,x_0\big).$$

The **only** code change is the `path` argument. Both models share architecture, optimiser and budget; we repeat with **3 seeds**. Two measurements: few-step sample quality (SW, lower is better) and **straightness** of the *learned* flow = (trajectory length) / (distance between its endpoints); 1.0 is perfectly straight.

> **Prediction from the papers:** straight conditional paths should be "simpler to fit" and need fewer solver steps (Lipman et al. Fig. 2, Fig. 7). Let's see whether this 2-D toy agrees.

**The honest reading (this contradicts the simple story).**

- **The "straight path => fewer steps" prediction is *not* reproduced in this toy.** The cosine path gave better few-step Euler samples in all 3 seeds, and its learned trajectories are *straighter*, not more curved.
- **Why the N=1 row is so bad for the straight path:** the first step uses the velocity at $t=0$, which is the *average* arrow $\mathbb E[x_1]-x_0\approx -x_0$. One Euler step therefore lands every point on the data mean - a tiny blob (the check above measures its spread), which scores *worse than raw noise*. The cosine path's field is ~0 at $t=0$ (its target depends on $x_1$ only there), so its first step barely moves the noise - bad, but not a collapse.
- **"Straight conditional paths" is not "straight learned flow".** The *training targets* are straight lines, but the lines cross; the learned marginal flow bends around the crossings (Step 3). Straightness ~1.6 above is exactly that bend - and exactly what **reflow** (next step) removes.
- **What the papers actually claim** is for high-dimensional images (ImageNet, CIFAR). A 2-D, two-mode toy does not have the geometry where the OT path's advantage shows up, so treat this as a caution against over-generalising a slogan - not as evidence against the papers. With 64+ steps both paths work, which is why diffusion models work too: they simply pay more steps.

## Step 8 - Straighter still: *reflow* -> one-step generation

Even the straight-line **training targets** cross; the *learned flow* un-crosses them by "rewiring" - and that is why Step 7 measured a bent flow (path/chord about 1.6). Liu et al. exploit that: after training, run the flow to get pairs $(x_0,\,x_1'=\mathrm{ODE}(x_0))$ - noise points matched to the sample they actually land on. **These pairs do not cross**, so a second model trained on them (initialised from the first) learns almost straight paths.

![Rectified flow Fig. 2](../../assets/flow_matching/rectflow_fig2_reflow.png)

**Liu et al., Fig. 2 (PDF p. 4):** "(a) Linear interpolation of data input $(X_0,X_1)\sim\pi_0\times\pi_1$. (b) The rectified flow $Z_t$ induced by $(X_0,X_1)$; the trajectories are 'rewired' at the intersection points to avoid the crossing. (c) The linear interpolation of the end points $(Z_0,Z_1)$ of flow $Z_t$. (d) The rectified flow induced from $(Z_0,Z_1)$, which follows straight paths."

![three flows](../../assets/flow_matching/three_flows.gif)

*What to look for:* the **green** (reflowed) trails are near-straight lines from grey start to orange end; the blue ones (straight-path flow) bend and pass through the middle; the purple ones (cosine path) curve gently.

**Read it.** Reflow turns a model that needed many steps into one that is usable in a *single* step - the basis of the fast "distilled" flow models. The honest trade-off: pairs come from the model's own samples, so any error in the first flow is baked in.

## Step 9 - Conditioning and classifier-free guidance

To generate a *particular* moon we feed a class label $y$ into the network. **Classifier-free guidance** (CFG) trains one network for both cases by randomly replacing $y$ with a "no label" token (15 % of the time), then at sampling time *extrapolates* from the unconditional toward the conditional velocity:

$$\tilde v = v_\theta(x,t,\varnothing) + w\,\big(v_\theta(x,t,y)-v_\theta(x,t,\varnothing)\big).$$

$w=0$ ignores the label, $w=1$ is plain conditional sampling, $w>1$ pushes harder toward the class. To make guidance matter we use **noisier moons** (noise 0.3) whose classes overlap, and measure **class accuracy** (15-nearest-neighbour vote against labelled data) and **diversity** (the horizontal spread of one class's samples, relative to the real data's).

**Read it.** $w=0$ draws from the *unconditional* mixture (orange and blue mixed, accuracy at chance); $w=1$ respects the label but the overlap still costs accuracy; larger $w$ separates the classes (accuracy up) while each class **shrinks toward its core** - the fidelity-vs-diversity dial of guidance, visible in 2-D.

## Step 10 - Where this shows up (this repo, and driving)

**Discrete flow matching for text (this repo, `variant: flow_matching`).** Text tokens cannot be nudged by a velocity, so the same *recipe* is applied to a **masking path**: at time $t$ each token is still masked with probability `mask_prob(t) = 1 - t`; the model predicts the clean token, and the loss is weighted by `1 / mask_prob(t)` (the "rate"). The code below is read straight from the repo files (not re-implemented):

| | continuous (this notebook) | discrete text (`models_flow_matching`) |
|---|---|---|
| state | position $x\in\mathbb R^2$ | token sequence (some masked) |
| path | $x_t=(1-t)x_0+t\,x_1$ | mask each token w.p. $1-t$ |
| network predicts | velocity $x_1-x_0$ | the clean token |
| sampling | ODE steps | unmask tokens step by step |

**Driving and robotics.** Flow matching is also how many action heads produce *continuous trajectories*: the Alpamayo-R1 paper (`assets/lifting_papers/alpamayo_fig1.png` in `Halos-lift`) states that *"an action-expert decoder using flow matching converts the discrete trajectory tokens into continuous, kinematically feasible waypoints conditioned on the reasoning output"* - the same velocity regression as above, with the waypoints as $x_1$ and the scene context as the condition.

## Takeaways

1. **Generation = transport.** A network outputs a velocity; sampling integrates an ODE from noise to data.
2. **Training is plain regression**: pick a path (straight), compute its constant velocity $x_1-x_0$, minimise a squared error (Eq. 9). No simulation, no noise schedule to tune. The optimum is the *average* pair-velocity - verified against a closed form in Step 4.
3. **Straight *targets* do not make a straight *flow*.** In this toy the straight-path flow was bent (and the cosine path was not worse at few steps) - Step 7. **Reflow** is what straightens it and enables one-step sampling (Step 8). Diffusion is the same machinery with another path.
4. **CFG** works unchanged: one network, label dropout, extrapolate at sampling time.
5. The same recipe carries over to **discrete tokens** (masking path - this repo) and to **action trajectories**.

### Exercises
- Replace the two moons by a 3-spiral set; how many Euler steps are needed now?
- Make the source a *different* distribution (e.g. a ring) instead of Gaussian noise: nothing in the loss requires $x_0$ to be Gaussian (domain transfer, Liu et al.).
- Try `p_uncond=0` in Step 9 and see why guidance needs the "no label" token.
- Add a third path, e.g. $x_t=(1-t^2)x_0+t^2x_1$, and compare its straightness.

### Honest limits
2-D toy data, small MLPs and single seeds: the *trends* (reflow straightening, guidance trading diversity for accuracy, step-count/quality curves) are measured above, as is one that went *against* the usual slogan (Step 7), but the absolute numbers will move with seeds and sizes. Large-scale claims are the papers', not tested here.

# End-to-end 4-bit training, reproduced in emulation - runs on a MacBook
### NVFP4 forward, MXFP8 backward, 4-over-6 scaling, stochastic rounding, RHT + S2F6 - every number format built from scratch and tested

**Source.** Thinking Machines Lab, *End-to-End 4-Bit Training* (blog post, https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/end-to-end-4-bit-training/). The post trains a large MoE model with a native 4-bit forward pass on NVIDIA GB300 racks. Definitions the post defers to other papers are taken from those papers, whose text I read directly:
- NVIDIA, *Pretraining LLMs with NVFP4*, [arXiv 2509.25149](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25149) - the format, two-level scaling, 2D weight scaling, RHT, stochastic rounding.
- Cook et al., *Four Over Six*, [arXiv 2512.02010](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.02010) - adaptive block scaling (4/6).

**What this notebook is, and is not.** A MacBook has no FP4 tensor cores. Everything here is **emulation**: values are rounded to the exact grid of each format and then multiplied in float32. That reproduces the *numerics* (error, bias, training behaviour) but gives **no speed-up**. The speed-ups, the kernels and the NVLink communication in the post need Blackwell hardware; the companion notebook `02_nvfp4_native_on_blackwell_cuda.ipynb` covers the native path.

| step | question | how it is checked |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | what are NVFP4 and MXFP8, and what do they cost in bits? | arithmetic + figure |
| 2 | how does two-level block scaling work? | hand-worked example reproduced exactly |
| 3 | what is 4/6 and does it lower the error? | the paper's Table 2 + measured error |
| 4 | why stochastic rounding? | the post's worked example + measured bias |
| 5 | what is the transpose problem, and the post's fix? | measured on a weight matrix |
| 6 | the recipe as one layer (forward NVFP4, backward MXFP8) | gradient error and unbiasedness |
| 7 | does a small transformer train with it? | 5 configurations, 2 seeds |
| 8 | RHT + S2F6 for communication | measured error |
| 9 | does it all run on this Mac (cpu and Apple GPU)? | parity tests + the repo's unit tests |

Claims from the post that this notebook **cannot** test are listed in Step 10.

## Step 1 - The formats and what they cost

A **block-scaled** format stores small elements plus one shared scale per block of values. Cost per value = element bits + scale bits / block size.

| format | elements | block | scale | bits / value |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **NVFP4** | FP4 (E2M1) | 16 | E4M3 per block, **+ one FP32 per tensor** | 4 + 8/16 = **4.5** |
| MXFP4 | FP4 (E2M1) | 32 | E8M0 (power of two) | 4 + 8/32 = 4.25 |
| **MXFP8** | FP8 (E4M3) | 32 | E8M0 | 8 + 8/32 = **8.25** |
| BF16 | - | - | - | 16 |

The FP32 outer scale is one number per tensor (negligible). The post's table lists NVFP4 at 4.5, MXFP8 at 8.25 and BF16 at 16 bits and says NVFP4 uses 3.6 fewer bits than BF16.

**The coarse top end matters.** Between 4 and 6 the only FP4 values are 4 and 6, so a scaled value near 5 can be off by up to 1 (i.e. 20 %). The Four-Over-Six paper identifies exactly these near-maximal values as the main source of NVFP4 error (Step 3).

## Step 2 - Two-level scaling, by hand

Per the NVIDIA paper (Appendix B), for a tensor `x`:

1. **Tensor scale** (FP32): ` = amax(x) / (6  448)` - 6 is the largest FP4 value, 448 the largest E4M3 value.
2. **Block scale** (E4M3, one per 16 values): ` = e4m3( amax_block / (  6) )`.
3. **Elements**: `q = fp4( x / (  ) )`, and the stored tensor decodes as `x  q    `.

The block maximum is mapped to 6 (the FP4 maximum). Because  is rounded to E4M3 (3 mantissa bits), the maximum lands *near* 6, not exactly on it. Reproducing it with the kit for one block:

## Step 3 - Four Over Six (4/6): pick the better of two scales per block

Standard NVFP4 always maps the block maximum to **6**. 4/6 also tries mapping it to **4**. With a maximum of 4 the grid's useful top step is `3  4` (75 % of the maximum) instead of `4  6` (66.7 %), which represents near-maximal values better - at the cost of the range above 4. Per block, **quantize twice and keep the version with the lower mean squared error** (the paper tested max-error, MAE and MSE; MSE won). The paper's hand-worked example (Table 2, block size 4, outer scale 1):

The two curves cross: neither scale is better everywhere, which is why the choice is made **per block from the measured error**, not globally (the paper also shows scaling *every* block to 4 is worse than standard NVFP4).

Now the measurement that matters: relative quantization error (error energy / signal energy) on three kinds of data, for the formats in play. INT4 with a float scale per 16 values is included as a reference.

Read the table honestly: 4/6 gives a consistent reduction ( 10-15 % in error energy here), not an order of magnitude; NVFP4 is clearly better than MXFP4; and 4-bit stays about **10 less accurate than MXFP8** whatever you do - which is why the post keeps the backward pass in MXFP8.

## Step 4 - Stochastic rounding (SR)

Round-to-nearest (RTN) is deterministic: a value that always falls the same side of a midpoint is always rounded the same way - a **bias**. **SR** rounds up with probability proportional to how close the value is to the upper neighbour, so the *expected* result equals the input. The post's own example: a value `5.4` in a block with scale 9 becomes `0.6  0.5` in the forward pass, i.e. `4.5`. In the backward pass it is re-quantized in a block with scale 2: `4.5 / 2 = 2.25`, which SR sends to 2 (probability 0.75) or 3 (probability 0.25).

Two properties follow, and both appear in the sources: SR is **needed for gradients** (small consistent values must survive), but it **hurts the forward pass** because it adds noise on top of the rounding error (NVIDIA: "applying stochastic rounding to the forward pass tensors is detrimental"). So the recipe uses RTN (with 4/6) forward and SR backward.

## Step 5 - The transpose problem

A linear layer `y = x  W` has two backward products:
`dx = g  W` (contracts over the *output* dimension N) and `dW = g  x` (contracts over the *token* dimension T).
Block scaling must group values **along the axis being contracted**, so the forward pass blocks `W` along K, while the backward pass needs `W` blocked along N. Different blocks  different scales  **different quantized values** for the same weight.

**Option A - NVIDIA's 2D scaling:** one scale per **16  16 tile**, so the quantized weight is *identical* whichever axis you block along. The post tried this ("NVIDIA's suggestion") and found it "too coarse, hurting CE beyond what we were willing to accept". Here is the coarseness, measured:

**Option B - the post's fix:** keep fine 116 blocks and do not require the forward and backward weights to be *bitwise* equal. Require them to be **equal in expectation**:

> `E[ w_q ] = E[ (w_q)_q ]` - dequantize the forward tensor, transpose it in high precision, re-quantize with **stochastic rounding** (blocks now along N).

SR is unbiased, so averaged over many draws the re-quantized weight returns exactly the forward weight. Check this, and compare with deterministic (RTN) re-quantization:

The post re-quantizes to **MXFP8** (not NVFP4): one SR draw is already  10 closer to the forward weight than an NVFP4 re-quantization, and averaging removes the rest.

## Step 6 - The recipe as one layer

Putting the pieces together for one linear layer (`T` tokens, `K` inputs, `N` outputs). Green = NVFP4, orange = MXFP8, grey = high precision (the forward quantized tensors are saved and later de-quantized in high precision):

`fp4kit.fp4_linear` implements exactly this. Three checks on one layer with realistic outlier channels in the activations:

1. the forward output stays close to the fp32 output;
2. a **single** backward pass has noise (SR), but the **average over many passes converges** to the gradient of the *quantized* forward (`gw_q`, `gx_q`) - i.e. the backward is unbiased given the forward;
3. deterministic (RTN) re-quantization instead of SR leaves a **bias that does not average out**.

Read the plot carefully: per pass, RTN is actually *closer* (MXFP8-RTN 0.037 vs MXFP8-SR 0.052 here) - but its error is a fixed bias, while SR's error is noise that averages away (0.0033 after 256 passes, 10 below the RTN floor). NVFP4-SR departs slightly from the 1/n line at large n; a plausible cause is saturation when the E4M3-rounded block scale pushes a block maximum above 6 (not investigated here).

This is the quantitative reason for the post's design: the backward needs **many small, unbiased** contributions; MXFP8 gives much less noise per pass than NVFP4 for the same unbiasedness, so the post keeps the backward in MXFP8 and uses NVFP4 only where RTN is acceptable (forward).

## Step 7 - Does a small transformer train with it?

A 2-block causal transformer (d = 128, 4 heads, MLP 256, 0.4 M parameters) on text from a **random order-2 Markov chain** over 24 symbols. The data has a known **entropy floor** (the best possible loss), so "how close to the floor" is measurable. Only the four linear layers per block are quantized (`qkv`, attention output, both MLP layers) - the post also quantizes only the MoE expert linears; embeddings, norms, attention and the output head stay in fp32. Optimizer: AdamW on fp32 master weights. Fresh data every step (no memorization), 2 seeds.

**How to read this.** The measured gaps are the result - the table above prints them with the seed spread. At this scale (0.4 M parameters, 800 steps) differences between the quantized configurations can be comparable to the seed-to-seed spread, so this experiment supports statements of the form *"the recipe trains and tracks fp32 closely here"*; it **cannot** reproduce the post's reported  2 % compute-efficiency cost at 10-10 scale, and it does not decide whether 4/6 or the MXFP8 backward is worth it at scale (the numerical evidence for those is Steps 3 and 6).

## Step 8 - Quantized communication: RHT + S2F6

In expert parallelism every token's expert output must cross NVLink ("combine"). The post wants fewer than 16 bits per value but found plain MXFP8 hurt quality "because of poor outlier handling". Its fix: a **32-dim random Hadamard transform (RHT)**, then **S2F6** (an 8-bit signed fixed-point format: 2 integer bits, 6 fractional bits, range 128/64  127/64) with one scale per 32 values  `8 + 8/32 = 8.25` bits/value instead of 16.

An RHT is an orthogonal rotation: it preserves total energy, is exactly invertible, and spreads one large value across all 32 slots, so no single value dominates the block scale. (The post gives no scale rule for S2F6; the kit chooses the smallest power of two that fits the block maximum.)

The measurement agrees with the post's story *as an error measurement*: with the same 8.25 bits, S2F6 beats MXFP8 on ordinary data, loses its advantage when outliers set the block scale, and the RHT restores it. Whether this leaves "no measurable accuracy degradation during training" (the post's claim) is a large-model training result that is not tested here.

## Step 9 - Does it run on this Mac?

Three levels of evidence: (1) the same quantizers give **identical results on the CPU and on the Apple GPU (mps)**; (2) the repo's unit tests for `fp4kit` pass; (3) a short training run works on the Apple GPU.

## Step 10 - What this notebook does not show

| claim in the post | status here |
|---|---|
|  8 % wall-clock speed-up in pretraining,  20 % in RL, vs full MXFP8 | **not testable**: needs FP4 tensor cores (Blackwell). Emulation is *slower* than fp32 |
|  2 % compute-efficiency cost of the recipe | **not testable**: a large-scale pretraining measurement; Step 7 is a 0.4 M-parameter toy |
| NVFP4 at 15 PFLOPS vs MXFP8 5 PFLOPS vs BF16 2.5 PFLOPS (GB300) | hardware spec quoted by the post; native timing is in the Blackwell notebook |
| fused MoE kernels (BBBK), epilogue fusion, PTX `cvt` instructions | not reproducible without the hardware; the *numerics* of S2F6/RHT are tested in Step 8 |
| quantized dispatch/combine across NVLink | needs multi-GPU; Step 8 tests only the quantization error |
| last  1/6 of layers kept in MXFP8 | the kit supports `fwd="mxfp8"` per layer; not ablated here (2 blocks only) |
| MoE-specific design (per-expert outer scales) | a dense transformer is used; per-expert scales equal per-weight-matrix scales here |

Known simplifications in the kit (each marked ASSUMPTION in `fp4kit.py`): MXFP8 uses E4M3 elements everywhere; E8M0 scales are rounded up; the S2F6 scale rule is mine; no tensor-parallel or fused-kernel behaviour is modelled.

## Takeaways

1. **NVFP4 = FP4 elements + E4M3 scale per 16 + an FP32 scale per tensor = 4.5 bits/value**; MXFP8 costs 8.25. Verified arithmetic and decoders.
2. **4/6 helps a little, everywhere**: choosing between "max  6" and "max  4" per block lowered error on all three data sets (Step 3), and the paper's Table 2 is reproduced exactly.
3. **Backward needs unbiasedness, not low error**: SR averages to the right gradient, RTN re-quantization leaves a floor (Steps 4-6). MXFP8-SR is ~3 less noisy per pass than NVFP4-SR, which is why the post keeps the backward in MXFP8.
4. **The transpose problem is real** (> 30 % of weights quantize differently along the other axis), 16  16 tiles fix it but at higher error and with outlier collapse, and "equal in expectation via SR re-quantization" is the post's way out.
5. **RHT + S2F6** is a measurably better 8.25-bit format than MXFP8 for outlier-prone data.
6. **It all runs on a Mac** - identical numerics on cpu and mps - but only as emulation: no speed-up.

### Exercises
- Add a third 4/6 candidate (map the max to 3 or 5) and test whether Table 2's rule still wins; the paper says other scales offer only subsets of what 4 and 6 give.
- Quantize *only* the second block with MXFP8 forward (`fwd="mxfp8"`) and see whether the gap to fp32 shrinks (the post keeps the last  1/6 of layers in MXFP8).
- Replace the Markov data by your own tokens and a deeper model; check which configuration degrades first.
- Combine 4/6 with SR (the kit forbids it); what would "the better of two SR draws" even mean?

### Honest limits
Emulation only; toy data and a 0.4 M-parameter model with 2 seeds; the post was read through text extraction (its numbers are quoted, not re-measured), and the 4/6 and NVFP4 definitions come from the papers' text. Speed, kernels and communication are untested.

# Native NVFP4 and MXFP8 on NVIDIA Blackwell (CUDA) - the hardware half of end-to-end 4-bit training
### Pack the operands, swizzle the scales, call the block-scaled tensor-core GEMMs, and check them against the emulated reference

Companion to `01_end_to_end_4bit_training_on_macbook.ipynb`, which builds the numerics of the *End-to-End 4-Bit Training* recipe ([Thinking Machines Lab](https://thinkingmachines.ai/blog/end-to-end-4-bit-training/)) in emulation. This notebook runs the same recipe's matrix multiplies on **real FP4 / FP8 tensor cores**.

> **Status of this notebook - read first.**
> It was written and executed on a MacBook with **no NVIDIA GPU**. Every cell that needs a Blackwell GPU printed `SKIP` there; **those cells have never run on Blackwell hardware**. The CPU cells (packing, scale layout, containers, API plumbing) did run and pass. The GPU cells are built so that a mistake cannot pass silently: each one compares the native result to the emulated reference and reports `PASS` or `FAIL` with the measured difference, and the final cell lists what ran. **If you run it on a Blackwell GPU, the `FAIL`/`PASS` lines are the real test of this notebook.**

**Hardware that has the FP4 tensor cores:** data-centre Blackwell (B200, GB200, GB300 - compute capability 10.x) and consumer / workstation Blackwell (RTX 50-series, RTX PRO Blackwell - compute capability 12.x). **Software:** a PyTorch build with CUDA 12.8 or newer.

| step | where it runs | what it establishes |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | anywhere | what hardware/software you have, and whether the GPU cells will run |
| 1 | anywhere | PyTorch's FP4 / FP8 dtypes exist |
| 2 | anywhere (CPU) | FP4 packing (two values per byte) and the scale layout, checked against PyTorch's own reference helpers |
| 3 | anywhere (CPU) | the operand containers carry exactly the emulated tensors, at 4.5 / 8.25 bits per value |
| 4 | anywhere | the `scaled_mm` call signature is accepted by PyTorch (a weak, shape-only check); the whole GPU code path run on the CPU with a stand-in kernel |
| 5 | **Blackwell** | native NVFP4 and MXFP8 GEMM == emulated GEMM |
| 6 | **Blackwell** | the whole recipe layer (NVFP4 forward, MXFP8 backward) native == emulated |
| 7 | **Blackwell** | throughput: bf16 vs MXFP8 vs NVFP4 |
| 8 | **Blackwell** + libraries | the vendor implementations (Transformer Engine, torchao), if installed |
| 9 | **Blackwell** | a small network trained with native GEMMs vs the emulation |

## Step 1 - PyTorch's low-precision dtypes

Block-scaled GEMMs take **packed FP4** (two E2M1 values per byte), **FP8 E4M3** elements, and scale tensors in **E4M3** (NVFP4) or **E8M0** (MXFP8). PyTorch exposes them as `torch.float4_e2m1fn_x2`, `torch.float8_e4m3fn` and `torch.float8_e8m0fnu`.

## Step 2 - Packing FP4 and swizzling the scales

Two layout rules decide whether a native GEMM returns the right numbers. Both are easy to get wrong, so we check ours against **PyTorch's own reference helpers** (`torch/testing/_internal/common_quantized.py`; we read the functions from that file's source rather than importing it, because importing it pulls in a test-only dependency).

**Rule 1 - packing.** An FP4 value is a 4-bit code: 1 sign bit + a 3-bit index into `{0, .5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6}`. Two codes share a byte: **the even element in the low nibble, the odd element in the high nibble**.

**Rule 2 - scale layout.** The scale matrix (`rows  K/block`) is not passed row-major. cuBLAS wants it in *128  4 tiles stored as 32  16* ("swizzled", `SWIZZLE_32_4_4`). Element `(r, c)` of a tile lands at flat offset `(r mod 32)16 + (r div 32)4 + c`.

## Step 3 - Operand containers: what the GEMM actually receives

`nvfp4_parts(x)` turns a 2-D tensor into the exact things an NVFP4 GEMM consumes: **packed FP4 bytes, E4M3 block scales, an FP32 outer scale**. `mxfp8_parts(x)` does the same for MXFP8 (E4M3 elements, E8M0 scales). Two properties to establish on the CPU, because they hold regardless of the GPU:

1. **Nothing is lost**: de-quantizing the parts reproduces the emulated tensor from notebook 01 exactly.
2. **The cost is real**: counting the bytes of the actual tensors gives 4.5 and 8.25 bits per value, as in the post's table.

## Step 4 - The GEMM call, and what can be checked without a GPU

The native matmul is one PyTorch call (`kit._scaled_mm`, shown below). `scaled_mm` takes the operands, the scale tensors, a **recipe** per operand (`BlockWise1x16` for NVFP4, `BlockWise1x32` for MXFP8) and the swizzle enum; the second operand is passed transposed (`b.t()`), i.e. column-major. The FP32 outer scales are applied to the output afterwards - exact, because they factor out of the sum.

### Step 4b - Run the GPU code path on the CPU with a stand-in kernel

The weakness of the meta check is that no numbers flow. So here the *same* `scaled_mm` code path - hardware dtypes, swizzled scales, outer scales, transposed second operand, the backward-pass re-quantization - is run with `_scaled_mm` replaced by `cpu_standin_scaled_mm`, which decodes exactly those packed bytes and swizzled scales and multiplies in float32. This verifies **our** formatting and bookkeeping end to end (and catches e.g. a swapped nibble order, shown below); it cannot verify cuBLAS itself.

## Step 5 - Native GEMM versus emulated GEMM *(Blackwell only)*

Same quantized operands, two ways to multiply them: dequantize and use a float32 matmul (the emulation), or hand the packed operands to the tensor cores. They should agree up to the **bf16 rounding of the GEMM output** (relative error  210) and accumulation order. A wrong nibble order or a wrong scale layout gives an error of order 1 - so a tolerance of 2 % cleanly separates "correct" from "wrong".

If the NVFP4 check fails, the diagnostic cell right after it tries the two most likely layout mistakes (swapped nibbles, un-swizzled scales) and reports which one matches.

## Step 6 - The whole recipe layer, native versus emulated *(Blackwell only)*

`fp4_linear_packed(x, w, cfg, backend)` is the recipe from the post on packed operands: **forward** NVFP4 GEMM (4/6 on activations with per-token outer scales, per-weight-matrix scale, RTN); **backward** two MXFP8 GEMMs (`dx = gw_q`, `dW = gx_q`) after de-quantizing the saved forward tensors, transposing, and re-quantizing with stochastic rounding.

Run with `backend="emulated"` it reproduces `fp4_linear` exactly (a CPU unit test verifies this). Run with `backend="scaled_mm"` the three matmuls use the tensor cores. We seed the random generator identically so both backends draw **the same stochastic-rounding numbers**; then forward output, `dx` and `dW` must agree up to bf16 output rounding.

## Step 7 - Throughput *(Blackwell only)*

Only the GEMM is timed (operands are quantized beforehand): bf16 `x @ w` versus the two block-scaled GEMMs. The post quotes, for GB300, peak dense throughput of **2.5 PFLOPS (BF16), 5 (MXFP8) and 15 (NVFP4)**, i.e. peak ratios of 1 : 2 : 6. Measured ratios will be lower (memory-bound shapes, launch overhead) and **will differ on consumer cards**; what you see is whatever your GPU does - nothing in this notebook asserts a speed-up.

## Step 8 - Vendor implementations *(Blackwell + the library installed)*

You rarely hand-roll this for production. Two maintained implementations, shown here **as a smoke test** (each is guarded: skipped if the package is not installed). Names and arguments are from the libraries' documentation; this notebook was not able to run them.

- **NVIDIA Transformer Engine** - `NVFP4BlockScaling` recipe: 2D (1616) weight scaling, random Hadamard transform on the weight-gradient inputs and stochastic rounding of gradients, all on by default per its docs. Requires SM 10.0+.
- **torchao** - `NVFP4DynamicActivationNVFP4WeightConfig` (inference / PTQ; prototype API whose names have changed between versions).

## Step 9 - Train a small network with the native GEMMs *(Blackwell only)*

A two-layer MLP student learns a random teacher (regression). Three runs from the same initialization and data: **fp32**, **recipe, emulated**, **recipe, native `scaled_mm`**. The native run should track the emulated one; both should track fp32 within the 4-bit noise. Sizes are multiples of 128 so every GEMM dimension is safe for the tensor cores.

## What to do with the results

- **All `PASS` on a Blackwell GPU:** the packed operands, the swizzled scales, the `scaled_mm` calls and the recipe layer are right; Step 7's table is your hardware's real FP4 / FP8 / bf16 throughput.
- **A `FAIL` in Step 5:** the printed relative difference tells you the kind of error.  1 means a layout problem (run the diagnostic cell: swapped nibbles / un-swizzled scales); a few percent means a precision/accumulation difference worth looking at. Exceptions are printed in full (typically unsupported dimension, dtype or architecture for that `scaled_mm` path).
- **Consumer GPUs (compute capability 12.x):** the FP4 tensor-core instructions exist, but which `scaled_mm` paths PyTorch exposes there depends on the version; the guarded cells report what works.

## What this notebook does not cover

The post's other hardware-level parts - the fused MoE kernels, PTX `cvt` conversions for S2F6/RHT in the GEMM epilogue, and quantized dispatch/combine over NVLink - need custom kernels and multiple GPUs. Their *numerics* (S2F6, RHT, stochastic rounding, 4/6) are tested in notebook 01. Also not covered: the speed-up and compute-efficiency figures the post reports for its large MoE run ( 8 % pretraining,  20 % RL wall-clock;  2 % compute-efficiency cost); a microbenchmark of one GEMM says little about an end-to-end training run.

### Honest limits
Never executed on Blackwell by the author of this notebook (Mac only); API names for `scaled_mm`, Transformer Engine and torchao are taken from PyTorch's installed source and the libraries' documentation; the meta-device check is shape-only; swizzle and packing were verified against PyTorch's reference helpers on the CPU.

# ResNet from zero: the identity shortcut, the residual, and how backprop flows 
### *Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition* (He, Zhang, Ren, Sun - 2015), explained for a complete beginner

**The one-sentence idea:** instead of asking a stack of layers to learn the *whole* answer `H(x)`, we give them a shortcut that carries `x` straight through, and ask them to learn only the **difference** `F(x) = H(x) - x`. The output is `x + F(x)`.

That tiny `+ x` is the reason we can train networks with 50, 152, even 1000+ layers. This notebook shows *why*, one small step at a time. Every claim is checked by code - look for the `PASS` lines.

| step | question | what you will see |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why not just stack more layers? | the paper's "degradation" plot |
| 2 | Why is learning the *identity* hard for normal layers? | a measured experiment |
| 3 | What is a residual block? | the paper's diagram + 2 diagrams from the web, then 3 numbers traced by hand |
| 4 | How is the block written in code? | a real conv block, shapes, and a proof it starts as identity |
| 5 | How does the network "learn the residual"? | a 1-D toy: watch `F(x)` converge to the missing piece |
| 6 | How does backprop work through `x + F(x)`? | a worked example with real numbers and a graph |
| 7 | Why does the gradient survive 50 layers? | measured gradient size per layer |
| 8 | Does it really train deeper nets? | plain vs residual at depth 2 ... 40 |
| 9 | What if shapes change? | the projection shortcut (1x1 conv) |
| 10 | The real ResNet-18 / ResNet-50 | paper architecture figures + we build both and count parameters |
| 11 | Three ways to think about *why* it works | gradient highway, ensemble of paths, smooth loss surface |
| 12 | Cheat sheet, misconceptions, further reading | |

*Prerequisites:* you know a neural network is layers of "multiply by weights, add bias, apply ReLU", and that training = gradient descent using gradients from backpropagation. If "gradient" is fuzzy, step 6 rebuilds it from scratch. CPU only, ~1 minute to run.

*Figure credits:* figures marked **(paper)** are from the original papers (He et al. 2015; Veit et al. 2016; Li et al. 2018), reproduced here for teaching. The two web diagrams are from Wikimedia Commons (`ResBlock-stream.svg`, CC0) and *Dive into Deep Learning* (d2l.ai, Zhang, Lipton, Li, Smola; CC BY-SA 4.0).

## Step 1 - The puzzle: deeper networks got *worse*

Common sense says: more layers = more capacity = better. In 2014-15 people stacked layers (VGG had 16-19) and it worked. But when the ResNet authors went further, something odd happened.

They trained a **20-layer** and a **56-layer** plain network (just conv layers stacked, no shortcuts) on CIFAR-10:

**(paper) Figure 1 - training error (left) and test error (right)**

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_fig1_degradation.png" width="620"/>

Look at the **left** plot, *training* error: the 56-layer net (red) is **worse than the 20-layer net (yellow) even on the data it is training on.**

This matters for a beginner:

* It is **not overfitting.** Overfitting means *low* training error but *high* test error. Here training error itself is higher.
* A 56-layer net could, in theory, copy the 20-layer net and make its extra 36 layers do *nothing* (pass the input through unchanged) - so it should be **at least as good**. It isn't. The optimizer (gradient descent) simply **fails to find** that solution.

The authors called this the **degradation problem**. The rest of this notebook is about the fix.

## Step 2 - Why is "do nothing" hard for a normal layer?

"Do nothing" = the **identity mapping**: output equals input, `H(x) = x`.

For a normal layer `y = ReLU(W x + b)`, doing nothing means gradient descent must land on **exactly `W = I` (the identity matrix) and `b = 0`** - out of millions of possible weight settings. And with 10 or 20 layers in a row, *every one* of them must land there simultaneously.

Compare with a **residual** layer `y = x + F(x)`. Doing nothing means `F(x) = 0`, i.e. **all weights of `F` equal 0**. Zero is the easiest value there is - it is where weight decay pulls, and we can simply *start* there.

Let us measure it. **Task: make a stack of 10 blocks reproduce its input** (`target = input`). The best possible answer is known to exist (identity), so any failure is purely an optimisation failure.

* *plain* block: `x -> ReLU( W2 . ReLU(W1 x) )`
* *residual* block: `x -> x + W2 . ReLU(W1 x)`  (same number of weights - only the `x +` differs)
* *residual, zero-init*: same, but `W2` starts at 0, so the network is *exactly* the identity at step 0.

**Reading the plot:** the plain stack starts far from "do nothing" and has to *discover* it; the residual stack starts closer and gets there much faster; the zero-initialised one is already perfect at step 0. That is the **identity shortcut at work**: doing nothing is the *default*, and learning is only needed for whatever *extra* the task requires.

## Step 3 - The residual block

Let `x` be the input to a small group of layers, and `H(x)` the mapping we would like them to produce.

| | plain network | residual network |
|---|---|---|
| layers must learn | `H(x)` (everything) | `F(x) = H(x) - x` (just the *difference*) |
| output | `F(x)` | `F(x) + x` |

Rearranging `F(x) = H(x) - x` gives the famous formula **`output = x + F(x)`**. The `x` travels along a **skip connection** (also called *shortcut* or *identity connection*); `F(x)` is the **residual branch** - "residual" just means *what is left over* after you take `x` away.

**(paper) Figure 2 - "a building block"** &nbsp;&nbsp;|&nbsp;&nbsp; **(web, CC0) the same idea, redrawn**

<table><tr>
<td><img src="resnet_assets/paper_fig2_residual_block.png" width="330"/></td>
<td><img src="resnet_assets/web_resblock_stream.png" width="200"/></td>
</tr></table>

How to read them: the input `x` enters at the bottom. It splits in two. One copy goes **through the weight layers** (`F(x)`); the other copy **skips over them** untouched (the "identity"). They meet at the ****, which simply *adds* them element by element.

**Intuition - editing vs. writing.** A plain layer is like writing a whole essay from a blank page every time. A residual block is like being handed the previous draft (`x`) and being asked only to *mark corrections* (`F(x)`). If the draft is already fine, the correct edit is "no change" = 0, which is trivial.

Here is the whole thing with three numbers, so nothing is hidden:

## Step 4 - The block as real code (the CNN version from the paper)

In an image network, `F(x)` is two 3x3 convolutions with BatchNorm and ReLU. The diagram below (from the *Dive into Deep Learning* book, CC BY-SA 4.0) shows every box. **Left:** the normal block. **Right:** the version with a **1x1 conv on the shortcut**, used when the shape changes (we cover that in Step 9).

<img src="resnet_assets/web_d2l_resnet_block.png" width="560"/>

Reading the left diagram bottom-to-top: `x` -> Conv3x3 -> BatchNorm -> ReLU -> Conv3x3 -> BatchNorm -> **(+ x)** -> ReLU. Everything inside the dashed box is `F(x)`; the arrow that bypasses the dashed box is the identity.

Now the code. Read `forward` - it is five lines.

## Step 5 - How does the network "learn the residual"?

Nothing special happens in training - we do **not** tell the network what the residual is. We only write `out = x + F(x)` and train with the normal loss on `out`. Because the `x` is *already* in the output, the only way for the loss to drop is for `F` to supply **whatever `x` is missing**. So `F` automatically becomes `H(x) - x`.

Toy to see it: the true function is `H(x) = x + 0.3 sin(3x)`. The "big part" is `x` (the identity); the small missing piece is `0.3 sin(3x)`. We train `out = x + F(x)` where `F` is a tiny network that has never been told the answer.

**Takeaways**
1. **"Learning the residual" = learning the *correction*.** `F` converges to exactly `H(x) - x`.
2. If the best thing to do is nothing, `F` just shrinks to 0 (second check). A plain layer would have to *reconstruct* the identity.
3. The paper's hypothesis: for very deep nets, the ideal mapping is often *close* to the identity, so a small correction is far easier to find than the whole function from scratch.

## Step 6 - Backpropagation through `x + F(x)`

**Backprop in one paragraph.** To train, we need `dL/dw` (how the loss `L` changes when a weight `w` changes). Backprop gets it with the **chain rule**: go backwards through the network and *multiply* local derivatives. If `L` depends on `y`, and `y` depends on `x`, then

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`dL/dx = dL/dy * dy/dx`.

**The addition node is the key.** For a block `y = x + F(x)`:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`dy/dx = d(x)/dx + dF/dx = 1 + F'(x)`

so

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**`dL/dx = dL/dy * ( 1 + F'(x) )`**

An add operation just **copies the incoming gradient to both of its inputs**. One copy flows down the skip path *unchanged* (the `1`); the other goes through `F` (the `F'(x)` part). The gradient is therefore *never forced to pass through a long chain of weight matrices*.

Let us do a full worked example with real numbers, by hand **and** by PyTorch autograd, and compare:

* `x = 2`, branch `F(x) = w2 * ReLU(w1 * x)` with `w1 = 0.5`, `w2 = -0.3`
* output `y = x + F(x)`, loss `L = 0.5 * (y - t)^2` with target `t = 3`

Now the same example as a picture: **blue = forward values, red = gradients flowing backwards.** Watch the red number at the  get *copied* onto both outgoing branches.

### Stacking many blocks - the "gradient highway"

Write block `i` as `x_{i+1} = x_i + F_i(x_i)`. Unrolling `L` blocks gives

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`x_L = x_l + F_l(x_l) + F_{l+1}(x_{l+1}) + ... + F_{L-1}(x_{L-1})`  &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;(x_l plus a **sum** of corrections)

and so

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`dLoss/dx_l = dLoss/dx_L * ( 1 + d/dx_l [ F_l + ... + F_{L-1} ] )`

The `1` means a piece of the gradient reaches *any* earlier block **directly from the loss**, no matter how deep the net is. In a **plain** network the same gradient is a *product* of `L` Jacobians (one per layer):

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`dLoss/dx_l = dLoss/dx_L * J_{L-1} * J_{L-2} * ... * J_l`

and a product of many numbers that are mostly < 1 shrinks exponentially (**vanishing gradients**); if they are mostly > 1 it explodes. *Sum* (residual) vs. *product* (plain) - that is the whole difference. Next we measure it.

**What the plot says**

* **Plain, default init (red-orange):** the gradient shrinks so fast going backwards that after 50 blocks it has underflowed to **exactly 0** in float32 (the line drops to the floor of the plot). The earliest layers receive *no* learning signal. This is the textbook vanishing gradient.
* **Plain, He init (dashed):** careful initialisation (and, in real ResNets, **BatchNorm**) *fixes* the vanishing gradient **at initialisation** - the dashed line is flat, no collapse. This is an important honesty point: the ResNet paper says exactly this - for their plain nets *with BatchNorm* the gradients were healthy, **yet the degradation problem remained**. So the shortcut is not *only* about gradient size; it also changes **what is easy to represent** (identity = zero) and the **shape of the optimisation problem** (Step 11).
* **Residual (blue):** stays in a healthy band with *no* special initialisation - the `+1` provides a guaranteed path.

## Step 8 - Does it actually train deeper networks? (the degradation experiment, in miniature)

We reproduce the paper's Figure 1 / Figure 4 story on a toy: classify points on **3 intertwined spirals** with MLPs of increasing depth. Same width, same optimiser, same number of steps; the **only** difference is the `x +`.

**Reading it:** at depth 1-2 everything works. As depth grows, the plain nets' *training* accuracy **collapses** (to ~0.33, i.e. guessing among 3 classes) - the same *deeper-is-worse-on-training-data* pattern as the paper's Figure 1 - while the residual net fits perfectly at every depth.

The **orange dashed** line is the important one. It is a plain net with He initialisation, whose gradients at step 0 were perfectly healthy (Step 7). It trains fine up to 10 blocks, then **still fails at 20 and 40**. So "just fix the gradient size" does not remove degradation - exactly the paper's point. (Our toy has no BatchNorm; see the honest note below.)

Now compare the paper's real-data version of this experiment, on **ImageNet** (left: plain-18 vs plain-34; right: ResNet-18 vs ResNet-34). Thin lines = training error, bold = validation error:

**(paper) Figure 4**

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_fig4_imagenet_curves.png" width="760"/>

Left: the deeper plain net (34, red) has *higher* error than the shallower one (18, cyan) - degradation. Right: with shortcuts the order **flips** - ResNet-34 (red) beats ResNet-18 (cyan). Depth finally helps.

> **Honest note on the toy:** these are *ReLU-only, no-BatchNorm* MLPs trained with plain SGD, which are more fragile than the paper's plain CNNs, so our plain curves fail more dramatically (to chance) than the paper's (a few points of extra error). The qualitative lesson - residual nets keep training as depth grows, plain nets do not - is the same.

## Step 9 - What if the shapes don't match? (projection shortcut)

`x + F(x)` needs `x` and `F(x)` to have the **same shape**. Between stages, ResNet halves the spatial size (stride 2) and doubles the channels, so `F(x)` changes shape but a bare `x` does not. The paper's fix (Eq. 2, "option B"): apply a **1x1 convolution** to the shortcut so it matches. It is drawn as the right-hand block in the d2l diagram of Step 4. The identity shortcut is used everywhere else, because it adds **zero** parameters and zero compute.

## Step 10 - The full network: ResNet-18 ... ResNet-152

Stack the blocks in four **stages** (channels 64 -> 128 -> 256 -> 512), start with a 7x7 conv and max-pool, finish with global average pooling and one linear layer.

**(paper) Figure 3 - left: VGG-19; middle: a 34-layer *plain* net; right: the 34-layer *residual* net.** Solid curved arrows are identity shortcuts; **dotted** ones are the projection (1x1 conv) shortcuts where the shape changes.

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_fig3_architectures.png" width="330"/>

For networks of 50+ layers the paper switches to the cheaper **bottleneck** block: a 1x1 conv *squeezes* the channels, a 3x3 conv works on the small tensor, a 1x1 conv *expands* back. **(paper) Figure 5** - left: the basic block (ResNet-34), right: the bottleneck (ResNet-50/101/152):

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_fig5_bottleneck.png" width="420"/>

Now we build them ourselves. Sanity check: parameter counts must match the well-known figures for ResNet-18 (**11,689,512**) and ResNet-50 (**25,557,032**) with a 1000-class head.

## Step 11 - Three mental models for *why* it works

### (a) A gradient highway  *(Step 6-7)*
The `+1` gives every layer a direct line to the loss. Sum instead of product.

### (b) An ensemble of many shallow networks
Unroll three blocks: `x3 = (1+F1)(1+F2)(1+F3) x` (writing `F_i` as an operator). Multiply it out and you get **8 = 2^3 terms** - 8 different *paths* from input to output, each path either takes a block's `F` or skips it. Veit, Wilber & Belongie (2016) drew exactly this:

**(paper, Veit et al. 2016) Fig. 1 - left: how we usually draw a residual net; right: the same net "unraveled" into 2^n paths**

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_veit_unraveled.png" width="620"/>

Most of those paths are *short* (a binomial distribution of lengths), so a 110-layer ResNet behaves like a bag of mostly-shallow networks, which are easy to train. Let us verify the path-counting identity numerically, then do their "lesion" test: **delete** one block from a trained network.

**What we measured:** deleting a single block of the trained residual net leaves accuracy at 0.82-1.00 (mean 0.96), while the same surgery on the plain net drops it to about chance (mean 0.40). Veit et al. found the same on real ImageNet ResNets: removing one block barely changes accuracy, whereas deleting one layer from a plain VGG-style net destroys it. The blocks are *not* a fragile chain - they are partially independent contributors to a sum.

### (c) A smoother loss landscape
Li, Xu, Taylor, Studer & Goldstein (2018) *plotted* the loss surface of a ResNet-56 around its trained weights (loss on the vertical axis, two random directions in weight space on the horizontal axes; the blue spike at the bottom is the minimum):

**(paper, Li et al. 2018) Fig. 1**

<img src="resnet_assets/paper_losslandscape.png" width="640"/>

* **(a) without skip connections:** a chaotic, jagged surface full of cliffs and ridges - gradient descent gets stuck or thrown around. This is what "hard to optimise" looks like.
* **(b) with skip connections:** a wide, smooth bowl that funnels toward the minimum. This is why plain `SGD` just works on ResNets.

All three views are the *same fact* seen from different sides: the `+ x` makes the function easier to optimise.

## Step 12 - Cheat sheet, misconceptions, further reading

### One-page summary
| concept | meaning |
|---|---|
| **identity mapping** | `H(x) = x`; output equals input |
| **skip / shortcut / identity connection** | the wire that carries `x` around the weight layers |
| **residual `F(x)`** | `H(x) - x`; what the weight layers actually learn - the *correction* |
| **block output** | `x + F(x)` (original paper: `ReLU(x + F(x))`) |
| **forward** | add two tensors of the *same shape* |
| **backward** | `dL/dx = dL/dy * (1 + F'(x))`; add copies the gradient to both inputs |
| **plain net gradient** | *product* of Jacobians -> vanishes / explodes |
| **residual net gradient** | *sum* with a `+1` term -> always a direct path |
| **projection shortcut** | 1x1 conv on the skip path when shape changes (else free identity) |
| **bottleneck** | 1x1 reduce -> 3x3 -> 1x1 expand; cheaper deep blocks |
| **zero-init residual** | start the last BN's gamma at 0 so every block starts as identity |

### Common misconceptions
1. *"ResNet is just a fix for vanishing gradients."* Partly. The paper itself shows plain nets **with BatchNorm** have healthy gradients but still degrade. The shortcut also makes the identity easy to represent and the loss surface smoother (Step 11).
2. *"The skip connection adds parameters."* The identity shortcut adds **none**; only the occasional 1x1 projection does.
3. *"The network learns H(x) directly."* The *layers* learn `F(x)`; the *block* represents `H(x) = x + F(x)`.
4. *"Degradation = overfitting."* No - it is **worse training error** (Step 1).
5. *"Deeper ResNet is always better."* Gains saturate; the paper's 1202-layer CIFAR net did slightly *worse* than the 110-layer (overfitting on a small dataset).
6. *"The skip connection must go around exactly 2 layers."* No - 2-3 is typical, 1 layer gives little benefit, and Transformers use the same trick around attention and MLP sub-layers (`x + Attention(x)`, `x + MLP(x)`) - **ResNet's idea is inside every LLM**.

### Variants worth knowing
* **Pre-activation ResNet (ResNet-v2, He et al. 2016):** move BN/ReLU *before* the convs so the skip path is a *perfectly* clean identity - no ReLU after the add. Trains even 1001-layer nets.
* **ResNeXt, Wide ResNet, DenseNet (concatenate instead of add), Highway networks (learned gate on the shortcut).**

### Further reading
* He, Zhang, Ren, Sun. *Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition.* CVPR 2016 - arXiv:1512.03385
* He et al. *Identity Mappings in Deep Residual Networks.* ECCV 2016 - arXiv:1603.05027
* Veit, Wilber, Belongie. *Residual Networks Behave Like Ensembles of Relatively Shallow Networks.* NeurIPS 2016 - arXiv:1605.06431
* Li, Xu, Taylor, Studer, Goldstein. *Visualizing the Loss Landscape of Neural Nets.* NeurIPS 2018 - arXiv:1712.09913
* *Dive into Deep Learning*, chapter "Residual Networks (ResNet) and ResNeXt" - d2l.ai
* Wikipedia: "Residual neural network"

# VGGT from scratch: one transformer that turns photos into 3D 
### *VGGT: Visual Geometry Grounded Transformer* (Wang, Chen, Karaev, Vedaldi, Rupprecht, Novotny - Oxford VGG & Meta AI, CVPR 2025) - explained for a beginner, rebuilt in pure PyTorch

**What it does, in one sentence:** you give VGGT a handful of ordinary photos of a scene; in **one forward pass** (under a second) it hands back, for every photo, **where the camera was**, **how far away each pixel is**, **the 3D position of each pixel**, and **features for tracking points** between photos.

Before VGGT, getting that required a long classical pipeline (find matching points, solve geometry, run an optimiser called *bundle adjustment* for seconds to minutes). VGGT's bet: *"3D reconstruction is just another prediction problem - throw a big plain transformer and lots of 3D-labelled data at it."*

<img src="vggt_assets/paper_fig1_overview.png" width="760"/>

**(paper) Figure 1** - input photos on the left; VGGT's output on the right: a 3D point cloud of the scene, camera frustums (the little pyramids), depth maps and point tracks.

| step | question | what we do |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What is "3D reconstruction" and what were people doing before? | pinhole camera, epipolar geometry, the old pipeline |
| 2 | What exactly does VGGT output? | camera `g`, depth `D`, point map `P`, tracks `T` |
| 3 | What is the network? | the paper's architecture figure, token by token |
| 4 | A tiny 3D world to experiment in | we build a renderer with ground-truth cameras/depth, and verify the geometry |
| 5 | **Build it:** patchify images into tokens | pure PyTorch |
| 6 | **Build it:** camera + register tokens (and why frame 1 is special) | pure PyTorch |
| 7 | **Build it:** attention and the transformer block | pure PyTorch, checked against PyTorch's own |
| 8 | **Build it:** *Alternating Attention* - VGGT's key idea | pure PyTorch, with experiments |
| 9 | **Build it:** camera head and dense (depth/point/track) head | pure PyTorch |
| 10 | Put it together: `MiniVGGT` | parameter count by part |
| 11 | The loss: how it learns without being told the scale | uncertainty loss, normalisation |
| 12 | Train it on our toy world and measure | vs. naive baselines |
| 13 | Does Alternating Attention really beat plain global attention? | our mini ablation, next to the paper's |
| 14 | The real model + how to run it | size, training recipe, usage |
| 15 | Applications | what you can do with it |
| 16 | Cheat sheet and further reading | |

*Prerequisites:* know what a neural network and a transformer's "attention" roughly are (we re-explain attention from scratch in step 7). If residual connections are new to you, see the companion notebook `01_resnet_residual_learning.ipynb` - every transformer block below uses them. CPU only.

*Figure credits:* **(paper)** figures are from Wang et al. 2025 (arXiv:2503.11651). Web diagrams are from Wikimedia Commons: *Pinhole camera model* by O. Peters (CC BY-SA 4.0), *Epipolar geometry* by A. Nordmann (CC BY-SA 3.0), church photos by `.gs8` (CC BY-SA 3.0), *Vision Transformer* by D. V. Godoy (CC BY 4.0).

## Step 1 - What is 3D reconstruction, and why was it hard?

### A camera squashes 3D into 2D
A photo throws away one number per pixel: **how far away** the thing was. The standard mathematical model is the **pinhole camera**:

<table><tr><td><img src="vggt_assets/web_pinhole_camera.png" width="430"/></td><td>

*(web, CC BY-SA 4.0)* Light from a point in the world travels in a straight line through a single point - the **centre of projection** `O` - and lands on the **image plane**. Three things define the camera:

* **where it is and which way it faces** -> *extrinsics*: a rotation `R` and a translation `t` (6 numbers)
* **how zoomed-in it is** -> *intrinsics*: focal length / **field of view** (`principal distance c` in the picture)
* the **principal point** `H` - where the optical axis hits the image (VGGT assumes it is the image centre)

Every pixel therefore corresponds to a **ray** leaving the camera; the 3D point is *somewhere along that ray*. Depth tells you *where*.

</td></tr></table>

### Two photos pin the depth down
If the same physical point appears in two photos taken from different places, its two rays *cross* in 3D (**triangulation**). The geometric constraint relating the two views is **epipolar geometry**:

<table><tr><td><img src="vggt_assets/web_epipolar_geometry.png" width="380"/></td><td><img src="vggt_assets/web_epipolar_church_lines.png" width="460"/></td></tr></table>

*(web)* **Left:** a point `X` seen from two cameras `OL`, `OR`. A point in the left image can only appear on one **line** (the epipolar line) in the right image. **Right:** a real example - each coloured point on the left image has its match somewhere along the same-coloured line in the right image.

### The classical pipeline (Structure-from-Motion, SfM)
1. **Detect & match** keypoints between all pairs of photos.
2. **Estimate** the relative camera poses from the matches (epipolar geometry, RANSAC).
3. **Triangulate** 3D points.
4. **Bundle adjustment (BA):** an iterative optimiser that jointly nudges *all* cameras and *all* points to minimise reprojection error. Accurate, but slow (seconds-minutes), fragile when views barely overlap, and a pile of hand-built components (COLMAP is the classic tool).

VGGT asks: *can one network replace all four steps?*

## Step 2 - What VGGT predicts

The network is a function of `N` images `I_1 ... I_N` (each `3 x H x W`). For **every** image it returns four things:

| symbol | name | shape | meaning |
|---|---|---|---|
| `g_i` | **camera** | 9 numbers | quaternion `q` (4, the rotation) + translation `t` (3) + field of view (2). Principal point assumed at the image centre. |
| `D_i` | **depth map** | `H x W` | distance along the camera's forward axis for each pixel |
| `P_i` | **point map** | `3 x H x W` | the **(x, y, z) of each pixel's 3D point**, in the coordinates of **camera 1** |
| `T_i` | **track features** | `C x H x W` | per-pixel descriptors; a small tracking module uses them to follow a point across images |

Two ideas to hold on to:

* **Frame 1 is the reference.** Everything is expressed in camera 1's coordinate system, so camera 1's output is "do nothing": rotation = identity `q=[0,0,0,1]`, translation = `0`. (There has to be *some* reference - a photo alone does not say where the world's origin is.)
* **Outputs are redundant on purpose.** Depth + camera *determine* the point map (we verify this formula in code in Step 4). The authors still train the network to output **all of them**, because predicting extra related things made the *main* result better (paper ablation, Table 6; we repeat their numbers below).

**(paper) Figure 3 - VGGT vs. the previous method DUSt3R** (which only takes *two* photos at a time and needs a slow global optimisation afterwards): VGGT reads 1, 2 or 32 views in one pass; on 32 views DUSt3R needs > 200 s, VGGT < 0.6 s.

<img src="vggt_assets/paper_fig3_vs_dust3r.png" width="640"/>

## Step 3 - The network, in one picture

**(paper) Figure 2 - architecture overview**

<img src="vggt_assets/paper_fig2_architecture.png" width="820"/>

Read it left to right:

1. **Input images** (a few photos of one scene).
2. **DINO**: each image is cut into square **patches** and each patch becomes one **token** (a vector). Think of tokens as the "words" of an image. *(The real model starts from a pretrained DINO vision transformer; we use a plain patch embedding.)*
3. **Concat / add camera token:** next to each frame's patch tokens, the network adds one extra learnable **camera token** (orange) - a slot whose job is to *collect everything the model knows about that camera*. (Four "register" tokens are added as well - see Step 6.)
4. **Alternating attention x L:** the heart of the model. Layers alternate between **Frame attention** (yellow: tokens look only at tokens of *their own* image) and **Global attention** (blue: tokens look at tokens of *all* images). Repeated `L = 24` times in the real model.
5. **Heads:** the camera tokens go to the **camera head** -> camera poses. The patch tokens go to a **DPT head** -> depth maps, point maps and tracking features.

That is all. There are **no cross-attention layers and no hand-built geometry** (no epipolar constraints, no RANSAC, no bundle adjustment). The paper's phrase: *"minimal 3D inductive biases"* - the transformer is trusted to learn geometry from data.

The transformer family tree, if you know ViT: VGGT's tokens are exactly ViT tokens:

<img src="vggt_assets/web_vit.png" width="520"/>

*(web, CC BY 4.0)* A ViT cuts the image into patches, embeds each, adds positional encoding and runs a transformer. VGGT does this for **every** input image, then lets the images talk to each other through the global-attention layers.

## Step 4 - A tiny 3D world to build in

To build and *test* a VGGT we need training data with **ground-truth cameras and depth**. The real model uses ~17 datasets (CO3D, ScanNet, Kubric, ...). We make our own: a **checkered floor with three coloured spheres**, photographed by **3 cameras** at 32 x 32 pixels, with exact depth, exact camera poses, and exact 3D points (rendered with closed-form ray-sphere and ray-plane intersections - no libraries).

Three small geometry helpers first - read them, they are the "physics" under everything else:

* `look_at`: build a camera pose (`R`, `t`) from a position and a target.
* `quat_to_mat` / `mat_to_quat`: a rotation as 4 numbers (quaternion) <-> a 3x3 matrix. VGGT predicts quaternions.
* `unproject(depth, camera)`: **turn a depth map + a camera into 3D points.** For pixel `(u,v)` with depth `d`: the point in the camera's frame is `d * (x/z, y/z, 1)`, then `R^T (X_cam - t)` moves it into camera 1's frame.

Now the world generator `make_batch`. Pay attention to the last lines: it expresses **everything in camera 1's frame** and then **divides by the average distance of the 3D points to the origin** - the paper's *ground-truth coordinate normalisation* (Step 11 explains why).

**What you see:** three photos of the same scene from different angles. Their **point maps** (third column; xyz coloured as RGB) are all in camera 1's coordinates - so in the 3D plot the three clouds **overlap** on the same floor and spheres. *That* is the "viewpoint-invariant point map" idea: every pixel of every image lands in one shared 3D space.

## Step 5 - Build: turn images into tokens (patchify)

A transformer eats a **sequence of vectors**, not a grid of pixels. So we cut each image into non-overlapping `p x p` patches (here `4x4`; real VGGT: `14x14`) and map each patch (`3*p*p` numbers) to a `C`-dimensional vector with **one shared linear layer**. A convolution with `kernel = stride = p` does exactly that in one line.

A 32x32 image with `p=4` -> an 8x8 grid -> **64 tokens per image**. (Real VGGT: 518x518 with `p=14` -> 37x37 = **1369 tokens per image**.)

## Step 6 - Build: camera token and register tokens (and why frame 1 is special)

After patchifying, each frame has `T` patch tokens. VGGT prepends **5 extra tokens** per frame:

* **1 camera token** - an empty learnable slot. It starts as a constant, and through attention it *gathers* what the network learns about *this* camera. At the end, only this token is read by the camera head. (Compare BERT's `[CLS]` token.)
* **4 register tokens** - extra scratch-pad slots that the transformer can use for internal bookkeeping (from the "Vision Transformers Need Registers" paper). They are discarded at the output.

**The clever bit:** frame 1 gets its **own separate learnable camera/register tokens**; frames 2...N all share another set. Because frame 1 *looks different to the network*, the network can learn *"the frame with these special tokens is the reference - express everything in its coordinates."* No special-case code: the reference frame is just a different token.

*A detail we simplify:* the paper does not say how positions inside an image are encoded; we add a plain learned positional embedding per patch (see `MiniVGGT` later). I believe the released code uses rotary position embeddings - check the repo if you need to match it exactly.

## Step 7 - Build: attention and the transformer block

**Attention in plain words.** Every token asks a *question* (**query**, `q`), advertises *what it contains* (**key**, `k`), and carries a *payload* (**value**, `v`). Token `i` compares its query to every token's key (a dot product = "how relevant are you to me?"), turns those scores into weights that sum to 1 (**softmax**), and takes a **weighted average of the values**. Result: each token's new vector is *a mixture of the other tokens' information*, weighted by relevance.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax( Q K^T / sqrt(d) ) V`

*Multi-head* = do this several times in parallel with different learned projections, so different heads can look for different things (one head: "same colour", another: "same position in the floor pattern"). The `/ sqrt(d)` keeps the dot products from growing with `d` and saturating the softmax.

A **transformer block** = attention + a small MLP, each wrapped with LayerNorm and a **residual connection** (`x + ...`). Those `x +` are the exact skip connections from ResNet - they are why 48-block transformers train at all.

## Step 8 - Build: Alternating Attention (VGGT's key idea)

Now we have `N` images, each with `L = 5 + T` tokens. We must let the images **share information** (that is how the network works out *where* each camera is relative to the others) - but full attention over all `N*L` tokens is expensive and, the authors argue, makes it harder to *normalise* each image's own features. Their compromise: **alternate**.

| layer type | which tokens does a token see? | tensor reshape | purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| **frame-wise** self-attention | only tokens of **its own image** | `(B, N, L, C) -> (B*N, L, C)` | understand each image on its own (what is nearby, what is a surface) |
| **global** self-attention | tokens of **all images** | `(B, N, L, C) -> (B, N*L, C)` | match across images, compare viewpoints |

**The whole trick is a `reshape`:** the frame index `N` is either folded into the batch dimension (frames can't see each other) or folded into the sequence dimension (they can). The blocks themselves are identical. VGGT stacks `L=24` pairs `[frame -> global]`.

> Note: there is **no cross-attention** anywhere - only self-attention, just over different groupings of tokens. The paper tried cross-attention and found it worse (Table 5).

### Why not just use global attention everywhere? Cost.

Attention compares every token with every other: cost grows with the **square** of the sequence length. With `N` frames of `T` tokens each:

* frame-wise layer: `N` separate sequences of length `T`  ->  cost  `N  T`
* global layer: one sequence of length `NT`  ->  cost  `(NT) = N T`

so global attention is **`N` times** more expensive than frame-wise. Alternating means half your layers are the cheap kind. With the *real* sizes (518 px, patch 14 -> `T = 1369 + 5 = 1374` tokens/frame, width 1024):

## Step 9 - Build: the prediction heads

After the Alternating-Attention stack, each token has been enriched with information from its own image **and** all the other images. Now we **read the answers off**:

### Camera head
Take only the **camera token** of each frame (`B, N, C`). Run **4 more transformer blocks** over those `N` tokens (the cameras exchange information once more), then one **linear layer -> 9 numbers**: quaternion (4), translation (3), field of view (2). We normalise the quaternion to length 1 and make `w >= 0`; the field of view goes through `softplus` so it is positive.

### Dense head (depth, point map, track features, confidence)
Take the **patch tokens** and turn the sequence back into an image-shaped grid (`T = 64` -> 8x8), upsample by the patch size back to `32x32` and use convolutions to make per-pixel outputs. The paper uses the **DPT** head ("Vision Transformers for Dense Prediction"), which fuses features from several transformer depths and upsamples progressively; ours is a much smaller stand-in with the same *interface*:

| per-pixel output | activation | why |
|---|---|---|
| depth | `exp()` | depth must be positive |
| depth confidence `_D` | `1 + exp()` | confidence >= 1 (the loss uses it as a weight; see Step 11) |
| point map (xyz) | none | any real coordinates |
| point confidence `_P` | `1 + exp()` | same |
| track features `T` | none | descriptors for matching |

*Tracking:* the paper feeds the track features to a separate **CoTracker2** module. We implement a small stand-in in Step 11: correlate a query pixel's feature with every pixel of the other frames and take the soft-argmax.

## Step 10 - Put it together: `MiniVGGT`

Everything above wired into one module. Read `forward` top to bottom - it is the paper's Figure 2:

1. patchify every image -> tokens (+ position)
2. prepend camera + register tokens (frame 1 has its own set)
3. Alternating-Attention stack
4. camera token -> **camera head**; patch tokens -> **dense head**

## Step 11 - The loss: how it learns

The paper's total loss (their eq. 2):

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`L = L_camera + L_depth + L_pmap +   L_track`, with ` = 0.05`

* **`L_camera`**: Huber loss between predicted and true `g = [q, t, fov]`. (Huber = L2 near zero, L1 for big errors: robust.)
* **`L_depth`, `L_pmap`**: the **aleatoric-uncertainty loss** inherited from DUSt3R, plus a **gradient term** (so edges/slopes are right, not only values):

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;`L =   (D  D) +   (D  D)   log `

* **`L_track`**: L1 distance between predicted and true 2D track positions.

*Deviation in our toy:* our camera targets are tiny numbers (rotations of ~0.1), so we weight the camera term **10x** (`w_cam = 10`) instead of the paper's 1. We also use `smooth_l1_loss` (Huber-style but on an L1 scale) - PyTorch's `huber_loss` multiplies the L1 region by `delta`, which silently shrinks the gradients when `delta` is small.

### The uncertainty trick, in plain words
The network outputs a **confidence ``** for each pixel next to its prediction. The loss multiplies the error by `` - so a confident wrong answer is expensive. If that were all, the network would just set ` = 0` and pay nothing. The **`  log `** term stops that: it *rewards* being confident. The balance gives: **trust yourself exactly as much as you are accurate** - set `   / error`. Hard pixels (sky, glass, edges) automatically get low confidence; those are the pixels you should drop when building a clean point cloud. Let's verify that claim numerically rather than trust it:

### Ground-truth normalisation (why the scale is not a free choice)
A set of photos does not tell you the **scale** of the world (a toy house photographed from 10 cm looks identical to a real house photographed from 50 m) nor which point is the origin. If the training labels had arbitrary scales the network could never know what to output. So the authors make one canonical choice and **train the network to output it**:

1. express everything in camera 1's coordinates (done in `make_batch`)
2. compute the **average distance of all 3D points to the origin** and divide camera translations, depths and point maps by it.
