We did exactly this (look back at the check "mean distance = 1"). At test time VGGT therefore predicts a scene whose average point distance is ~1 - *up to this convention, the units are arbitrary* (metric scale is not recoverable from images alone).

Finally the **combined loss** used for training:

## Step 12 - Train it, then measure

Training recipe, scaled down from the paper (AdamW, warm-up + cosine learning-rate, gradient-norm clipping at 1.0 - all as in the paper; ours: a few minutes on CPU instead of 9 days on 64 A100s). Every step draws **fresh random scenes**, so there is no overfitting to a fixed dataset.

### Evaluate on scenes the model has never seen
Each metric is printed **next to a naive baseline** so that "good" means something:

* *depth:* AbsRel = mean `|pred  true| / true`. Baseline: guess the same depth for every pixel.
* *rotation of cameras 2,3 relative to camera 1:* angle error in degrees. Baseline: "all cameras face the same way as camera 1" (identity).
* *translation:* distance error (in the normalised units). Baseline: "all cameras sit on camera 1" (zero).
* *field of view:* degrees. Baseline: always guess the mean.
* *point map:* mean absolute error per coordinate; compared both for the **point head** and for **depth + camera -> unproject** (the paper found the second more accurate).

**Reading the pictures:** the predicted depth recovers the floor's gradient and the sphere shapes. Compare the **error map** with the **confidence map** (right column): the model reports its *own* uncertainty without anyone labelling it - that is the `` of the uncertainty loss at work.

**In the 3D plots** (true | point-map head | depth + camera head): the point-map head places the three views into a shared space reasonably well. The **depth + camera** panel is the one to read critically - it is only as good as the *camera head*, and see the next note.

> **An honest finding about our toy.** Depth, field of view, point maps and tracking learn quickly. **Relative camera rotation/translation of frames 2 and 3 did not emerge** in our few-minute budget (check the line printed above the plots; we also confirmed it with a camera-loss-only run of 800 steps). Estimating *how two cameras relate* requires matching features **across** images - the hardest thing the network learns, and the slowest to appear - and our model is ~1 M parameters trained for minutes on 32x32 images. The real VGGT is 1.2 B parameters trained 9 days on millions of real images (Step 14). Notice also what that implies for the paper's design: because the point head and the depth head are trained *per pixel with geometry-rich supervision*, they can learn well before pose does - exactly why the authors keep **all** the outputs as joint training signals. *Related caution:* in the table above "point-map L1 (depth+camera)" is lower than the point head's. In the paper that gap is a real finding, but with our pose unlearned it is **not** a clean replication - treat it as unverified here. (To make our toy's pose learn you would train far longer or on bigger/easier data; we leave that as an exercise rather than tune until the number looks good.)

**Tracking.** The same dense head also produced track features, trained with the geometric loss from Step 11. Does feature matching find the right pixel?

## Step 13 - Does Alternating Attention really help? (a mini version of the paper's ablation)

The paper compares three backbones with the same parameter count on ETH3D point-map error (**lower is better**):

| backbone | Acc  | Comp  | Overall  |
|---|---|---|---|
| cross-attention | 1.287 | 0.835 | 1.061 |
| global self-attention only | 1.032 | 0.621 | 0.827 |
| **alternating attention (VGGT)** | **0.901** | **0.518** | **0.709** |

*(paper Table 5)*. We repeat the idea at toy scale: same parameters, same data, same steps, same seed - only the attention pattern differs (`mode="global"` = 8 global blocks vs `mode="alternating"` = 4 frame + 4 global).

## Step 14 - The real VGGT, and how to run it

| | our `MiniVGGT` | real VGGT (paper) |
|---|---|---|
| image size | 32 x 32 | up to 518 px on the long side |
| patch size / tokens per image | 4 / 64 | 14 / 37x37 = 1369 |
| backbone | plain patch embed | **DINO** ViT patchifier |
| Alternating-Attention depth `L` | 4 | **24** (24 frame + 24 global blocks) |
| camera head | 4 blocks + linear | 4 self-attention layers + linear (same) |
| dense head | tiny conv decoder | **DPT** |
| tracking | feature correlation soft-argmax | **CoTracker2** module |
| frames per scene | 3 | 2-24 in training; tested on up to hundreds |
| parameters | ~1 M | **~1.2 billion** |
| training | minutes, CPU | 160K iterations, 64 A100 GPUs, **9 days**, bf16 + gradient checkpointing |
| data | procedural spheres + floor | ~17 datasets (CO3D, ScanNet, MegaDepth, Kubric, Habitat, ...) |

Training details from the paper that we copied: AdamW, peak LR 2e-4 with 8K warm-up iterations and cosine decay, gradient clipping 1.0, random 2-24 frames per batch, colour-jitter / blur / grayscale augmentations, loss weights `1 : 1 : 1 : 0.05`.

### Running the released model
*(Not executed here - needs a GPU and a ~GB weight download. The snippet follows the project README as I remember it; check the repository for the current API.)*

```python
import torch
from vggt.models.vggt import VGGT
from vggt.utils.load_fn import load_and_preprocess_images

device = "cuda"
model = VGGT.from_pretrained("facebook/VGGT-1B").to(device)
images = load_and_preprocess_images(["scene/1.jpg", "scene/2.jpg", "scene/3.jpg"]).to(device)

with torch.no_grad(), torch.autocast("cuda", dtype=torch.bfloat16):
    predictions = model(images)   # camera poses, depth maps, point maps, confidences, track features - one forward pass
```

Code and weights: github.com/facebookresearch/vggt (the paper links it on its first page).

## Step 15 - What is it good for?

**(paper) Figure 4 - point maps predicted on real photos** (camera frustums show the estimated poses):

<img src="vggt_assets/paper_fig4_pointmaps.png" width="760"/>

| application | how VGGT is used | paper evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **Camera pose estimation** (structure-from-motion) | read the camera head | beats optimisation-based SfM in 0.2 s (table below) |
| **Multi-view depth / dense reconstruction** | depth head + camera head, unproject to a point cloud | DTU: accuracy 0.389 without knowing the cameras, close to methods that are *given* them |
| **Point cloud / scene capture** from casual photos | point-map head | ETH3D, 0.2 s vs 7-9 s for DUSt3R / MASt3R |
| **Point tracking / feature matching** | track features + tracker | best two-view matching AUC on ScanNet among compared baselines; also helps dynamic tracking when fine-tuned |
| **Initialisation for classical pipelines** | feed VGGT's cameras and points to bundle adjustment | **"Ours (with BA)"**: even higher accuracy, 1.8 s total |
| **Feed-forward novel view synthesis** | fine-tune: replace the output heads so the network **renders a new view** from unposed photos | competitive with methods that *need* camera poses (below) |
| **A 3D-aware backbone** for other models | reuse the pretrained features | paper: "VGGT features significantly enhance downstream tasks" |

### Novel view synthesis from *unposed* photos
They fine-tune VGGT so it takes 4 input photos and the *ray image* of a target viewpoint and directly outputs the target image - **no input camera poses**:

<img src="vggt_assets/paper_fig6_nvs.png" width="420"/>

*(paper Fig. 6)* top row: input views, middle: ground truth, bottom: VGGT's synthesised views.

## Step 16 - Cheat sheet, misconceptions, further reading

### One-page summary
| piece | what it is | key shape (ours) |
|---|---|---|
| input | `N` photos of one scene | `(B, N, 3, 32, 32)` |
| patchify | conv `kernel=stride=patch` | -> `(B*N, 64, C)` |
| special tokens | 1 camera + 4 register per frame; **frame 1 has its own set** | -> `(B, N, 69, C)` |
| frame-wise attention | reshape `(B*N, L, C)` - frames isolated | cost  `NT` |
| global attention | reshape `(B, N*L, C)` - frames mix | cost  `NT` |
| Alternating Attention | `[frame, global]` x `L` (real: 24) | same shape out |
| camera head | camera token -> 4 blocks -> linear | `(B, N, 9)` = quat 4 + trans 3 + fov 2 |
| dense head (DPT) | patch tokens -> depth, point map, confidence, track features | `(B, N, , H, W)` |
| unproject | `R^T (depth  ray  t)` | depth + camera  3D points |
| loss | camera Huber + uncertainty-weighted depth & point map + 0.05  track | |
| normalisation | frame 1 = origin; divide by mean point distance | scale is a *convention* |

### Common misconceptions
1. *"VGGT has built-in geometry (epipolar constraints, triangulation, BA)."* No. It is a **plain transformer**; the only 3D-aware design choices are the alternating attention pattern, the first-frame tokens and the output parametrisation. The geometry is *learned from data*.
2. *"The point map head and depth+camera give different things."* They are **redundant by design**; the paper found unprojecting the depth and camera heads is slightly **more accurate** than the point head (0.677 vs 0.709 overall on ETH3D) - decomposing a hard task into easier ones helps.
3. *"Frame order does not matter at all."* Order matters for **frame 1 only** (it defines the world frame). The rest is permutation-equivariant - we tested it.
4. *"It outputs metric (metres) depth."* No: depth is in the **normalised unit** where the average point distance is 1. Scale is a convention fixed at training time.
5. *"Global attention would be strictly better; alternating is a speed hack."* The paper's ablation (Step 13) shows alternating is **more accurate** at equal parameter count, not just cheaper - their explanation: frame-wise layers keep each image's activations well-normalised. Our toy run (printed in Step 13) is a single-seed demonstration, not proof.
6. *"It replaces all of SfM."* It beats classical pipelines in speed and often accuracy, and adding bundle adjustment on top still **improves** it - the two are complementary.

### Where this connects to what you already know
* Every `Block` is a **residual** block (`x + f(x)`) - see `01_resnet_residual_learning.ipynb`.
* The "special tokens" are the same trick as `[CLS]` in BERT/ViT and "registers" in DINOv2.
* "Predict several related outputs jointly" is multi-task learning - Table 6 shows it helps even when outputs are mathematically redundant.

### Further reading
* Wang, Chen, Karaev, Vedaldi, Rupprecht, Novotny. *VGGT: Visual Geometry Grounded Transformer.* CVPR 2025 - arXiv:2503.11651
* Wang et al. *DUSt3R: Geometric 3D Vision Made Easy.* CVPR 2024 - the two-view point-map predecessor (arXiv:2312.14132)
* Leroy et al. *MASt3R* (2024); Karaev et al. *CoTracker* (2023/24); Ranftl et al. *Vision Transformers for Dense Prediction (DPT)* (2021); Darcet et al. *Vision Transformers Need Registers* (2023)
* Hartley & Zisserman, *Multiple View Geometry in Computer Vision* - the classical theory (pinhole camera, epipolar geometry) that VGGT learns implicitly
* Schnberger & Frahm, *COLMAP* - the classical SfM pipeline VGGT is compared to

# Autoregressive language model (`variant: autoregressive`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/autoregressive.gif" width="100%">

The animation above is generated by this notebook's code from a real (tiny) trained model. **Training**: the target row is the input shifted by one - every position learns the next character, all 47 lessons in one forward pass. **Generation**: the model box lights up once per forward pass, the real next-character distribution grows out of it, one character is sampled and flies into its slot (cyan underlines = attention of the new position). The two 50/50 branch points are held in slow motion; the counter shows one forward pass per character.

**Contents:** intuition  math  where it lives in the code  train a toy  teacher forcing and the causal
mask, hands-on  the branch points of the corpus  temperature  step-by-step trace  takeaways.

## Intuition

Read left to right and, at every position, guess the next token. If every guess is a proper probability
distribution, multiplying the guesses gives the probability of the whole sequence - that is the chain
rule, no approximation involved. Generation replays the same game: guess, commit, append, repeat.

## Math

$$p_\theta(x_{1:N}) = \prod_{i=1}^{N} p_\theta(x_i \mid x_{<i}), \qquad
  \mathcal{L}(\theta) = -\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i} \log p_\theta(x_{i+1} \mid x_{\le i}).$$

* **Teacher forcing.** In training the model always conditions on the *true* prefix, so all $N$
  predictions of a sequence are computed in **one** forward pass.
* **Causal mask.** Attention from query $i$ to key $j$ is allowed only if $j \le i$
  ($M_{ij} = -\infty$ for $j > i$), so position $i$ cannot peek at the answer $x_{i+1}$.
* **Sampling.** $x_{i+1} \sim \mathrm{softmax}(z_i / T)$ with temperature $T$; $T \to 0$ is greedy.
  Generating $N$ tokens costs $N$ sequential forward passes.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `src/hale_llm/models/models_autoregressive/model.py` | `AutoregressiveLM` + `collate_autoregressive` (labels = inputs shifted by one, pads  -100) |
| `.../loss.py` | `autoregressive_loss`: token NLL on `logits[:, :-1]` vs `labels[:, :-1]` |
| `.../sample.py` | `sample_autoregressive`: crop to `max_length`, sample, append |
| `hale_core.nn.layers.attention.build_attn_mask("causal", )` | the mask (HaleBlocks) |

## Train a toy model

Any backbone works (`model.arch`); the toy uses the default GPT stack. The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## Teacher forcing: what one batch looks like

`collate_autoregressive` builds the targets by shifting: `labels[i] = input_ids[i + 1]`.

## The branch points

The corpus has two binary choices: "model" vs "robot" right after the prompt and "write" vs "spell"
later. A well-trained model should put ~50/50 mass on the two continuations exactly there and be
near-certain everywhere else.

## Temperature

Dividing the logits by $T$ sharpens ($T<1$) or flattens ($T>1$) every distribution. With this model the
branch points are the only places where it matters - higher $T$ eventually produces characters that are
in no training sentence.

## Generation, step by step

`explainer.trace` mirrors `sample_autoregressive` operation for operation (the unit tests check that the
two produce identical tokens for the same seed) and records the distribution at every step.

## Takeaways

* Exact likelihood and the simplest possible training signal - every position is supervised in parallel.
* Inference is inherently sequential: $N$ tokens = $N$ forward passes (a KV cache makes each pass cheap,
  but not parallel). MTP (`02_mtp`) and the diffusion family (`04``07`) attack exactly this.
* Every committed token is final: there is no way to revise an early mistake.

**Exercises.** (1) Train with `train_kwargs={"steps": 30}` and watch the branch-point distributions
before convergence. (2) Swap the backbone: `get_explainer("autoregressive")` with
`train_toy("autoregressive", CORPUS, model={"arch": "lgt", "attn_type": "causal"})`.

# Multi-token prediction (`variant: mtp`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/mtp.gif" width="100%">

**Training**: head $k$'s target row is the input shifted by $k+1$; a sweep shows one position feeding all 4 heads, then the per-head loss (far heads are harder). **Generation**: self-speculative decoding - each forward checks the purple drafts from heads 1-3 (green  = head 0 agrees, red  = replaced) and drafts again in the same pass. Watch the counter: ~3 characters per forward pass instead of 1.

## Intuition

A next-token model only ever has to think one step ahead. Asking the same hidden state to also predict
2, 3, 4 tokens ahead forces it to encode a short *plan* (Gloeckle et al., 2024). The extra heads are
then free drafters at inference: they guess a few tokens, and the ordinary next-token head checks the
guesses in a single pass.

## Math

Trunk states $h_i$; head $k \in \{0,\dots,K-1\}$ outputs $p_k(\cdot \mid h_i)$, trained on $x_{i+k+1}$:

$$\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=0}^{K-1} \mathrm{CE}\big(p_k(\cdot\mid h_i),\, x_{i+k+1}\big).$$

Head 0 is the usual LM head; heads $1..K-1$ are a residual MLP followed by the **shared** unembedding
(`MTPHead`), initialised to the identity so every head starts as a next-token head.

**Self-speculative decoding.** With drafts $d_1..d_m$ appended to the prefix, one forward gives head 0's
distribution at every draft position. Walk left to right: sample $s_j \sim p_0(\cdot \mid
\text{prefix}, d_{<j})$; if $s_j = d_j$ accept and continue, otherwise emit $s_j$ and stop; if all hold,
emit a bonus sample. Every emitted token is a sample from head 0 given the true prefix, so **the output
distribution is exactly autoregressive sampling of head 0** - only the number of forwards changes.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `models_mtp/model.py` | `MultiTokenPredictionLM` (`trunk`, `forward  [K logits]`), `MTPHead`, `collate_mtp` |
| `models_mtp/loss.py` | `head_losses`, `mtp_loss` (mean of per-head shifted CEs) |
| `models_mtp/sample.py` | `sample_mtp` (draft  verify), `SpeculativeStep`, `model.tokens_per_forward` |
| `models_mtp/metrics.py` | `head_accuracy`  `head{k}_accuracy` |

## Train a toy model

Four heads (`n_mtp_heads: 4`) on a 2-layer trunk. The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## Every head on its own offset

Position 8 sits right before the "model"/"robot" choice: head 0 is predicting the branch itself, heads 1-3
predict characters *inside* the not-yet-chosen word, so their guesses only make sense for one branch.
This is where drafts get rejected.

## Greedy speculative decoding equals greedy autoregressive decoding

## Speed-up vs temperature and number of heads used

## Takeaways

* Same parameters as an AR model plus $K-1$ small heads; training adds look-ahead pressure on the trunk.
* Speculative decoding is **lossless**: identical output distribution, fewer forward passes. The speed-up
  is set by how often heads agree with head 0 - high in predictable text, low at genuine choices.
* `hale-mtp` in HaleBlocks loads an MTP checkpoint as a VLM / VLA language backbone (main head only).

**Exercises.** (1) Train with `model={"n_mtp_heads": 8}` - where does acceptance stop improving?
(2) Plot `head_accuracy` against $k$ with the `head_accuracy` metric.

# Looped transformer (`variant: looped`, `arch: looped`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/looped.gif" width="100%">

**The loop**: one block of weights $W$ applied again and again - a state dot laps the block while the unrolled strip fills; compute depth grows, parameters do not. **One forward, slowed down**: after every pass the ring under each character fills with its ACT halting probability until it stops, and the logit lens shows what the model would predict if it stopped there. **Generation**: one character per forward pass, with a bar under each showing how many loop passes its position used.

## Intuition

A standard transformer has $L$ distinct layers. A looped transformer has **one** block and runs it $T$
times, refining a hidden state - depth becomes iteration count (Universal Transformers, Dehghani et al.
2019). Because the same weights run at every pass, the model must learn a good *iterative operator*;
in exchange it can think longer at test time, or let each token decide how long to think (ACT).

## Math

$$e = \mathrm{prelude}(\mathrm{embed}(x)),\qquad h_0 = 0,\qquad
  h_t = f_W\big(h_{t-1} + e\big) + E^t \;\; (t = 1..T),\qquad \hat y = \mathrm{head}(\mathrm{coda}(h_T)).$$

* **Input injection** ($+e$ every pass) keeps the input visible however long the loop runs.
* **Depth embedding** $E^t$ tells the shared block which pass it is in (sinusoidal: defined for any $t$).
* **ACT** (Graves 2016): per token $p^i_t = \sigma(w\cdot h^i_t + b)$; the token halts when
  $\sum_t p^i_t$ would exceed $1-\epsilon$, its output is $\sum_t \lambda^i_t h^i_t$ ( = $p$, then the
  remainder $R^i$), and the loss adds the **ponder cost** $\tau \cdot \overline{N^i + R^i}$.
* **Gradients.** Backprop through $T$ tied passes multiplies Jacobians $\prod_t (I + J_{f_W})$; the
  residual form, gradient clipping, optional sandwich norms and truncated backprop (`bptt_loops`) keep it
  stable. Running to a fixed point $h^* = f_W(h^*)$ is the DEQ view (`exit_tolerance`).

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| HaleBlocks `hale_core/nn/stacks/looped.py` | `LoopSpec`, `LoopedStack`, `loop_budget`, `loop_stats`, `loop_aux_loss` |
| HaleBlocks `hale_core/nn/layers/loop.py` | `LoopDepthEmbedding`, `LoopInputInjection`, `ACTHalting` |
| `models_looped/model.py` | `LoopedLM` (`forward_with_loops`, `last_loop_stats`) |
| `models_looped/{loss,sample,metrics}.py` | NLL (ponder added by the trainer), loop-budget sampler, `loop_scaling`, `ponder_histogram` |
| `losses/auxiliary.py` | `with_auxiliary_losses`: task loss + ponder cost, used by the trainer |

## Train a toy model

One shared layer, up to 6 passes, ACT halting with ponder weight  = 0.003. The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## Parameters vs compute

## One forward in slow motion: logit lens and halting

## Passes per token during generation

## Test-time compute: loss vs number of loops

`forward_with_loops(batch, n)` runs a different loop budget at inference. ACT caps the passes per token,
so extra budget only helps tokens that have not halted; with `halting: none` and `min_loops` training
(`configs/looped/recurrent_depth.yaml`) the loss curve keeps improving past the training $T$.

## Takeaways

* Parameters of one block, depth of many; the loop count is a knob at inference.
* ACT makes depth per token adaptive;  trades compute for loss (raise it and watch the histogram shift).
* Training is BPTT over shared weights - clip gradients; use `bptt_loops` for memory, `sandwich_norm` for
  long loops. Passes are sequential, so latency grows with $T$.

**Exercises.** (1) Retrain with `"ponder_weight": 0.05` and compare passes per token. (2) Train
`halting: none` with `min_loops: 2` and plot the loss vs loop budget again. (3) Add `scratch_tokens: 4`.

# Masked diffusion language model (`variant: diffusion`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/diffusion.gif" width="100%">

**Training**: raising the noise level $t$ masks more and more characters (`?`), then the bidirectional denoiser guesses every mask at once (green = right, red = wrong). **Generation**: start from 40 masks at $t=1$; every step the model guesses *every* `?` (faint gold letters, brighter = more sure), keeps a few of them (they pop) and re-masks the rest, while $t$ walks to 0. 16 forward passes for 40 characters.

## Intuition

Instead of writing left to right, start from a blank canvas of `[MASK]` tokens and fill it in over a few
rounds, any order, many positions per round. Training mirrors this: hide a random fraction of a real
sentence and learn to restore it (MDLM, Sahoo et al. 2024; absorbing-state D3PM).

## Math

**Forward (corruption)** with the log-linear schedule $\alpha_t = 1 - t$:
$$q(x_t^i \mid x_0^i) = (1-t)\,\delta_{x_0^i} + t\,\delta_{[\mathrm{M}]}.$$

**Objective** (continuous-time ELBO): with $t \sim U[\epsilon, 1]$,
$$\mathcal{L} = \mathbb{E}_{t, x_t}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i:\,x_t^i = [\mathrm{M}]} -\log p_\theta(x_0^i \mid x_t, t)\Big].$$

**Reverse step** $t \to s < t$: a masked token stays masked with probability $s/t$, otherwise it becomes
a sample from $p_\theta(x_0^i \mid x_t)$ - so each masked position is revealed w.p. $(t-s)/t$; the last
step reveals everything.

**The catch.** Tokens revealed in the *same* step are sampled independently given $x_t$. With few steps
the model can mix branches ("rodel" = "robot"  "model"); more steps  fewer simultaneous reveals 
closer to the true joint.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `models_diffusion/model.py` | `MaskedDiffusionLM`, `_corrupt`, `diffusion_loss`, `sample_diffusion` |
| `models_diffusion/noise_schedule.py` | `LogLinearSchedule` ($\alpha_t = 1-t$) |
| `models_diffusion/metrics.py` | `masked_accuracy` |

## Train a toy model

Bidirectional attention + time conditioning (`use_time_cond: true`). The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## The forward process

## What the denoiser sees and predicts

## Sampling budget vs coherence

Fewer steps = more tokens revealed per step = more independence errors. "valid" = the output is exactly a
training sentence.

## The traced generation

## Takeaways

* Any-order, parallel generation: the number of forward passes is the number of steps, not tokens.
* The loss is an ELBO (1/t-weighted masked CE), so likelihoods are bounds, not exact.
* Fixed canvas length; tokens revealed together are conditionally independent - the "rodel" failure.
* Fixed in this repo while building the animation: the sampler's last step used to reveal with
  probability $(t-\epsilon)/t < 1$ and could leave masks in the output; it now reveals everything.

**Exercises.** (1) Sample with `temperature=0.3` and re-run `validity`. (2) Swap `arch: lgt` / `dit`
via `train_toy("diffusion", CORPUS, model={...})`.

# Discrete flow matching language model (`variant: flow_matching`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/flow_matching.gif" width="100%">

**Training**: the probability path - as $t$ goes 0  1 each character turns from a mask into data with probability $\kappa(t)=t$ (one coupled draw per character, so the path is continuous); training picks a $t$ and learns to fill the masks. **Generation**: Euler steps $t: 0 \to 1$; each step the model guesses every `?` and each jumps to its guess with probability $h\,\kappa'/(1-\kappa)$.

## Intuition

Flow matching defines a path of distributions $p_t$ from a simple source ($t=0$) to the data ($t=1$) and
learns the "velocity" that moves samples along it. For tokens with a **masking** source, the velocity is
"jump from `[MASK]` to the predicted token", so the model is again a denoiser $p_\theta(x_1 \mid x_t)$
(Campbell et al. 2024; Gat et al. 2024).

## Math

$$p_t(x^i \mid x_1^i) = \kappa(t)\,\delta_{x_1^i} + (1-\kappa(t))\,\delta_{[\mathrm{M}]},\qquad
  \kappa(0) = 0,\ \kappa(1) = 1.$$

The generating rate of the mixture path moves a masked token to $x_1$ at rate
$u_t = \frac{\kappa'(t)}{1-\kappa(t)}\, p_\theta(x_1 \mid x_t)$. Training minimises the masked CE weighted by
the same rate; with $\kappa(t) = t$ it is $1/(1-t)$:

$$\mathcal{L} = \mathbb{E}_{t \sim U[0,1)}\Big[\frac{1}{1-t}\sum_{i:\,x_t^i=[\mathrm{M}]} -\log p_\theta(x_1^i \mid x_t, t)\Big].$$

**Euler step** $t \to t+h$: each masked token jumps w.p. $h\,\kappa'(t)/(1-\kappa(t))$ (here $h/(1-t)$);
the last step has probability 1.

**Relation to masked diffusion.** With linear $\kappa$, flow time $t$ is diffusion time $1-t$:
the two training objectives and samplers coincide up to that reparameterisation. Flow matching makes the
path ($\kappa$) and the step rule explicit design choices - see `07_block_flow_matching` for a cosine path.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `models_flow_matching/model.py` | `FlowMatchingLM`, `flow_matching_loss`, `sample_flow_matching` |
| `models_flow_matching/vector_field.py` | `mask_prob` (the linear path) |
| `models_flow_matching/metrics.py` | `masked_accuracy` |

## Train a toy model

Bidirectional attention + time conditioning. The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## One coupled path from noise to data

## Rate = loss weight

## Takeaways

* The masking flow is a denoiser trained with a rate-weighted CE; sampling integrates $t: 0 \to 1$.
* Time runs the other way from masked diffusion, and the path $\kappa$ is a free design choice.
* Same parallel-decoding trade-off: fewer Euler steps  more simultaneous jumps  more mixing.

**Exercise.** Implement a cosine $\kappa$ for this variant using `MaskingPath` from
`models_block_flow_matching/path.py` and compare `validity` at 4 steps.

# Block diffusion language model (`variant: block_diffusion`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/block_diffusion.gif" width="100%">

**Training**: two streams, a clean copy and a masked copy with one $t$ per block; the sweep shows that noised block $b$ reads only the clean blocks before it and itself (cyan links, and the matching row of the block-causal mask). **Generation**: blocks left to right (orange outline), a short masked diffusion $t: 1 \to 0$ inside each; later blocks do not exist yet.

## Intuition

Autoregressive models can stop anywhere and reuse their past; diffusion models fill many tokens per step.
Block diffusion (Arriola et al. 2025) does both: autoregressive **across** blocks of $B$ tokens, masked
diffusion **within** the current block, conditioned on all previous clean blocks.

## Math

$$p_\theta(x) = \prod_{b} p_\theta\big(x^{(b)} \mid x^{(<b)}\big),\qquad
  \mathcal{L} = \sum_b \mathbb{E}_{t_b}\Big[\frac{1}{t_b}\sum_{i \in b,\ \text{masked}} -\log p_\theta(x^i \mid x_{t_b}^{(b)}, x^{(<b)})\Big].$$

**Two streams, one forward.** The backbone input is $[x ; x_t]$ (length $2N$). The `block_causal` mask
lets clean token $i$ see clean blocks $\le$ its own, and noised block $b$ see clean blocks $< b$ plus
itself. All blocks, each at its own noise level, train in one pass.

**Sampling.** For block $b$: start all-masked at $t=1$, run `sampling_steps` reveal steps with probability
$(t-s)/t$, greedy-fill leftovers, freeze the block, move on.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `models_block_diffusion/model.py` | `BlockDiffusionLM` (two-stream forward), `block_diffusion_loss`, `sample_block_diffusion` |
| HaleBlocks `build_attn_mask("block_causal", n, block_size=B)` | the $2N \times 2N$ mask |

## Train a toy model

Block-causal attention, blocks of 8, time conditioning. The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## The block-causal mask over [clean ; noised]

## A training example: one time per block

## Takeaways

* Variable-length generation and reusable clean context (KV-cacheable) like AR; parallel fills inside a
  block like diffusion. Block size interpolates between the two (B=1 is autoregressive).
* Mixing errors are confined to a block - and still possible (look for "rodel" in the GIF).

**Exercise.** Train with `block_size: 4` and `16` (`train_toy("block_diffusion", CORPUS, model={...})`)
and compare `validity` at 2 steps per block.

# Block flow matching language model (`variant: block_flow_matching`)

<img src="../../assets/explainers/block_flow_matching.gif" width="100%">

**The path**: the cosine schedule $\kappa(t) = 1-\cos(\pi t/2)$ against the linear one - characters appear slowly, then all at once. **Training**: the same two-stream block-causal layout as block diffusion, one flow time per block. **Generation**: blocks left to right; inside each block Euler steps $t: 0\to 1$ where a masked character jumps with the exact path probability $(\kappa(t')-\kappa(t))/(1-\kappa(t))$.

## Intuition

Block flow matching is to flow matching what block diffusion is to diffusion: autoregressive across blocks,
a discrete masking **flow** within the current block. The flow view makes two choices explicit: the
path $\kappa(t)$ (how fast tokens become data) and the integration rule.

## Math

$$p_t(x^i \mid x_1^i) = \kappa(t)\,\delta_{x_1^i} + (1-\kappa(t))\,\delta_{[\mathrm{M}]},\qquad
  \text{rate } \frac{\kappa'(t)}{1-\kappa(t)}\ \begin{cases}\frac{1}{1-t} & \text{linear}\\[2pt]
  \frac{\pi}{2}\tan\frac{\pi t}{2} & \text{cosine}\end{cases}$$

$$\mathcal{L} = \sum_b \mathbb{E}_{t_b}\Big[\frac{\kappa'(t_b)}{1-\kappa(t_b)}\sum_{i\in b,\ \text{masked}}
  -\log p_\theta(x_1^i \mid x_{t_b}^{(b)}, x^{(<b)}, t_b)\Big].$$

The clean stream sits at the end of the flow ($t = 1$). Each Euler step uses the exact transition
$P(\text{masked at } t \text{ is data at } t') = \frac{\kappa(t')-\kappa(t)}{1-\kappa(t)}$, so the product
over steps reaches 1 at $t=1$ for any step count.

## In the code

| File | What |
|---|---|
| `models_block_flow_matching/path.py` | `MaskingPath` (`kappa`, `rate`, `unmask_probability`), `corrupt_blocks` |
| `.../model.py` | `BlockFlowMatchingLM` (two streams, clean at t=1), collate |
| `.../loss.py`, `.../sample.py`, `.../metrics.py` | rate-weighted CE, block Euler sampler, `masked_accuracy` |
| config | `model.fm_schedule: linear | cosine`, `sample.sampling_steps` = Euler steps per block |

## Train a toy model

Blocks of 8, cosine path (`fm_schedule: cosine`). The explainer's recipe trains a ~100k-parameter model on the four corpus sentences
in a few seconds on a CPU - small enough to inspect every number, trained enough to show real
behaviour.

## Linear vs cosine paths

## A training example: per-block flow times

## Takeaways

* Same two-stream, block-causal machinery as block diffusion; the within-block generator is a flow with a
  chosen path and an exact Euler transition.
* Cosine reveals few tokens early (when the context is thin) and most near $t=1$.

**Exercise.** Train a `linear` model (`model={"fm_schedule": "linear", ...}`) and compare `validity` at
1 and 2 Euler steps per block.

# Number formats for machine learning: how a computer stores a number 
### int8, fp32, fp16, bf16, fp8, fp4 - drawn bit by bit, then measured

A neural network is a pile of numbers. **Which format you store them in** decides how much memory the model needs, how fast it runs, and whether training silently breaks. This notebook builds the idea from one question - *"how can 16 switches (bits) stand for a number?"* - and checks every claim with code (look for the `PASS` lines and the table at the end).

| step | question |
|---|---|
| 1 | why care? memory per number |
| 2 | integers: bits, and the "wheel" that makes negatives work |
| 3 | floating point: sign, exponent, mantissa - decode one by hand |
| 4 | the formats side by side (fp32, fp16, bf16, fp8, fp4) - and a decoder we verify against PyTorch on **every** bit pattern |
| 5 | the number line: where the representable numbers actually sit |
| 6 | range vs precision: why bf16 and fp16 are not the same |
| 7 | when it goes wrong: rounding, stuck sums, overflow, underflow (and loss scaling) |
| 8 | quantization: squeezing weights into int8 / int4 / fp4 / NF4 |
| 9 | how much error does a matrix multiply pick up? |
| 10 | cheat sheet |

*Prerequisite:* none beyond knowing what a bit is. Code is plain PyTorch + NumPy; CPU only.

## Step 1 - Why care? Memory per number

Everything stored costs **bytes = bits / 8**. A model with 7 billion parameters:

Fewer bits also means less data to move between memory and compute units - on modern hardware that movement is often the real bottleneck. **The catch:** fewer bits can represent fewer distinct numbers (`2^bits` of them). The rest of the notebook is about *which* numbers you keep.

## Step 2 - Integers: bits, and the wheel

With **n bits** you get `2^n` patterns. For *unsigned* integers the pattern is just binary place value: `1011 = 8+2+1 = 11`.

For *signed* integers every modern machine uses **two's complement**: the top bit counts as **2^(n1)** instead of +2^(n1). The picture to hold in your head is a **wheel**: the same 16 patterns, read two ways.

Read the wheel: `0111` is **+7**, add one and you land on `1000`, which is **8** - the number *wraps around*. That is why integer overflow does not crash; it silently flips sign. Ranges for `n` bits:

- unsigned: `0  2^n  1`  - signed (two's complement): `2^(n1)  2^(n1)  1` (one more negative than positive).

### The limit of integers
An `int8` has 256 values and they are **evenly spaced**: 1, 2, 3  . It cannot store 0.37, nor 1e-6, nor 3 000 000. A network's weights live around `0.02` and its activations can reach thousands, so we need numbers whose **spacing grows with size**. That is exactly what floating point is.

## Step 3 - Floating point: scientific notation in binary

You already know scientific notation: `6.5 = 1.625  2`. A float stores three things:

$$\text{value} = (-1)^{s}\;\times\;\underbrace{\left(1 + \tfrac{M}{2^{m}}\right)}_{\text{significand } 1.xxx}\;\times\;2^{\,E-\text{bias}}$$

| field | bits | role |
|---|---|---|
| **s** sign | 1 | 0 = positive, 1 = negative |
| **E** exponent | `e` | the power of two (stored with an offset `bias = 2^(e1)  1` so that it needs no sign) |
| **M** mantissa (fraction) | `m` | the digits after the point of `1.xxx`; the leading `1` is *implied*, not stored |

Two special rows of `E` get different rules: **E = 0** means *subnormal* (no implied 1, so numbers can shrink smoothly to zero) and **E = all ones** usually means *infinity / NaN*. Let's decode one number completely by hand, in the 8-bit format **E4M3** (4 exponent bits, 3 mantissa bits, bias 7):

Notice what the three fields do: **E picks the octave** (`[8,16)` here), **M picks one of 2^m evenly spaced points inside that octave**. With `m = 3` there are only 8 points between 8 and 16 - spacing 1.0. Flip a mantissa bit and the number changes by a little, flip an exponent bit and it changes by a factor of 2^k.

## Step 4 - The formats side by side

All of them share the layout `[ s | exponent | mantissa ]`; they only differ in how many bits go to each. *Box widths below are proportional to bit counts.*

Look at **fp16 vs bf16**: the same 16 bits, split differently. bf16 ("brain float", from Google Brain) is simply *fp32 with the bottom 16 mantissa bits cut off*, so it keeps fp32's exponent (range) and gives up precision. And **fp8 comes in two flavours** for the same reason: E4M3 favours precision, E5M2 favours range.

### A decoder, verified on every bit pattern
Instead of trusting the formula, write the decoder and compare it with PyTorch's own hardware-format casts on **all** 65 536 patterns of fp16 and bf16, all 256 of both fp8 types and 200 000 random fp32 patterns.

The decoder is eleven lines and agrees bit-for-bit with the hardware formats - so the formula in Step 3 *is* the format. Now the numbers that define each one:

Reading the table: **max** and **min normal** = the *range*; **eps** = the *precision* (the gap between 1.0 and the next number: `2^-m`, i.e. roughly `m0.3` decimal digits). Seven decimal digits for fp32, three for fp16, only **two** for bf16, and one for fp8.

Two details worth knowing:
- **E4M3 has no infinity** and only one NaN pattern, which buys it the range up to **448** instead of 240.
- **fp4 (E2M1)** has just 16 patterns - 8 non-negative values: `0, 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6`. Let us verify that and look at *all* of them.

## Step 5 - The number line: where do the numbers actually sit?

A format can only store the numbers drawn as ticks below; everything else is **rounded to the nearest tick**. Left: a linear axis. Right: the same values on a log axis.

**The key idea:** floats have **constant *relative* precision**. Between 1 and 2 an E4M3 number has 8 choices (step 1/8 = 12 % of the value); between 8 and 16 it still has 8 choices (step 1 = 12 % of the value). The *absolute* gap doubles every octave, the *relative* gap stays the same. That is what you want for numbers that span many orders of magnitude.

We can plot exactly that - the relative rounding error of each format as we sweep the magnitude (cast with PyTorch, compare to the true value):

## Step 6 - Range vs precision: why fp16  bf16

Two independent properties:

- **Range** (how big / small can you go) comes from the **exponent** bits.
- **Precision** (how finely can you resolve values) comes from the **mantissa** bits.

Same 16 bits, opposite choices:

**Rule of thumb:** bf16 trades precision for range, so training "just works" like fp32 (no overflow worries) but each number is blurrier. fp16 is more precise but overflows past 65 504, which needs extra care (Step 7). Both are 2 bytes.

## Step 7 - When it goes wrong

Four failure modes you will meet. Each is run for real below.

**(a) Not every decimal is representable.** `0.1` has no finite binary expansion, in *any* format:

**(b) Adding a small number to a big one does nothing.** Once the running sum is large, its *gap to the next number* exceeds the increment. Keep adding `1.0` to a sum, in each format, and watch it **freeze**:

At 2048 an fp16 number's neighbours are 2 apart (`2^11  2^-10`), so `2048 + 1` is a tie that rounds back to 2048 - forever. This is why **hardware accumulates in fp32** even when the inputs are fp16/bf16/fp8, and why summing millions of values (a loss, a softmax denominator, a long dot product) is done in higher precision.

**(c) Overflow.** `exp(12)`  162 755 is fine in fp32 and bf16 but **inf** in fp16. This is why softmax subtracts the row maximum before exponentiating:

**(d) Underflow, and the trick that fixes it - loss scaling.** Gradients are often tiny. The smallest fp16 number is `6e-8`; anything smaller becomes **0**, and those weights stop learning. The fix used in fp16 mixed-precision training: multiply the loss (hence every gradient) by a big factor, e.g. 2, run backward in fp16, then divide by the same factor in fp32.

**Practical summary of Step 7:** keep **master weights and accumulators in fp32**, do the heavy matrix maths in bf16 / fp16 / fp8, and (for fp16) scale the loss. This is the idea behind *mixed-precision training* - conventionally about 16 bytes per parameter with Adam (2 for the bf16/fp16 weight copy, 2 for its gradient, 12 for fp32 master weights + the two Adam moments; see the ZeRO paper, Rajbhandari et al. 2020).

## Step 8 - Quantization: squeezing trained weights into 8 or 4 bits

After training you can often store the weights in **int8 / int4 / fp4** and lose little accuracy. The idea: choose a **scale** `s`, store small integers `q = round(w / s)`, and recover `w  q  s`.

The whole game is picking `s`. If one huge outlier sets the scale, every normal weight gets rounded onto a handful of levels. Let us measure that with a toy weight matrix: Gaussian weights with std 0.02 (typical of a trained layer) plus 16 large outliers - outliers are a known problem in large language models (e.g. Dettmers et al., LLM.int8(), 2022).

With the outliers present, the per-tensor int4 scale is ~0.14 - larger than almost every real weight - so **almost the entire matrix collapses to 0**. Computing one scale per **block of 64** weights confines the damage of an outlier to its own block. Now the error numbers:

What each variant does:
- **Per-tensor int8**: one scale for the whole matrix. Easy, fast, fine for int8, fragile for int4.
- **Block-wise (here 64)**: one scale per small block, stored alongside. Costs a little extra memory (a 16-bit scale per 64 weights = +0.25 bit/weight) and bounds how far an outlier can spread.
- **fp4 / NF4**: instead of *evenly spaced* levels use a **non-uniform codebook**. fp4's levels (0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6) are denser near zero like the float number line of Step 5; NF4 (*NormalFloat*, QLoRA) places its 16 levels at the **quantiles of a normal distribution**, because trained weights are roughly bell-shaped. Look at where the levels sit:

## Step 9 - How much error does a matrix multiply pick up?

Everything in a transformer is a matmul. Take two random matrices, compute `A @ B` exactly in float64, then again after storing the inputs in each format (the product is accumulated in fp32 - as hardware does - except for the native bf16 / fp16 CPU kernels).

Every mantissa bit removed doubles the error (fp16  bf16 drops 3 bits: ~8, measured above). Note per-tensor **int8 is much noisier than fp16 here**: 256 evenly spaced levels stretched over a Gaussian whose rare 4-5 values set the scale leave typical values coarsely quantized. fp8 and int4 are far noisier **on random Gaussian inputs, which is the worst case**; in trained networks, quantization-aware calibration, per-block scales and keeping a few sensitive layers in higher precision are what make 8-bit and 4-bit models work in practice. Whether a given model keeps its accuracy at 4 bits is an empirical question this notebook does not test.

## Step 10 - Cheat sheet

**One-line memory aid** - exponent bits = range, mantissa bits = precision:

| | exponent bits (range) | mantissa bits (precision) |
|---|---|---|
| fp32 | 8 | 23 |
| bf16 | 8 | 7 |
| fp16 | 5 | 10 |
| fp8 E5M2 | 5 | 2 |
| fp8 E4M3 | 4 | 3 |
| fp4 E2M1 | 2 | 1 |

## Takeaways

1. **A format is a budget of bits.** Integers: evenly spaced. Floats: `[sign | exponent | mantissa]` = *range* (exponent)  *precision* (mantissa), with constant **relative** precision.
2. **The decoder is eleven lines** and matches PyTorch on every bit pattern (Step 4); there is no magic in fp16/bf16/fp8.
3. **bf16 vs fp16**: same size, bf16 keeps fp32's range and loses digits; fp16 keeps digits and overflows at 65 504.
4. **Low precision breaks in three ways**: values are rounded (0.1), sums freeze (2048 in fp16, 256 in bf16), and tiny/huge values underflow/overflow - hence fp32 accumulators, loss scaling, max-subtracted softmax.
5. **Quantization is "pick a scale"**: outliers make a single scale disastrous at 4 bits; block-wise scales and non-uniform codebooks (fp4, NF4) fix most of it.

### Exercises
- Write `encode(x, e, m, bias)` (the inverse of `decode`) with round-to-nearest-even and test it against `torch.tensor(x).to(dtype)`.
- Add a *stochastic rounding* quantizer and show it removes the "stuck sum" of Step 7(b) on average.
- Change the outlier magnitude in Step 8 and find the value where per-tensor int8 starts to hurt.
- Find the smallest block size at which int4 error stops improving.

### Honest limits
Everything here is measured on CPU with PyTorch's reference casts and **synthetic** data (random Gaussians, a toy weight matrix). Real hardware (tensor cores, fp8 kernels) adds its own accumulation and rounding behaviour, and accuracy-after-quantization of a real trained model is not tested. The NF4 levels are copied from the QLoRA paper, not recomputed. The memory table counts weights only (no activations, KV cache, optimizer state).

# Mixed-precision training, explained from scratch 
### Do the heavy maths in a cheap number format, keep the important bookkeeping in a precise one

In the previous notebook (`01_number_formats_datatypes`) we saw that **fp16 / bf16 are small and fast but blurry**, while **fp32 is big and precise**. *Mixed-precision training* is the recipe that gets most of the speed and memory benefit of the small formats **without** the training falling apart.

> **In one sentence:** keep a precise (fp32) *master copy* of the weights, run the expensive forward/backward passes on a cheap (fp16/bf16) *working copy*, and fix the three things that go wrong with cheap numbers.

| step | what you will see |
|---|---|
| 1 | the idea in plain words (an analogy) and **one training step as a diagram** |
| 2 | **animation**: the diagram running, stage by stage |
| 3 | problem 1 - tiny weight updates get lost  *fp32 master weights* (**animation**) |
| 4 | problem 2 - tiny gradients vanish  *loss scaling* (measured on a deep network) |
| 5 | the scaler that adapts itself: *dynamic loss scaling* (built, then **verified against PyTorch's `GradScaler`**, **animation**) |
| 6 | problem 3 - some operations must stay fp32  what `torch.autocast` does (measured) |
| 7 | putting it together: train one network four ways and compare |
| 8 | what it saves (and what it does **not**): memory, measured |
| 9 | **PyTorch recipes** - copy-paste code, each one executed here |

*Prerequisite:* know what fp32 / fp16 / bf16 mean (see notebook 01, Steps 3-7). CPU only; every claim is checked in a cell (`PASS` lines, summary table at the end).

## Step 1 - The idea, in plain words

**An analogy.** An accountant keeps the *official ledger* in exact figures (fp32). For quick scratch work she uses a cheap pocket calculator that only shows 3 digits (fp16 / bf16). Two things would go wrong if she did *everything* on the calculator:

1. A tiny daily deposit of 0.0001 added to a balance of 1.000 does nothing on the 3-digit display - after a year the balance never moved.  **keep the ledger exact**, only copy numbers to the calculator when you need to compute.
2. Some numbers are so small the calculator shows 0.  **multiply them up first**, calculate, then divide back down.

That is the whole technique. Here it is as one training step:

| in a normal (fp32) step | in a mixed-precision step |
|---|---|
| weights fp32 | **master** weights fp32 + a **cheap copy** in fp16/bf16 |
| forward & backward in fp32 | forward & backward on the *cheap copy* (this is ~all the compute) |
| loss fp32 | loss still fp32 |
| optimizer updates the weights | optimizer updates the **fp32 master**, then the copy is refreshed |
| - | gradients are multiplied by a scale `S` for backward and divided again afterwards (fp16 only) |

Read it left to right: master weights  cheap copy  forward  loss  backward  unscale  update the master. **Only the orange boxes are the expensive ones** (they contain all the big matrix multiplications), and those are the ones moved to the cheap format. Everything blue is small or delicate and stays precise.

## Step 2 - Animation: the step running, stage by stage

Now the three questions that make this *work* instead of just *run*. Each problem is real - we reproduce it, then show the fix.

## Step 3 - Problem 1: tiny weight updates are lost  keep **fp32 master weights**

Training adds many tiny updates to a weight: `w  w  lrgradient`. A typical update (say `1e-4`) is *much smaller than the gap between neighbouring fp16 numbers around 1.0* ( 0.001, from `eps = 2`, notebook 01 Step 4). The addition is **rounded away**.

Experiment: one weight `w = 1.0`, apply `3000` updates of `+1e-4`. The true answer is `1.3`.

Animation of exactly this: **left** the whole run, **right** a zoom on the first 40 updates. The *master* climbs smoothly; its fp16 *copy* is a staircase that hops each time the master passes the halfway point to the next fp16 number; the **fp16-only** weight stays frozen.

**Fix 1 - fp32 master weights.** The optimizer (SGD/Adam and its moment buffers) works on the fp32 master, so every tiny update is kept. After each step the cheap copy is refreshed from the master. This is also why the *optimizer state* does **not** get smaller in mixed precision (Step 8).

Does it matter in a real network? A last check on the "optimizer in pure fp16" idea - Adam's default `eps = 1e-8` cannot even exist in fp16:

Adam divides by `sqrt(v) + eps`; with both `v` and `eps` flushed to 0 that is `0/0 = NaN`. We will see this happen in Step 7.

## Step 4 - Problem 2: tiny gradients vanish  **loss scaling**

fp16 cannot represent anything below `6e-8` and loses precision already below `6e-5` (its *subnormal* zone, notebook 01). Gradients in deep networks - especially in **early layers** and **late in training** - are often that small. If a gradient is flushed to `0`, that weight silently stops learning.

**Fix 2 - loss scaling.** Backprop is linear: multiply the loss by `S` and *every* gradient is multiplied by `S`. Choose `S` (e.g. 4096) so they land in fp16's comfortable zone, do the backward pass, then **divide by `S` in fp32** to recover the true gradient.

Measured on a 12-layer tanh network run entirely in fp16. "late in training" is mimicked by multiplying the loss by `1e-3` (gradients shrink as the model fits the data):

**Which `S`?** Too small and gradients still underflow; too large and `S  gradient` **overflows** past 65 504 to `inf`. The best value changes during training. So frameworks adapt it automatically:

## Step 5 - Dynamic loss scaling

The rule (this is what PyTorch's `GradScaler` does):
1. start with a large `S` (2);
2. after backward, **check the gradients for `inf`/`NaN`**. If found: **skip this optimizer step** and halve `S`;
3. if `N` steps in a row were fine: **double `S`** (probe upward again).

So `S` hovers just below the overflow edge - as large as safely possible. Let me write it (8 lines) and **verify it against PyTorch's `GradScaler`** on a scripted pattern of good and bad (`inf`) steps:

Same behaviour, so the 8-line version *is* the algorithm. Now watch it work. The simulated run below has gradients that **grow** during training, so the safe `S` keeps shrinking. `S` starts far too large: `S  gradient` exceeds fp16's maximum (red line), the step overflows and is **skipped**, `S` halves, and this repeats until `S` sits just under the edge. After that it keeps probing upward every 20 clean steps (a few more skips).

**bf16 needs none of this.** It has fp32's exponent range, so gradients do not underflow/overflow: no `S`, no skipping, no `GradScaler`. That is the main reason bf16 became the default where it is supported (precision is what it gives up, notebook 01 Step 6).

## Step 6 - Problem 3: some operations must stay in fp32  what `autocast` does

You do **not** convert operations to fp16 by hand. `torch.autocast` picks a dtype **per operation**: matrix multiplies and convolutions (compute-heavy, tolerant) go low; reductions, losses and exponentials (precision-sensitive, cheap) stay fp32. We can measure its choices rather than trust a list - feed fp32 tensors through each operation under `autocast(bf16)` and see what comes out:

This is **the CPU list**; the CUDA list is similar but not identical (the PyTorch docs give the per-device lists). The pattern is the point: **heavy, tolerant ops  low precision; accuracy-critical ops  fp32**, decided automatically. Inside `autocast`, *weights stay fp32* - each is cast to the low dtype on the fly when an op needs it (that cast is the "cheap copy" of Step 1, and PyTorch caches it within the forward).

## Step 7 - Putting it together: train one network four ways

A 7-layer tanh MLP fitting a sine wave. Same data, same seed, same Adam optimizer. We implement mixed precision **by hand** with exactly the pieces above (master weights, copy, scaler) so nothing is hidden:

- **fp32** - the baseline
- **pure fp16** - weights and optimizer stored in fp16 (no master copy)
- **mixed fp16** - master fp32 + fp16 copy + dynamic loss scaling
- **mixed bf16** - master fp32 + bf16 copy, no scaling

**How to read this honestly:**
- the loss spikes (in *every* curve, fp32 included) are Adam at `lr = 1e-3` on a tiny target - not a precision effect.
- *pure fp16* diverges (the NaN is the Adam `0/0` from Step 3). Master weights are **not optional**.
- the mixed runs and fp32 all fit the curve. The tiny differences between their final losses are run-to-run noise, **not** "mixed precision is more accurate". The claim is only *"it keeps up with fp32"*.
- On this small network loss scaling made little difference to the outcome (Adam rescales updates by gradient size, which hides the effect). Step 4 showed where it **does** matter: gradients themselves being flushed to zero. In big networks that is common.
- Speed is not measured: the speed-up comes from GPU tensor cores / matrix engines, and a CPU laptop run says nothing about it.

## Step 8 - What it saves, and what it does *not*

A common surprise: **with `torch.autocast`, the weights, gradients and optimizer states are all still fp32.** The saving is mostly in *activations* - the intermediate tensors the forward pass keeps for backward, which dominate memory for large batches/sequences. We measure it with PyTorch's `saved_tensors_hooks`, which sees exactly the tensors kept for backward:

Some frameworks (e.g. DeepSpeed/ZeRO-style *fp16 model + fp32 optimizer*) go further and store the **model itself** in fp16, keeping fp32 master copies only inside the optimizer: per parameter `2 (fp16 weights) + 2 (fp16 grads) + 12 (fp32 master + Adam m, v) = 16 bytes` (ZeRO paper, Rajbhandari et al. 2020) - the same 16 bytes as plain fp32 + Adam (`4 + 4 + 8`). So *mixed precision does not reduce the memory of model state; its win is activation memory, memory traffic and matmul speed.* (Sharding or 8-bit optimizers are what shrink the 16 bytes.)

## Step 9 - PyTorch recipes (copy-paste, each executed here)

Everything above reduces to a few lines. **Each recipe below is a real function that runs on this CPU and is checked to train.**

First, the shared toy problem and an assertion helper:

### Recipe 1 - bf16 autocast (simplest; no scaler)
Use this on any hardware that supports bf16 (recent NVIDIA/AMD GPUs, TPUs, Apple silicon, modern CPUs).

### Recipe 2 - fp16 autocast + `GradScaler`
Use this for fp16 (older GPUs without bf16, or when you specifically want fp16). The scaler is what Step 5 built.

### Recipe 3 - with gradient clipping and gradient accumulation
Two things people forget: **clip the *unscaled* gradients** (so call `scaler.unscale_(opt)` first, otherwise you clip `S  gradient`), and divide the loss by the number of accumulation steps. Same code works for bf16 - just drop the scaler lines.

### Recipe 4 - choose the setup per device
bf16 where it is supported, else fp16 + scaler. (This repo has a `device.py` that resolves cpu / cuda / rocm / mps - `HaloDiffusionLLM/src/hale_llm/utils/device.py`; the function below only decides the *dtype* given a device.)

Only the **cpu** path of Recipe 4 is executed here; the `cuda`/`mps` branches are the documented patterns and were **not** run on that hardware in this notebook.

### The recipes agree with the by-hand version
Recipes 1-2 use PyTorch's `autocast` + `GradScaler`; Step 7 used our hand-written version. Different problem, so we compare *behaviour*: both reach a loss close to the fp32 run on their own problem.

### Checklist (the usual mistakes)
- **Keep the loss in fp32:** `mse_loss(pred.float(), y)`; most built-in losses already do it under autocast.
- **bf16  no scaler. fp16  always a scaler.**
- **`scaler.unscale_(opt)` before clipping** (Recipe 3), and one `scaler.update()` per optimizer step.
- **Save the scaler with the checkpoint** (`scaler.state_dict()`), or resumed fp16 runs restart at `S = 65536` and skip steps.
- **Do not call `.half()` on the model for training** - that is the "pure fp16" failure of Step 7. Let `autocast` do it per-op.
- **Look for `inf`/`NaN` losses and a collapsing scale** - if `S` keeps halving, something genuinely overflows (a bad learning rate, an unstable layer), it is not the scaler's fault.

## Takeaways

1. **Mixed precision = fp32 master weights + a cheap fp16/bf16 working copy.** Heavy compute (matmuls) runs cheap; delicate things (weights, optimizer, loss, reductions) stay precise.
2. **Three problems, three fixes:** tiny updates lost  *fp32 master weights*; tiny gradients underflow  *loss scaling* (dynamic: halve on overflow, grow when safe); precision-critical ops  *autocast keeps them fp32*.
3. **bf16 vs fp16:** bf16 has the range, so no scaler; fp16 needs the scaler.
4. **Honest accounting:** it does not shrink weights/grads/optimizer state (all still fp32 here); it shrinks activations and enables fast low-precision matmuls. Speed was not measured on this CPU.
5. In code it is `torch.autocast` (+ `GradScaler` for fp16) around the forward pass and loss - Step 9.

### Exercises
- In Step 7, replace Adam by plain SGD for *pure fp16* and find the learning rate below which it stops learning (hint: update < half an fp16 gap).
- Change the scaler `interval` in Step 5 to 5 and to 500 - how many steps are skipped vs how large `S` stays?
- Run Step 4 with `torch.bfloat16`: does it need scaling?
- Add `fp8` to Step 6's idea: which ops would you keep in 16-bit? (Hint: see the fp8 formats in notebook 01.)

### Honest limits
CPU only, tiny models, one seed: the *mechanisms* (lost updates, underflowing gradients, the scaler's behaviour, which ops autocast lowers, what memory is saved) are measured, but **speed-ups are not**, and real large-model training adds further effects (fp8 recipes, sharded optimizers, different per-device op lists). The cuda/mps branches of Recipe 4 were not executed.

# How to turn on mixed-precision training - line by line 
### A small, hands-on notebook: start from a normal training loop and switch mixed precision on, one line at a time

You already know *why* (notebook `02_mixed_precision_training`). This notebook is only about **how**: *which lines do you add to your training loop, and what does each one do?*

**The short answer.** You add **three things** to a normal loop:

| # | add | when | what it gives you |
|---|---|---|---|
|  | `with torch.autocast(...):` around the forward pass | always | matmuls run in bf16/fp16 |
|  | `GradScaler` (`scale  step  update`) | **fp16 only** | gradients do not underflow |
|  | `scaler.unscale_(opt)` before gradient clipping | only if you clip, **fp16 only** | clipping sees the true gradient size |

Nothing else changes: the model, the optimizer, the data and the learning rate stay the same.

| step | content |
|---|---|
| 1 | the normal fp32 loop, every line explained |
| 2 |  bf16: add `autocast` - and **prove** it is on |
| 3 |  fp16: add `GradScaler` - watch the scale, a skipped step |
| 4 |  add gradient clipping correctly |
| 5 | the one universal loop (the template to copy) |
| 6 | the same thing as a config switch in this repo (`AMPManager`) |
| 7 | one-liners in Hugging Face Trainer / Lightning / Accelerate |
| 8 | "is it really on?" and common errors |

Every cell below is executed and checked (`PASS` lines); CPU only.

## Step 1 - The normal (fp32) loop, every line explained

This is the loop we will *modify*. Read it once carefully - the other loops differ from it by only a few lines.

| line | what it does | why it matters here |
|---|---|---|
| **(1)** `model, x, y = toy()` | builds the network and the data. All weights are created as **fp32** (4 bytes each) - PyTorch's default dtype. | These fp32 weights will become the *master weights* in mixed precision. We never convert them. |
| **(2)** `opt = AdamW(model.parameters(), lr=3e-3)` | creates the optimizer. It keeps its own fp32 state (two extra numbers per weight). | The optimizer updates the **fp32** weights, which is exactly what we want to keep. |
| **(3)** `losses = []` | a list to record the loss per step. | bookkeeping only. |
| **(4)** `for step in range(steps)` | one iteration = one optimizer step on the whole (tiny) data set. | - |
| **(5)** `opt.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)` | clears the gradients left over from the previous step. `set_to_none=True` frees the memory instead of filling it with zeros (faster). | Gradients *accumulate* in PyTorch, so forgetting this adds old gradients to new ones. |
| **(6)** `pred = model(x)` | the **forward pass**: pushes `x` through the layers. This is where nearly all the arithmetic (matrix multiplies) happens. | **This is the line mixed precision speeds up.** |
| **(7)** `loss = mse_loss(pred, y)` | one number saying how wrong `pred` is: the mean of `(pred  y)`. | It is an average over many values, so we will keep it in fp32. |
| **(8)** `loss.backward()` | the **backward pass**: computes `d loss / d weight` for every weight and stores it in `weight.grad`. | The second big compute cost. Runs in whatever dtype the forward used. |
| **(9)** `opt.step()` | uses `weight.grad` to update each weight: `w  w  lr  (adjusted gradient)`. | Must happen in fp32 (tiny updates, see notebook 02 Step 3). |
| **(10)** `losses.append(loss.item())` | `.item()` turns the 1-element tensor into a Python float. | - |

Everything we add next goes around lines **(6)-(9)**.

## Step 2 -  bf16: add `torch.autocast`  (the simplest way to turn it on)

Here is the *entire* change, as a diff. Only **two lines** are different:

```diff
   for step in range(steps):
       opt.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)
-      pred = model(x)
-      loss = nn.functional.mse_loss(pred, y)
+      with torch.autocast(device_type="cpu", dtype=torch.bfloat16):      #   NEW
+          pred = model(x)                                                 #   (indented)
+      loss = nn.functional.mse_loss(pred.float(), y)                      #  .float() NEW
       loss.backward()
       opt.step()
```

### What each new line means

**(A) `with torch.autocast(device_type="cpu", dtype=torch.bfloat16):`**
- `torch.autocast(...)` creates a *context*: a region of code with a changed rule.
- `with ...:` means "apply the rule to every line indented below, and restore the normal rule when the block ends". Outside the block nothing is different.
- `device_type="cpu"` - which hardware's operations to change: `"cpu"`, `"cuda"` (NVIDIA **and** AMD ROCm both use `"cuda"`), or `"mps"` (Apple). It must match where your tensors live.
- `dtype=torch.bfloat16` - the *cheap* dtype to use. Use `torch.float16` for fp16 (then you also need the scaler, Step 3).
- The rule: *for each operation, autocast looks up a list. Heavy operations (matmul, linear, convolution) run in the cheap dtype; precision-sensitive ones (sums, losses, softmax) stay fp32.* Inputs are cast on the fly - **your model's weights are not changed**.

**(B) `pred = model(x)`** - the identical line as before, now *inside* the block. Each `nn.Linear` casts its fp32 weight and input to bf16 for the multiply (the "cheap copy" of notebook 02) and returns a bf16 result.

**(C) `mse_loss(pred.float(), y)`** - `pred` is bf16 now. `.float()` converts it to fp32 so that the squaring and averaging - and therefore the number that `backward()` differentiates - are done precisely. (Many built-in losses do this automatically under autocast; writing `.float()` makes it explicit and safe.)

**(D) `loss.backward()`** - **unchanged, and outside the block.** You do not wrap `backward` in autocast: PyTorch automatically runs each backward operation in the same dtype as its forward operation.

**(E) `opt.step()`** - **unchanged.** The weights it updates are still the fp32 originals, so this *is* the master-weights update.

### Prove it is on
Don't trust that autocast "worked" - look at the dtypes. We attach a small observer to every `Linear` layer and also look at the weights, the loss and the gradients:

That table is the whole trick in one picture: **your fp32 data enters the first layer, every layer then outputs bf16 (so later layers receive bf16), while weights, gradients and the loss stay fp32.** For bf16 you are done - no scaler needed, because bf16 has the same range as fp32.

## Step 3 -  fp16: add `GradScaler`

fp16 has a much smaller range, so small gradients can underflow to 0. `GradScaler` fixes that with *loss scaling* (notebook 02, Steps 4-5). The change to the loop - the autocast lines are the same as Step 2 except `dtype=torch.float16`:

```diff
+ scaler = torch.amp.GradScaler("cpu")                  #  create ONCE, before the loop
   for step in range(steps):
       opt.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)
       with torch.autocast(device_type="cpu", dtype=torch.float16):
           pred = model(x)
       loss = nn.functional.mse_loss(pred.float(), y)
-      loss.backward()
-      opt.step()
+      scaler.scale(loss).backward()                     #  backward on (loss  S)
+      scaler.step(opt)                                  #  unscale, check for inf/NaN, then opt.step() (or skip)
+      scaler.update()                                   #  adapt S for the next step
```

### What each scaler line means

**(A) `scaler = torch.amp.GradScaler("cpu")`** - creates the scaler **once, outside the loop** (it has to remember `S` between steps). The argument is the device type. `S` starts at `2 = 65536`. Optional arguments: `init_scale`, `growth_factor` (default 2), `backoff_factor` (0.5), `growth_interval` (2000 steps). Pass `enabled=False` and it turns into a harmless pass-through (handy, Step 5).

**(B) `scaler.scale(loss).backward()`** - `scaler.scale(loss)` returns `loss  S`. Calling `.backward()` on it makes *every* gradient `S` times bigger, which lifts tiny gradients out of fp16's underflow zone.

**(C) `scaler.step(opt)`** - replaces `opt.step()`. Inside it: (1) divides all gradients by `S` again (so the optimizer sees true gradients), (2) checks them for `inf`/`NaN`, (3) if all finite calls `opt.step()`, otherwise **skips the update** so one bad batch cannot corrupt the weights.

**(D) `scaler.update()`** - once per step, after `step`. If the step was skipped it halves `S`; after `growth_interval` good steps in a row it doubles `S`.

Let's watch these happen with real numbers instead of taking the words on trust:

Both behaviours are the ones promised: scaled by `S`, unscaled before the update, and **skip + halve `S` on overflow**. In a long run you will see `S` start at 65536, drop a few times in the first steps, then sit around a stable value.

**Which one to use?** bf16 if your hardware supports it (no scaler, fewer surprises); fp16 + scaler otherwise. On NVIDIA/AMD GPUs `torch.cuda.is_bf16_supported()` tells you; Step 5 uses it.

## Step 4 -  Gradient clipping: call `unscale_` first (fp16 only)

Clipping caps the *size* of the gradient vector (`clip_grad_norm_(params, max_norm)`), which stabilises training. With a scaler the gradients are still multiplied by `S` at that point, so you would clip the wrong thing - `S` times too large. The fix is one line:

```diff
   scaler.scale(loss).backward()
+  scaler.unscale_(opt)                                   #  gradients are now the true ones
+  torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 1.0)
   scaler.step(opt)
   scaler.update()
```
Measured:

Rules of thumb: **one `unscale_` per optimizer step**, **before** clipping, **before** `scaler.step`. With bf16 there is no scaler, so you simply clip as you always did.

## Step 5 - The universal loop: one function for fp32, bf16 and fp16

In real code you want to switch precision with a flag, not edit the loop. The trick: a **disabled** `GradScaler` and a **disabled** `autocast` are no-ops, so a single loop covers all three modes. *This is the template to copy.*

**Line by line (only what is new compared with the loops above):**

| line | explanation |
|---|---|
| **(3)** `use_amp = amp_dtype is not None` | `None` means "no mixed precision"; the same loop then behaves as the plain fp32 loop. |
| **(4)** `use_scaler = amp_dtype == torch.float16` | the scaler is needed **only** for fp16. bf16 and fp32 skip it. |
| **(5)** `GradScaler(device_type, enabled=use_scaler)` | when `enabled=False`, `scale(loss)` returns `loss` unchanged and `step`/`update`/`unscale_` do nothing but call the plain optimizer. One code path for every mode. |
| **(7)** `torch.autocast(..., dtype=amp_dtype or torch.float32, enabled=use_amp)` | `enabled=False` makes the `with` block do nothing. The `or torch.float32` only avoids passing `None` as a dtype. |
| **(10)** `scaler.scale(loss).backward()` | with a disabled scaler this is exactly `loss.backward()`. |
| **(11)-(12)** `unscale_` then `clip_grad_norm_` | the correct order from Step 4. |
| **(13)-(14)** `scaler.step(opt)`, `scaler.update()` | with a disabled scaler `step` just calls `opt.step()`. |

Lines (1), (2), (6), (8), (9), (15) are the same as in the fp32 loop of Step 1. **That is the point: mixed precision is a thin wrapper, not a rewrite.**

*Why do all three print almost the same final loss?* The task is easy, so every run converges to the data's noise floor (~0.011); the checks above show the curves differ along the way. On a large model the differences are what you tune and monitor.

**`pick_precision`** encodes the earlier advice: bf16 wherever it exists (CPU, Apple, recent GPUs), fp16 + scaler on older GPUs. Only the CPU branch is executed here.

## Step 6 - The same thing as a *config switch* in this repo

You usually do not hand-write that loop. `HaleBlocks` (the shared training library under every Hale* repo) already contains it:

- **config**: `HaleBlocks/src/hale_core/config/sections/amp.py`  `AMPConfig` (the knobs)
- **engine**: `HaleBlocks/src/hale_core/training/amp.py`  `AMPManager` (autocast + scaler behind five methods)
- **trainer**: `HaleBlocks/src/hale_core/training/trainer/trainer.py` calls it inside its loop

To turn mixed precision on you set **config values, not code**:

```yaml
train:
  amp:
    enabled: true        # master switch (default: false = plain fp32)
    dtype: bf16          # "bf16" | "fp16" | "fp32"
    # fp16 only:
    init_scale: 65536.0
    growth_factor: 2.0
    backoff_factor: 0.5
    growth_interval: 2000
    enabled_on_cpu: false   # CPU autocast is opt-in
```

Here is what the trainer does with it - the **same pattern as our universal loop**, with each of our lines mapped to a method:

| our loop (Step 5) | `AMPManager` method used by the trainer |
|---|---|
| `with torch.autocast(...)` | `with amp.forward_context():` |
| `scaler.scale(loss).backward()` | `amp.scale_loss(loss).backward()` |
| `scaler.unscale_(opt)` | `amp.unscale(opt)` |
| `scaler.step(opt)` | `amp.step(opt)` |
| `scaler.update()` | `amp.update()` |
| `opt.zero_grad(set_to_none=True)` | `amp.zero_grad(opt)` |
| (log the scale) | `amp.stats()`  `{"amp/scale": ..., "amp/dtype": ...}` |
| (save with checkpoint) | `amp.state_dict()` / `amp.load_state_dict()` |

Let's drive the real `AMPManager` source file with the same toy problem. (We load the file directly so the notebook has no dependency on the package being installed; `AMPConfig` only matters for its field names; we use a plain namespace with the same fields and defaults.)

Three details worth noticing in the output:

1. **Default = off.** `enabled: false` by default, so existing runs are unchanged until you opt in.
2. **CPU needs two switches** (`enabled: true` *and* `enabled_on_cpu: true`). The repo's gate deliberately limits CPU autocast to bf16; fp16 on CPU is refused outright (last case above: `amp.enabled=False` even with `enabled_on_cpu=True`). So the manager's fp16 + `GradScaler` path needs a GPU and was **not exercised end to end here**; the scaler mechanics themselves were verified with plain `GradScaler("cpu")` in Step 3.
3. **The scale is part of the checkpoint.** If you resume an fp16 run without restoring it, the scale resets to 65 536 and the first steps get skipped. The trainer handles this (`amp_state_dict` in the checkpoint).

*Not run here:* the full `Trainer` end to end - that needs a real config/dataset; the loop above is the `Trainer`'s own sequence of `AMPManager` calls (`trainer.py`, "Forward / Backward / Grad clipping / Optimizer step" blocks).

## Step 7 - Higher-level libraries: it is one flag

If you use a framework, the three additions are hidden behind one option. **These are shown for reference from their documentation and were *not* executed here (the packages are not installed in this environment).**

```python
# Hugging Face Transformers  -  the Trainer builds autocast (+ GradScaler for fp16) for you
from transformers import TrainingArguments
args = TrainingArguments(output_dir="out", bf16=True)      # or fp16=True

# PyTorch Lightning
import lightning as L
trainer = L.Trainer(precision="bf16-mixed")                # or "16-mixed" for fp16 + scaler

# Hugging Face Accelerate
from accelerate import Accelerator
accelerator = Accelerator(mixed_precision="bf16")          # or "fp16"
#   then:  accelerator.backward(loss)   instead of  loss.backward()
```
All of them do exactly what Steps 2-4 did by hand.

## Step 8 - "Is it really on?" and common errors

**A 10-second test** - run one batch and print dtypes (as in Step 2): the layer outputs should be bf16/fp16, while weights, loss and gradients stay fp32.

| symptom | usual cause | fix |
|---|---|---|
| no speed/memory change at all | `autocast` not entered, wrong `device_type`, or the device has no fast low-precision path | print `torch.is_autocast_enabled(device_type)` inside the block |
| `RuntimeError: expected scalar type Half but found Float` | you called `.half()` on the model/inputs yourself *and* use autocast, or mixed dtypes by hand | do not `.half()` the model; leave weights fp32 and let autocast cast per-op |
| loss becomes `NaN`/`inf` right away (fp16) | no scaler, or an overflow inside the model | add `GradScaler`; try bf16; lower the learning rate |
| fp16 training stalls, scale keeps halving | genuine overflow in some layer, not a scaler problem | check inputs/learning rate; use bf16 |
| clipping seems to do nothing / too much | `clip_grad_norm_` called on **scaled** gradients | call `scaler.unscale_(opt)` first |
| resumed run skips many steps | scaler state not restored | save/load `scaler.state_dict()` |
| `RuntimeError: unscale_() has already been called` | `unscale_` twice in one step | once per step |

## Takeaways

1. **Turning on mixed precision = wrapping the forward pass in `torch.autocast`.** For bf16 that is the *only* change (plus `.float()` on the loss input).
2. **fp16 additionally needs a `GradScaler`:** `scale(loss).backward()`  `step(opt)`  `update()`; call `unscale_(opt)` before clipping.
3. **Weights, gradients, loss and optimizer stay fp32** - verified by printing dtypes. You never convert the model with `.half()`.
4. **One loop, three modes:** disabled autocast/scaler are no-ops, so a flag switches precision (Step 5). In this repo that flag is `train.amp.enabled` / `train.amp.dtype`.
5. **Verify, don't assume:** one batch and a dtype print tells you whether it is really on.

### Exercises
- Make `train()` accept `amp_dtype="auto"` and call `pick_precision` for you.
- In Step 3, set `init_scale=2**30` and watch how many steps are skipped and where `S` settles.
- Add gradient accumulation to the universal loop (divide the loss by the number of micro-batches; call `unscale_`/`step`/`update` once per optimizer step).
- Turn on `amp.enabled` in a copy of a HaleBlocks config and check that `amp/scale` and `amp/dtype` appear in the logged metrics.

### Honest limits
CPU only, a tiny model: this notebook shows **correctness and mechanics** (which lines, which dtypes, what the scaler does), **not speed** - the speed-up needs GPU tensor cores or similar hardware. The `cuda`/`mps` branch of `pick_precision`, the Hugging Face / Lightning / Accelerate snippets and the full `Trainer` were **not executed**.

# Activation functions and their derivatives: the full catalogue 
### ReLU  GELU  Swish/SiLU  Mish  SwiGLU/GeGLU - formulas, derivatives, and what they do to gradients

An activation function is the only non-linearity in a neural network, and **its derivative is what back-propagation multiplies by at every layer**. So the shape of `f'(x)` decides whether gradients vanish, explode, or die. This notebook:

| step | what |
|---|---|
| 1 | the catalogue: ~40 activations, each with `f(x)` **and** a hand-derived `f'(x)` |
| 2 | verification: every derivative checked against PyTorch autograd *and* finite differences |
| 3 | pictures: `f` and `f'` for every family |
| 4 | the modern LLM/vision set side by side: GELU, SiLU/Swish, Mish, Hardswish, ReLU, ... |
| 5 | derivative facts: GELU/Swish are *non-monotonic*, the `0.5` at zero, learnable- Swish |
| 6 | gated units (GLU, GeGLU, SwiGLU, ReGLU): two inputs, two derivatives |
| 7 | softmax and maxout: vector-valued activations and their Jacobians |
| 8 | experiment A: gradient flow through a 40-layer net, per activation |
| 9 | experiment B: dead units and a small training race |
| 10 | cheat sheet and honest limits |

*Prerequisite:* the chain rule. Plain PyTorch + NumPy, CPU only.
