## Step 1 - The catalogue

Each entry is `(name, family, f, df, formula, note)`. `df` is written **by hand** from calculus, *not* taken from autograd - Step 2 then proves it right.

Useful building blocks (all derivatives are one-liners):

- `'(x) = (x)(1-(x))` &nbsp; `tanh'(x) = 1 - tanh(x)` &nbsp; `softplus'(x) = (x)` &nbsp; `'(x) = (x)` (Gaussian pdf) &nbsp; `erf'(x) = (2/) e^{-x}`
- product rule for anything of the form `xg(x)`: `(xg)' = g + xg'` - this single rule gives Swish, Mish, GELU, Serf, Hardswish.

## Step 2 - Verify every derivative two independent ways

A hand-derived derivative is easy to get wrong by a sign or a factor. For each activation we compare `df` with

1. **PyTorch autograd** of `f` (exact, from the computation graph), and
2. a **central finite difference** `(f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / 2h` with `h = 1e-6` (float64, no autograd at all).

Test points are a dense grid with the *kinks* removed (ReLU at 0, ReLU6 at 0/6, hard-sigmoid at 3, shrink thresholds ...) because derivatives there are conventions, not facts.

## Step 3 - Pictures: every `f` (solid) and `f'` (dashed)

One panel per activation, same x-range, so shapes are comparable. Look for: **where `f'` is 0** (no gradient), **where `f'` exceeds 1** (amplification), and **whether `f'` is smooth**.

## Step 4 - The modern set, side by side

The activations that actually appear in current models. Left: `f(x)`. Right: `f'(x)`.

Things to see on the right-hand plot:

- **ReLU's** derivative is a hard 0/1 switch.
- **GELU, SiLU, Mish** have *smooth* derivatives that **overshoot 1** (to 1.13, 1.10, 1.09) before settling at 1 - the "bump".
- On the left, for `x < 0` GELU/SiLU/Mish **dip below zero** and come back: they are *non-monotonic*. That is the price for being smooth and is a feature, not a bug.

## Step 5 - Derivative facts worth knowing (all measured)

1. **Where is the minimum / max slope?** Sigmoid max slope is `0.25` (so 10 sigmoid layers can shrink a gradient by `0.25^10  1e-6`). Tanh is `1`. ReLU is `1` on half the line.
2. **Swish/GELU/Mish overshoot** `f' > 1` - measured below.
3. **GELU at 0**: `f(0)=0`, `f'(0)=(0)=0.5`. Same for SiLU (`(0)=0.5`). ReLU has no derivative there.
4. **Non-monotonic minimum**: location and depth of the dip.
5. **Swish's ** is learnable: `f/ = x (x)(1-(x))`. Verified by autograd.
6. **Sigmoid/Tanh/SiLU/Softplus are *closed* under differentiation** (`f'` is written with `f`), which makes the backward pass reuse the forward value.

## Step 6 - Gated units: GLU, ReGLU, GeGLU, SwiGLU

Almost every modern LLM feed-forward block (LLaMA, Qwen, Gemma, PaLM, OLMo ...) replaces `Wact(Wx)` by a **gated** form (Shazeer 2020):

`FFN(x) = W ( act(W_g x)  (W_u x) )`

Write `a = W_u x` (the *value* branch) and `b = W_g x` (the *gate* branch). Then `h(a, b) = a  g(b)` has **two** partial derivatives:

`h/a = g(b)`  &nbsp;&nbsp;  `h/b = a  g'(b)`

| name | gate `g` | note |
|---|---|---|
| GLU |  | Dauphin 2017 |
| ReGLU | ReLU | |
| **GeGLU** | GELU | Gemma family |
| **SwiGLU** | SiLU | LLaMA, Qwen, PaLM, OLMo |
| Bilinear | identity | |

Because there are two branches, the hidden width is usually cut to `4d` to keep the parameter count equal to a plain MLP.

## Step 7 - Vector-valued activations: softmax and maxout

Not every activation maps scalar  scalar.

**Softmax** `p_i = e^{z_i} / _j e^{z_j}` couples all outputs. Its derivative is a **Jacobian matrix**:

`p_i/z_j = p_i (_ij  p_j)`  &nbsp;&nbsp; `J = diag(p)  p p`

Properties: symmetric, rows sum to 0 (adding a constant to all logits changes nothing), rank `n1`. With cross-entropy the whole chain collapses to `L/z = p  y`.

**Maxout** `max_k (w_kx + b_k)` has derivative 1 for the winning piece and 0 for the others (a sub-gradient selector).

## Step 8 - Experiment A: gradient flow through a deep network

Take a plain 40-layer MLP (width 256, no residuals, no normalisation), He/Kaiming init (`_w = 2/fan_in`, which assumes ReLU), and push gradients back from a loss on the output. For each activation we measure

`ratio_ = L/h_ / L/h_40`

at every layer . `ratio  1` = healthy; ` 1` = **vanishing**; ` 1` = **exploding**.

This is a stress test, **not** a recommendation: real networks use residuals and norms that hide most of these differences. It isolates what each *derivative* does to the signal. The init is the same for all, so activations whose gain differs from ReLU's will drift. **Measured surprise:** SiLU and Hardswish lose ~5 orders of magnitude here while ReLU stays near 1 - not because they are bad, but because `_w=2/fan_in` is ReLU's constant. SiLU/GELU shrink the signal's variance more than ReLU does (their `f` is smaller and their `f'` averages below 1 on the bulk of a Gaussian), so they need a larger init gain, or - in practice - LayerNorm/residuals, which absorb the mismatch. Exercise 3 asks you to compute that gain.

## Step 9 - Experiment B: dead units, and a small training race

**Dead units.** A ReLU unit whose pre-activation is negative for *every* input has `f' = 0` there and never recovers. We initialise a 1-hidden-layer net with a **large negative bias** (a harsh but realistic situation after one bad update) and count units with `|f/z| < 1e-6` on all data. Smooth/leaky activations keep a non-zero slope.

**Training race.** Two-spiral classification, a 4-layer MLP (width 64), AdamW, identical seeds and data, 600 steps. Small and synthetic, so read it as a sanity check about *optimisation ease*, not a ranking of activations for LLMs.

## Step 10 - The whole table, `f` and `f'`

Every activation in the catalogue with its formula (derivative is in the next column, derived by hand and verified in Step 2).

## Cheat sheet

| need | pick | why (derivative view) |
|---|---|---|
| default CNN / small MLP | **ReLU / Leaky ReLU** | `f'{0,1}`: no shrinkage, free to compute; dead units possible |
| transformer FFN, plain | **GELU** (exact or tanh) | smooth `f'`, `f'(0)=0.5`, slight overshoot |
| transformer FFN, modern LLM | **SwiGLU / GeGLU** | gate  value: `/a = g(b)`, `/b = ag'(b)` |
| vision, YOLO-style | **SiLU / Mish** | smooth, non-monotonic, `f'` up to 1.1 |
| mobile / int8 | **Hardswish, ReLU6** | piecewise-linear, no `exp` |
| self-normalising MLP | **SELU** (+ LeCun init, alpha-dropout) | `` chosen so mean/var are fixed points |
| implicit neural reps | **Sine, Snake** | periodic, `f'` also periodic; needs special init |
| output probabilities | **Softmax / Sigmoid** | Jacobian `diag(p)pp`; with CE gives `py` |
| never | sigmoid/tanh deep in a plain stack | max slope 0.25 / saturates -> vanishing |

## Exercises
1. Add **SMELU**, **Softexp**, **ShiftedSoftplus** and **ReLU-with-a-leak-schedule**; derive `f'` by hand and plug them into the Step-2 checker.
2. Derive the **second derivative** of GELU, `f'' = 2(x)  x(x)`, and verify with `torch.autograd.functional.hessian`. Why does a smooth `f''` matter for second-order optimisers?
3. Compute `E[f'(z)]` for `z~N(0,1)` for each activation - this is the per-layer gain of the backward signal. Which init constant makes it 1 for GELU/SiLU?
4. Replace the 40-layer MLP of Step 8 by one with residual connections + LayerNorm and see how much of the difference disappears.

## Honest limits
- Derivatives are verified in **float64** against autograd and finite differences at points away from kinks; at the kinks themselves (ReLU at 0 etc.) the value is a **convention** (PyTorch uses 0 for ReLU).
- Steps 8-9 use synthetic data and small nets (random-feature gradient flow; two spirals). They show the *mechanism*, not which activation wins in a real LLM - residual streams, normalisation and the optimiser change the picture a lot.
- The "used in" notes (BERT, LLaMA, Gemma, YOLOv4, MobileNetV3 ...) are recalled from the papers/model cards and were **not** re-checked against model code in this session.
- Several activations (Serf, ELiSH, Snake, LeCun tanh) are included for completeness; they are not mainstream today.

# 00  Reinforcement Learning Foundations: from rewards to Bellman, and why an LLM is a policy

**Who this is for:** you know PyTorch and supervised learning (loss, gradient, optimizer) but have never
studied reinforcement learning (RL). By the end of this notebook you will be able to:

1. describe any RL problem as a **Markov Decision Process (MDP)**;
2. compute **returns**, **value functions** $V^\pi$, $Q^\pi$ and **advantages** $A^\pi$ by hand and in code;
3. derive and implement the **Bellman equations**, **policy evaluation**, **value iteration** and **policy iteration** from scratch;
4. see exactly how *text generation by an LLM* (and a VLM) is the same MDP, which is the foundation for every later notebook (PPO-RLHF, DPO, GRPO, ...).

Where this notebook sits in the course:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    N00["<b>00 Foundations</b><br/>MDP, Bellman<br/>(you are here)"]:::here --> N01[01 Bandits]
    N01 --> N02[02 Tabular TD / Q-learning]
    N02 --> N03[03 DQN]
    N02 --> N04[04 Policy gradient]
    N04 --> N05[05 Actor-critic, GAE]
    N05 --> N06[06 TRPO / PPO]
    N06 --> N07[07 RLHF]
    N07 --> N08[08 DPO family]
    N07 --> N09[09 GRPO family]
    N09 --> N10[10 Reasoning RLVR]
    N08 --> N11[11 RLAIF / self-play]
    N09 --> N12[12 RL for VLMs]
    N10 --> N13[13 Frontier & pitfalls]
    classDef here fill:#2563eb,color:#fff,stroke:#1e3a8a
```

## 1. What is reinforcement learning?

**Analogy: teaching a dog a trick.** You cannot show the dog the "correct output" for every situation (that
would be supervised learning). Instead the dog *tries something*, and you give a treat (reward) when the
result is good. Over many tries, the dog figures out which behaviours lead to treats. Three things make this
harder than supervised learning:

| Supervised learning | Reinforcement learning |
|---|---|
| Correct label given for every input | Only a scalar **reward**, often much later ("delayed reward") |
| Data is a fixed dataset | Data is generated by **your own behaviour**: change the policy, change the data |
| Each example independent | Actions change future states (**sequential decisions**) |
| Loss tells you *what* the answer is | Reward tells you *how good* it was, not what would have been better |

The core loop is always the same: an **agent** observes a **state**, picks an **action**, and the
**environment** answers with a **reward** and the next state.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    A[" Agent<br/>policy (a|s)"] -- "action a" --> E[" Environment<br/>P(s'|s,a), R(s,a,s')"]
    E -- "reward r" --> A
    E -- "next state s" --> A
```

For an LLM: the *agent* is the language model, the *action* is the next token, the *state* is the prompt
plus the tokens generated so far, and the *reward* comes from a reward model, a human, or a verifier
(e.g. "is the final answer to this maths problem correct?"). We make this precise in section 8.

## 2. The Markov Decision Process (MDP)

An MDP is the tuple $(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{A}, P, R, \gamma)$:

| Symbol | Name | Gridworld example | LLM example |
|---|---|---|---|
| $\mathcal{S}$ | states | cells of the grid | prompt + tokens so far |
| $\mathcal{A}$ | actions | up / right / down / left | the vocabulary |
| $P(s' \mid s, a)$ | transition probabilities | 80% move as intended, 10% slip sideways each | deterministic: append the token |
| $R(s, a, s')$ | reward | +1 goal, -1 pit, -0.04 per step | reward model score at `<eos>`, 0 elsewhere |
| $\gamma \in [0, 1]$ | discount factor | 0.95 | usually 1.0 (episodes are short and finite) |

**The Markov property** says the next state depends only on the current state and action, not on how we
got there: $P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t, s_{t-1}, \dots) = P(s_{t+1} \mid s_t, a_t)$. For an LLM this holds
trivially because the "state" *is* the whole history of tokens.

A **policy** $\pi(a \mid s)$ is a probability distribution over actions for every state. That is what we
learn. A *deterministic* policy puts all its probability on one action.

Let's build a small, classic gridworld (after Russell & Norvig's 4x3 world) **from scratch in pure Python**.

Let's draw the world. A small helper renders a value function as a heat-map and a policy as arrows; we
reuse it for every figure and animation in this notebook.

## 3. Return: what the agent is trying to maximise

The agent does not maximise the *next* reward, it maximises the **return**: the (discounted) sum of all
future rewards from time $t$:

$$G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma r_{t+2} + \gamma^2 r_{t+3} + \dots = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \gamma^k r_{t+k+1}.$$

The single most useful identity in RL is the **recursive form** of the return. Pull out the first term:

$$G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma \underbrace{\left(r_{t+2} + \gamma r_{t+3} + \dots\right)}_{G_{t+1}} = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}.$$

Why discount?

* **Maths:** with $\gamma < 1$ and bounded rewards $|r| \le r_{\max}$ the infinite sum is bounded by $r_{\max}/(1-\gamma)$.
* **Preference:** a reward now is worth more than the same reward later (uncertainty about the future).
* **Horizon:** $1/(1-\gamma)$ is the "effective horizon": $\gamma=0.9$ looks about 10 steps ahead, $\gamma=0.99$ about 100.

For LLMs, responses are finite episodes (they end at `<eos>`) so $\gamma = 1$ is common: the return of a
response is just its final reward.

Notice in the right panel: the return is *high even at early steps* although those rewards are negative.
The return carries information about the future back to the present. That is credit assignment in one
picture.

## 4. Value functions: "how good is it to be here?"

Returns are random (the policy samples actions, the environment slips). Value functions are their
**expectations**:

* **State value** $V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s]$: expected return when starting in $s$ and following $\pi$.
* **Action value** $Q^\pi(s, a) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s, a_t = a]$: same, but the first action is forced to be $a$.
* **Advantage** $A^\pi(s, a) = Q^\pi(s, a) - V^\pi(s)$: how much *better than average* action $a$ is in $s$.

The advantage is the star of the whole course: policy gradients, PPO, GRPO all push up the probability of
actions with $A > 0$ and push down actions with $A < 0$.

The two value functions are related by two simple identities:

$$V^\pi(s) = \sum_a \pi(a \mid s)\, Q^\pi(s, a) \qquad\text{(average over the policy's action choice)}$$

$$Q^\pi(s, a) = \sum_{s', r} P(s', r \mid s, a)\,\big[r + \gamma V^\pi(s')\big] \qquad\text{(one step of the environment, then follow } \pi\text{)}$$

### 4.1 Deriving the Bellman expectation equation

Start from the definition and use the recursive return $G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
V^\pi(s) &= \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s] = \mathbb{E}_\pi[r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1} \mid s_t = s] \\
&= \sum_a \pi(a \mid s) \sum_{s', r} P(s', r \mid s, a)\Big[r + \gamma\, \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_{t+1} \mid s_{t+1} = s']\Big] \quad \text{(law of total expectation + Markov)} \\
&= \sum_a \pi(a \mid s) \sum_{s', r} P(s', r \mid s, a)\,\big[r + \gamma V^\pi(s')\big].
\end{aligned}
$$

This is the **Bellman expectation equation**: the value of a state equals the expected immediate reward
plus the discounted value of where you land. It is one linear equation per state, so for a known MDP we
could solve it with linear algebra. Instead we use the simpler fixed-point iteration below, because the
same idea ("make $V(s)$ agree with $r + \gamma V(s')$") reappears as TD learning, Q-learning, the critic in
PPO and the value head of an RLHF model.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    s(("s")) -->|"(a|s)"| a1["a"]
    s -->|"(a|s)"| a2["a"]
    a1 -->|"P(s'|s,a), r"| s1(("s'"))
    a1 -->|"P(s'|s,a), r"| s2(("s'"))
    a2 -->|"P(s'|s,a), r"| s3(("s'"))
    s1 -.->|"V(s')"| back["V(s) =     P  (r +  V(s'))"]
    s2 -.->|"V(s')"| back
    s3 -.->|"V(s')"| back
```

This picture is called a **backup diagram**: value information flows *backwards* from successor states to
the current state.

## 5. Algorithm 1: Iterative policy evaluation

**Goal:** given a fixed policy $\pi$ and a known model $P$, compute $V^\pi$.

> **Algorithm steps: policy evaluation**
> 1. **Initialise** $V(s) = 0$ for all states. *(Any start works: the update is a contraction, so it converges from anywhere.)*
> 2. **Sweep** over every non-terminal state $s$ and compute the right-hand side of the Bellman equation
>    $V_{\text{new}}(s) = \sum_a \pi(a|s) \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a)[r + \gamma V(s')]$. *(We use the old $V$ on the right: a "synchronous" sweep.)*
> 3. **Measure the change** $\Delta = \max_s |V_{\text{new}}(s) - V(s)|$ and set $V \leftarrow V_{\text{new}}$.
> 4. **Repeat** 2-3 until $\Delta < \theta$ (a small tolerance). *(Each sweep shrinks the error by at least a factor $\gamma$.)*
> 5. Terminal states keep $V = 0$: there is no future after the episode ends.

Why does it converge? The Bellman operator $T^\pi V = r + \gamma P^\pi V$ is a **$\gamma$-contraction** in the
max-norm: $\|T^\pi V - T^\pi U\|_\infty \le \gamma \|V - U\|_\infty$. Applying it repeatedly shrinks the
distance to the unique fixed point $V^\pi$ geometrically.

The random policy is *bad*: it stumbles into the pit often, so most values are negative. To improve it we
need the *optimal* value function.

## 6. Optimality: the Bellman optimality equation and value iteration

The **optimal value** $V^*(s) = \max_\pi V^\pi(s)$ is the best expected return achievable from $s$. An
optimal agent picks the best action instead of averaging over $\pi$, which turns the sum over actions into
a max:

$$V^*(s) = \max_a \sum_{s', r} P(s', r \mid s, a)\,\big[r + \gamma V^*(s')\big], \qquad Q^*(s, a) = \sum_{s', r} P(s', r \mid s,a)\big[r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q^*(s', a')\big].$$

Once we know $V^*$ (or $Q^*$), acting optimally is easy: be **greedy**,
$\pi^*(s) = \arg\max_a Q^*(s,a)$. Value iteration simply turns the optimality equation into an update.

> **Algorithm steps: value iteration** (Bellman 1957)
> 1. **Initialise** $V(s) = 0$.
> 2. **Bellman optimality backup** for every non-terminal $s$: compute $Q(s,a) = \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a)[r + \gamma V(s')]$ for each action and set $V_{\text{new}}(s) = \max_a Q(s,a)$.
> 3. **Check convergence** with $\Delta = \max_s |V_{\text{new}}(s) - V(s)|$. Stop when $\Delta < \theta$.
> 4. **Extract the policy**: $\pi(s) = \arg\max_a Q(s,a)$ using the final $V$.
>
> *Intuition:* after $k$ sweeps, $V$ is the optimal value of a game that lasts $k$ steps. The goal's +1
> "spreads" one cell per sweep across the grid: watch it in the animation below.

The animation below shows value iteration *thinking*: each frame is one sweep. Colour is $V(s)$, arrows are
the greedy action under the current $V$, and the right panel is the size of the update $\Delta$ on a log
scale (it drops by roughly a factor $\gamma$ per sweep, as the contraction argument promised).

Things to notice in the animation:

* In sweep 1 only the cells **next to the goal and pit** change: information travels one step per sweep.
* The arrows near the pit point *away from it* even when the shortest route passes next to it, because a
  slip (20%) could drop the agent in. The optimal policy is **risk-aware** because the model is stochastic.
* Long after the arrows stop changing, the values are still being refined. The *policy* converges before the
  *values* do: that observation is the motivation for policy iteration.

## 7. Algorithm 3: Policy iteration

Policy iteration alternates two steps that each do something we already know how to do.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    P0[" (any policy,<br/>e.g. random)"] --> E["<b>Evaluate</b><br/>compute V^<br/>(policy evaluation)"]
    E --> I["<b>Improve</b><br/>'(s) = argmax Q^(s,a)<br/>(act greedily)"]
    I -->|"policy changed"| E
    I -->|"policy stable"| Done["* and V*"]
```

> **Algorithm steps: policy iteration** (Howard 1960)
> 1. **Start** with any policy $\pi_0$ (we use "always go up").
> 2. **Policy evaluation:** compute $V^{\pi_k}$ exactly (section 5).
> 3. **Policy improvement:** for every state set $\pi_{k+1}(s) = \arg\max_a Q^{\pi_k}(s, a)$.
> 4. **Stop** when $\pi_{k+1} = \pi_k$; that policy is optimal. Otherwise go to step 2.
>
> **Why it works (policy improvement theorem):** if $Q^{\pi}(s, \pi'(s)) \ge V^\pi(s)$ for all $s$, then
> $V^{\pi'}(s) \ge V^\pi(s)$ for all $s$. Acting greedily w.r.t. $Q^\pi$ satisfies the condition by
> construction, so every iteration is at least as good as the last; with finitely many deterministic
> policies it must terminate, and when nothing changes the Bellman *optimality* equation holds.

This "evaluate, then improve" pattern is called **generalised policy iteration** and it is the skeleton of
almost every RL algorithm: actor-critic methods (and PPO-RLHF) do a little bit of evaluation (train the
critic) and a little bit of improvement (a policy-gradient step) at the same time.

Policy iteration needs only a handful of rounds (each round is expensive: a full evaluation), while value
iteration needs many cheap sweeps. Both reach the same $V^*$.

## 8. From planning to learning: Monte Carlo check of $V^*$

Everything above used the **model** $P(s' \mid s, a)$: that is *planning* (dynamic programming). Real RL
agents (and LLMs) usually do not have the model; they only get **samples** by acting. The simplest
model-free estimate of a value is to *average sampled returns* (Monte Carlo):

$$V^\pi(s) \approx \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N G^{(i)}, \qquad G^{(i)} \text{ = return of the } i\text{-th episode started in } s.$$

Let's verify our $V^*$ by actually running the optimal policy 2000 times with the `step` function that a
model-free agent would use. The animation compares a random agent and the optimal agent walking in the
world (same random seed for the slips).

## 9. The big picture: an LLM is a policy in a token MDP

Now the key step for the rest of the course. Generating a response with a language model **is** acting in an
MDP:

| MDP concept | LLM text generation |
|---|---|
| state $s_t$ | prompt $x$ + tokens generated so far $y_{<t}$ |
| action $a_t$ | next token $y_t \in$ vocabulary ( 32k-256k actions!) |
| policy $\pi_\theta(a_t \mid s_t)$ | the LM's softmax over the next token |
| transition $P$ | **deterministic**: $s_{t+1} = s_t \oplus y_t$ (append the token) |
| episode end | the model emits `<eos>` (or hits a max length) |
| reward | usually **only at the end**: a reward model / human preference / verifier ("is the answer correct?") |
| $\gamma$ | usually 1 |

For a **VLM** the only change is the initial state: $s_0$ = image tokens + text prompt. Everything else, the
actions (text tokens), the policy, the rewards, stays the same, which is why the same RL algorithms train
both. (We will see this concretely in notebook 12.)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph s0["state s"]
      P["prompt: 2+2="]
    end
    subgraph s1["state s"]
      P1["2+2= 4"]
    end
    subgraph s2["state s (terminal)"]
      P2["2+2= 4 &lt;eos&gt;"]
    end
    s0 -- "action: '4'<br/>(4|s)=0.4" --> s1
    s1 -- "action: '&lt;eos&gt;'<br/>(eos|s)=0.7" --> s2
    s2 -- "verifier" --> R["reward = 1<br/>(answer correct)"]
```

Because the transitions are deterministic and the reward comes at the end, the value functions simplify:

* $V^\pi(s_t)$ = the probability-weighted average final reward of all completions of the prefix $s_t$;
* $Q^\pi(s_t, y_t) = V^\pi(s_t \oplus y_t)$ (choosing a token just *is* moving to the next prefix);
* the advantage $A^\pi(s_t, y_t) = V^\pi(s_t \oplus y_t) - V^\pi(s_t)$ measures how much a single token
  changed the expected final reward. That is *credit assignment at the token level*.

Let's make it completely concrete with a tiny "language model" (a hand-written table of next-token
probabilities, standing in for a pretrained LM) answering `2+2=`. Because the tree of all responses is
small we can compute every value **exactly** with the Bellman equation, the same recursion as before.

What the tree teaches:

* The **reward only exists at the leaves**, but the Bellman recursion gives every intermediate prefix a value.
* The token `4` has a positive advantage ($+0.42$): emitting it raises the expected reward from 0.28 to 0.70.
  The tokens `3` and `5` have advantage $-0.28$: they make success impossible.
* After `4`, the token `<eos>` is what actually locks in the reward; continuing (`4` `4`  "44") destroys it.
* An RL algorithm for LLMs should therefore increase $\pi(\texttt{4} \mid \texttt{2+2=})$ and
  $\pi(\texttt{<eos>} \mid \texttt{2+2=4})$. Notebook 04 does exactly that with gradients.

The optimal policy here is trivial to read off (greedy w.r.t. $Q^*$), but real vocabularies have ~$10^5$
tokens and responses have hundreds of tokens, so the tree has more leaves than atoms in the universe. We
cannot enumerate it; we must **sample** responses and **learn** from their rewards. That is why LLM RL
uses sampling-based, model-free methods (policy gradients, PPO, GRPO) rather than dynamic programming.

## 10. A map of RL algorithms (and where LLM methods live)

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    RL["RL algorithms"] --> MB["Model-based / planning<br/>(know P): DP, value & policy iteration<br/><i>this notebook</i>"]
    RL --> MF["Model-free<br/>(only samples)"]
    MF --> VB["Value-based<br/>learn Q, act greedily<br/>MC, TD, SARSA, Q-learning, DQN<br/><i>02, 03</i>"]
    MF --> PB["Policy-based<br/>learn  directly<br/>REINFORCE<br/><i>04</i>"]
    VB --> AC["Actor-critic = both<br/>A2C, GAE, TRPO, PPO<br/><i>05, 06</i>"]
    PB --> AC
    AC --> LLM1["PPO-RLHF<br/><i>07</i>"]
    PB --> LLM2["Critic-free LLM RL<br/>RLOO, GRPO, DAPO<br/><i>09, 10</i>"]
    LLM1 --> DPO["Direct preference opt.<br/>(no sampling loop)<br/>DPO, KTO, SimPO<br/><i>08</i>"]
```

Why do LLM methods sit on the **policy-based** side? Because the action space (the vocabulary) is huge, the
policy is already a great neural network (the pretrained LM) and we want to *fine-tune* it, and the
episodes are short with a terminal reward, so we can afford to sample whole responses.

## 11. Common pitfalls

* **Confusing reward and return (or value).** Reward is one step's signal; return is the sum of future rewards; value is the *expected* return.
* **Forgetting to zero the value of terminal states.** Bootstrapping from $V(\text{terminal}) \ne 0$ leaks fake future reward into the episode end (the `* (not done)` factor in our code).
* **$\gamma = 1$ with infinite episodes** makes returns unbounded; that's fine for LLMs only because responses are capped at a max length.
* **Reward design changes behaviour.** Try `step_reward=-0.5` in the gridworld: the agent will happily jump into the pit to end the pain quickly. The LLM version of this is *reward hacking* (notebook 13).
* **Thinking the optimal policy is "shortest path".** With stochastic dynamics the optimal policy trades speed for safety (look at the arrows next to the pit).
* **Expecting DP to scale.** It needs the model and a sweep over every state; token MDPs have astronomically many states.

## 12. How this connects to LLM/VLM training

* The **reward model** in RLHF (notebook 07) defines $R$ at the terminal state; the **KL penalty** to the reference model adds a small per-token reward.
* The **value head** of PPO-RLHF learns $V^\pi(\text{prefix})$, exactly the colours in the token tree.
* **GRPO** (notebook 09) avoids learning $V$ by estimating it from a *group* of sampled responses per prompt (a Monte Carlo estimate, like section 8).
* **Token-level advantages** decide which tokens get reinforced; the token tree shows why a terminal reward still gives every token a meaningful advantage.
* For **VLMs**, the image tokens are part of the initial state $s_0$; the MDP over text tokens is unchanged.

## 13. Cheat sheet

| Concept | Formula | Code in this notebook |
|---|---|---|
| Return | $G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}$ | `discounted_return` |
| State value | $V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s]$ | `policy_evaluation` |
| Action value | $Q^\pi(s,a) = \sum P\,[r + \gamma V^\pi(s')]$ | `q_from_v` |
| Advantage | $A^\pi = Q^\pi - V^\pi$ | token tree section |
| Bellman expectation | $V^\pi = \sum_a \pi \sum P [r + \gamma V^\pi]$ | `policy_evaluation` |
| Bellman optimality | $V^* = \max_a \sum P [r + \gamma V^*]$ | `value_iteration` |
| Policy iteration | evaluate  greedy improve  repeat | `policy_iteration` |
| Monte Carlo value | average of sampled returns | section 8 |
| LLM as MDP | state = prefix, action = token, reward at `<eos>` | `toy_lm`, `value` |

**Next:** notebook 01 strips the MDP down to a single state, the *multi-armed bandit*, to study the other
core difficulty of RL: **exploration vs exploitation**.

# 01  Multi-Armed Bandits: the exploration vs exploitation trade-off

Notebook 00 assumed we **know** the environment and can plan. Real agents must *learn from trial and
error*, and that raises a question supervised learning never asks: **should I take the action that looks
best so far, or try something else to learn more?**

To study that question in isolation we remove everything else from RL: no states, no delayed rewards,
just one decision repeated many times. This is the **multi-armed bandit**. It is not a toy for LLMs: a
single prompt answered by one sampled response and scored by a verifier *is* a (contextual) bandit, and
most LLM RL algorithms treat the whole response as one "arm pull".

By the end you will have implemented, from scratch in pure Python (and one in PyTorch):

1. **greedy** and **-greedy** with incremental mean updates, plus **optimistic initialisation**;
2. **UCB1** (optimism under uncertainty, derived from Hoeffding's inequality);
3. **Thompson sampling** (Bayesian posterior sampling with Beta distributions);
4. the **gradient bandit** (softmax policy + baseline), the direct ancestor of REINFORCE, PPO and GRPO;
5. a **contextual bandit** trained with policy gradients in PyTorch: a miniature of "RLHF as a bandit".

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    N00[00 Foundations] --> N01["<b>01 Bandits</b><br/>exploration<br/>(you are here)"]:::here
    N01 --> N02[02 Tabular TD / Q-learning]
    N02 --> N03[03 DQN]
    N02 --> N04[04 Policy gradient]
    N04 --> N05[05 Actor-critic, GAE]
    N05 --> N06[06 TRPO / PPO]
    N06 --> N07[07 RLHF]
    N07 --> N08[08 DPO family]
    N07 --> N09[09 GRPO family]
    N09 --> N10[10 Reasoning RLVR]
    N08 --> N11[11 RLAIF / self-play]
    N09 --> N12[12 RL for VLMs]
    N10 --> N13[13 Frontier & pitfalls]
    classDef here fill:#2563eb,color:#fff,stroke:#1e3a8a
```

## 1. The problem

**Analogy: a new city, ten restaurants.** Every evening you pick one restaurant. Each has an unknown
probability $p_a$ of giving you a great meal. If you always return to the best one you've found so far,
you might never discover that the place around the corner is better. If you keep trying new places, you
waste evenings on bad meals. Bandit algorithms are principled answers to "how much should I explore?".

Formally a $K$-armed bandit has actions $a \in \{1,\dots,K\}$, each with an unknown reward distribution with
mean $q_*(a) = \mathbb{E}[r \mid a]$. At each step $t = 1..T$ the agent picks $a_t$ and receives
$r_t \sim$ distribution of arm $a_t$. We use **Bernoulli** arms ($r \in \{0, 1\}$, like "answer correct /
incorrect" for an LLM), so $q_*(a) = p_a$.

**How do we score an algorithm?** By **regret**: how much reward we lost compared to always pulling the
best arm $a^* = \arg\max_a q_*(a)$:

$$\text{Regret}(T) = \sum_{t=1}^{T} \big(q_*(a^*) - q_*(a_t)\big).$$

* Always exploring at random gives **linear** regret: we lose a constant amount every step.
* Never exploring (greedy) can also give linear regret: if we lock onto a bad arm we lose forever.
* Lai & Robbins (1985) proved that any good algorithm must have regret growing at least like
  $\log T$, and UCB and Thompson sampling achieve this logarithmic rate.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    S["estimate of each arm<br/>(mean, uncertainty)"] --> D{"decide"}
    D -- "exploit:<br/>best estimate" --> R["pull arm,<br/>observe reward"]
    D -- "explore:<br/>uncertain arm" --> R
    R --> U["update estimate<br/>of that arm"]
    U --> S
```

## 2. Estimating an arm's value: the incremental mean

Every algorithm below keeps an estimate $Q(a)$ of $q_*(a)$. The natural estimate is the sample average of
the rewards seen from that arm. Storing all rewards is wasteful; the average can be updated in $O(1)$:

$$Q_{n+1} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n} r_i = \frac{1}{n}\Big(r_n + (n-1) Q_n\Big) = Q_n + \frac{1}{n}\big(r_n - Q_n\big).$$

Read the last form as **new estimate = old estimate + step size  (target  old estimate)**. This pattern
(an *error-correcting update*) appears everywhere in RL: TD learning, Q-learning, value-function regression in
PPO. With a constant step size $\alpha$ instead of $1/n$ we get an exponential moving average that forgets old
data, which is useful when the arm's distribution changes over time (non-stationary, like a policy that is
itself being trained).

## 3. Algorithm 1: greedy and -greedy

**Idea:** usually exploit the best estimate, but with a small probability $\varepsilon$ pick an arm uniformly at
random so every arm keeps being sampled.

> **Algorithm steps: -greedy**
> 1. **Initialise** $Q(a) = Q_0$ and counts $N(a) = 0$ for every arm.
> 2. **Choose:** with probability $\varepsilon$ pick a random arm (*explore*); otherwise pick $\arg\max_a Q(a)$, breaking ties randomly (*exploit*).
> 3. **Act:** pull the arm, observe $r$.
> 4. **Update** only the pulled arm: $N(a) \mathrel{+}= 1$, $Q(a) \mathrel{+}= \alpha_n (r - Q(a))$ with $\alpha_n = 1/N(a)$ (sample mean) or a constant $\alpha$.
> 5. Repeat from step 2.
>
> * $\varepsilon = 0$ is **pure greedy**: it can lock onto a bad arm forever after one unlucky reward from the good arm.
> * **Optimistic initialisation** ($Q_0 = 1$, larger than any real reward, constant $\alpha$) is a trick that makes even greedy explore: every untried arm looks great until it is tried and "disappoints".
> * Weakness: exploration is *undirected*: -greedy wastes pulls on arms it already knows are bad, forever, so its regret grows linearly with slope $\approx \varepsilon \cdot$ (average gap).

## 4. Algorithm 2: UCB1, optimism in the face of uncertainty

**Idea:** instead of exploring at random, explore the arms we are *unsure* about. Give each arm a bonus that
is large when it has been tried rarely, then act greedily on *estimate + bonus*: "assume each arm is as good
as it plausibly could be".

**Deriving the bonus.** For rewards in $[0, 1]$, **Hoeffding's inequality** bounds how far a sample mean of
$n$ pulls can be from the truth:

$$P\big(q_*(a) > Q(a) + u\big) \le e^{-2 n u^2}.$$

Choose the failure probability to shrink over time, $e^{-2nu^2} = t^{-4}$, and solve for $u$:

$$u = \sqrt{\frac{2 \ln t}{n}} \quad\Longrightarrow\quad a_t = \arg\max_a \left[Q(a) + c\sqrt{\frac{\ln t}{N(a)}}\right], \; c = \sqrt{2}.$$

So $Q(a) + \sqrt{2 \ln t / N(a)}$ is an **upper confidence bound** that is wrong with tiny probability. This is
UCB1 (Auer, Cesa-Bianchi & Fischer, 2002), with regret $O(\sum_{a: \Delta_a > 0} \frac{\ln T}{\Delta_a})$, where $\Delta_a = q_*(a^*) - q_*(a)$.

> **Algorithm steps: UCB1**
> 1. **Pull every arm once** (the bonus is infinite for $N(a) = 0$).
> 2. **Score** each arm with $U(a) = Q(a) + c\sqrt{\ln t / N(a)}$.
>    *The $\ln t$ term grows slowly, so a neglected arm's bonus keeps rising until it is tried again; the $1/N(a)$ term shrinks the bonus of well-known arms.*
> 3. **Pull** $\arg\max_a U(a)$, observe $r$.
> 4. **Update** $N(a)$ and the sample mean $Q(a)$.
> 5. Repeat. Bad arms get pulled only $O(\ln T / \Delta_a^2)$ times: exploration *fades* automatically.

The animation shows UCB1 on the 4-arm bandit from above. Bars are the estimates $Q(a)$, the whiskers reach up
to the upper confidence bound $U(a)$, the dashed markers are the true $p_a$ (hidden from the agent), and the
highlighted arm is the one pulled at that step. Watch the whiskers of rarely pulled arms *grow* (the $\ln t$
term) until they become the highest and get pulled, while the bars converge to the true values.

## 5. Algorithm 3: Thompson sampling, probability matching with a posterior

**Idea (Thompson, 1933):** keep a full *belief distribution* over each arm's success probability. Each step,
**sample** one plausible value from every arm's belief and pull the arm whose sample is highest. An arm is
pulled with exactly the probability that it is the best arm under your current beliefs ("probability
matching"). Uncertain arms produce wide, occasionally high samples, so they get explored; well-known bad
arms almost never produce a winning sample.

**The Bayesian bookkeeping.** For a Bernoulli arm the conjugate prior is the **Beta** distribution:

$$p(\theta) = \mathrm{Beta}(\theta; \alpha, \beta) \propto \theta^{\alpha-1}(1-\theta)^{\beta-1}.$$

After observing reward $r \in \{0,1\}$ the posterior is again a Beta (multiply prior by likelihood $\theta^r (1-\theta)^{1-r}$):

$$\mathrm{Beta}(\alpha, \beta) \times \theta^r(1-\theta)^{1-r} \propto \theta^{\alpha + r - 1}(1-\theta)^{\beta + (1-r) - 1} \;\Rightarrow\; \alpha \leftarrow \alpha + r,\;\; \beta \leftarrow \beta + 1 - r.$$

So the whole posterior is two counters per arm: $\alpha - 1$ = successes, $\beta - 1$ = failures. Its mean is
$\alpha/(\alpha+\beta)$ and its width shrinks like $1/\sqrt{\alpha + \beta}$.

> **Algorithm steps: Thompson sampling (Beta-Bernoulli)**
> 1. **Prior:** $\alpha_a = \beta_a = 1$ for every arm (Beta(1,1) = uniform on [0,1]: "no idea").
> 2. **Sample** $\tilde\theta_a \sim \mathrm{Beta}(\alpha_a, \beta_a)$ independently for each arm.
> 3. **Act greedily on the samples:** pull $a_t = \arg\max_a \tilde\theta_a$.
> 4. **Bayesian update** of the pulled arm only: $\alpha_a \mathrel{+}= r$, $\beta_a \mathrel{+}= 1 - r$.
> 5. Repeat. There is no $\varepsilon$ or $c$ to tune: the posterior width *is* the exploration schedule.

In the GIF the posteriors start flat (Beta(1,1)), the best arm's curve becomes a tall, narrow spike near its
true value (it gets most pulls), while the clearly bad arm (red) stays wide: Thompson sampling stops pulling
it as soon as it is *confidently worse*, without needing to know its value precisely. That is efficient
exploration.

## 6. Algorithm 4: the gradient bandit, the first policy-gradient method

All methods so far learn **values** and derive actions from them. The gradient bandit learns a **policy**
directly: a softmax over learnable preferences $H(a)$,

$$\pi(a) = \frac{e^{H(a)}}{\sum_b e^{H(b)}},$$

and does **gradient ascent on the expected reward** $J(H) = \mathbb{E}_{a \sim \pi}[r] = \sum_a \pi(a)\, q_*(a)$.
This section is the most important one for the rest of the course, because REINFORCE, PPO and GRPO are this
exact idea with the softmax over arms replaced by an LLM's softmax over tokens.

**Derivation.** We cannot compute $\nabla J$ directly because $q_*$ is unknown, but we can turn it into an
expectation we can *sample*:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\nabla_H J &= \sum_a q_*(a)\, \nabla_H \pi(a)
            = \sum_a q_*(a)\, \pi(a)\, \nabla_H \log \pi(a) \qquad \text{(log-derivative trick: } \nabla\pi = \pi\nabla\log\pi) \\
           &= \mathbb{E}_{a \sim \pi}\big[\, q_*(a)\, \nabla_H \log \pi(a) \,\big]
            = \mathbb{E}_{a \sim \pi,\, r}\big[\, r\, \nabla_H \log \pi(a) \,\big].
\end{aligned}
$$

So "sample an action, observe its reward $r$, step along $r \nabla \log \pi(a)$" is an **unbiased** estimate of the
gradient. For a softmax, $\frac{\partial \log \pi(A)}{\partial H(b)} = \mathbb{1}[b = A] - \pi(b)$.

**The baseline.** Since $\sum_a \nabla \pi(a) = \nabla 1 = 0$, we can subtract any constant $\bar r$ from $r$
without changing the expected gradient: $\mathbb{E}[(r - \bar r)\nabla\log\pi(a)] = \nabla J$. But it changes
the **variance** a lot: with 0/1 rewards and no baseline every successful pull pushes its arm up and failures
do nothing; with a baseline equal to the average reward, better-than-average outcomes push up and
worse-than-average outcomes push *down*. $r - \bar r$ is our first **advantage**. (GRPO's "group mean" baseline in
notebook 09 is exactly this, computed over a group of sampled responses.)

> **Algorithm steps: gradient bandit** (Sutton & Barto 2.8; Williams 1992)
> 1. **Initialise** preferences $H(a) = 0$ (uniform policy) and the baseline $\bar r = 0$.
> 2. **Sample** an action from the policy: $A_t \sim \pi = \mathrm{softmax}(H)$. *(Exploration comes from the policy's own randomness.)*
> 3. **Observe** reward $r_t$ and compute the advantage $r_t - \bar r_t$.
> 4. **Policy-gradient step** for every arm $b$: $H(b) \mathrel{+}= \alpha\,(r_t - \bar r_t)\,(\mathbb{1}[b = A_t] - \pi(b))$.
>    *The chosen arm moves in the direction of the advantage; all other arms move the opposite way in proportion to their probability.*
> 5. **Update the baseline**: $\bar r \mathrel{+}= (r_t - \bar r)/t$ (running mean of rewards).
> 6. Repeat. As the policy concentrates on the best arm, exploration decreases naturally.

**The same update with PyTorch autograd.** Writing the loss as $-\,(r - \bar r)\log\pi_\theta(A)$ and calling
`.backward()` gives exactly the hand-derived update. This "surrogate loss" trick is how every policy-gradient
method for LLMs is implemented: you never write $\mathbb{1}[b=A] - \pi(b)$ by hand; autograd does it through
the whole transformer.

The animation compares the gradient bandit **with** and **without** a baseline. We use a bandit where *every*
arm pays out often ($p = 0.80, 0.85, 0.88, 0.92$), which is the typical LLM situation: most sampled answers get a
decent reward-model score, and the differences between them are small. Bars are $\pi(a)$ **averaged over 60
independent runs** (single runs are too noisy to compare); the green bar is the best arm.

Without the baseline almost every pull returns $r = 1$, so *whatever arm was sampled gets pushed up*: the
update mostly reinforces the policy's own random choices ("rich get richer") and only weakly distinguishes
good from slightly-worse arms. With the baseline, $r - \bar r$ is negative whenever an arm fails, so worse arms
are actively pushed down. (On the earlier bandit with $p$ from 0.3 to 0.7 the two variants perform about the
same: the baseline matters most when rewards are large relative to their differences.)

## 7. The showdown: regret on a 10-armed testbed

Now a fair comparison: 200 independent bandit problems, each with 10 Bernoulli arms whose probabilities are
drawn uniformly from $[0, 1]$, run for 2000 steps. We average the per-step regret and the fraction of times
each algorithm pulls the optimal arm (the experimental protocol of Sutton & Barto's 10-armed testbed, with
Bernoulli rewards).

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Q["How does the algorithm explore?"] --> U["Undirected<br/>(random noise)"]
    Q --> D["Directed by uncertainty"]
    Q --> P["Through a stochastic policy"]
    U --> EG["-greedy<br/>regret ~ linear"]
    U --> OI["optimistic init<br/>(explores early only)"]
    D --> UCB["UCB1: optimism<br/>regret ~ log T"]
    D --> TS["Thompson: posterior sampling<br/>regret ~ log T"]
    P --> GB["gradient bandit<br/>( REINFORCE, PPO, GRPO)"]
```

How to read the plot:

* **random** has a straight, steep regret line: linear regret, it never uses what it learned.
* **greedy** learns quickly but its regret keeps growing: in some runs it locks onto a suboptimal arm forever.
* **-greedy** fixes the lock-in but its regret grows linearly with slope    (average gap), because it never stops exploring.
* **Thompson** is the clear winner here, and its regret curve bends over (logarithmic growth).
* **UCB1 with the textbook $c=\sqrt{2}$ looks worse than -greedy at 2000 steps!** The Hoeffding bonus is a
  *worst-case* bound, so it is very conservative: with 10 arms it keeps re-checking bad arms for a long time.
  Its advantage is *asymptotic*: the bonus keeps shrinking, -greedy's exploration does not. A smaller,
  tuned bonus (**UCB c=0.5**) is far better at this horizon, at the price of losing the formal guarantee.
* The **gradient bandit** learns a policy directly; its performance depends on the step size  (try 0.05 or 0.5).

Look at the *shapes* of the curves rather than the exact numbers: they depend on the seed and the number of runs.
The next cell checks the "asymptotic" claim by running UCB1 and -greedy ten times longer.

## 8. A contextual bandit in PyTorch: "RLHF is a bandit"

In a **contextual bandit** the agent first observes a context $x$ (a prompt!) and then picks an action
(a response!). The best action depends on the context. There is still no sequence of states: one decision,
one reward. That is exactly how sequence-level LLM RL is framed: the *prompt* is the context, the *whole
response* is the action, and the reward model or verifier gives one scalar.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    X["context x<br/>(prompt)"] --> PI["policy _(y|x)<br/>(softmax / LLM)"]
    PI -- "sample y" --> RW["reward r(x, y)<br/>(verifier / reward model)"]
    RW -- "advantage r  b(x)" --> UP["policy-gradient step<br/> log _(y|x)  (r  b)"]
    UP --> PI
```

Below: 4 "prompt types" and 6 "candidate responses". Each (prompt, response) pair has a hidden success
probability. A linear softmax policy is trained with the batched gradient-bandit update (REINFORCE with a
per-prompt mean baseline), written with autograd. Replace the linear layer with a transformer and the 6
candidate responses with all possible token sequences and you have the core of LLM policy-gradient RL.

## 9. Exploration in LLMs: temperature, best-of-N and pass@k

LLMs explore by **sampling**: the temperature $\tau$ in $\pi(y) \propto \exp(\text{logit}(y)/\tau)$ plays the role of
$\varepsilon$. Low temperature  greedy (exploit), high temperature  more exploration. A second, very common
"bandit" trick for LLMs is **best-of-N**: sample $N$ responses and keep the best according to a verifier or
reward model. If one sample is correct with probability $p$, the chance that at least one of $N$ is correct is

$$\text{pass@}N = 1 - (1 - p)^N.$$

This is why RL on LLMs needs a policy that already succeeds *sometimes*: if $p \approx 0$, no amount of sampling
finds a reward signal (the exploration problem), and if $p \approx 1$ every sample gets the same reward and the
advantage $r - \bar r$ is zero (nothing to learn). Notebooks 09-10 return to this "zero-variance group" problem.

## 10. Common pitfalls

* **Deterministic tie-breaking.** `max(range(k), key=Q.__getitem__)` always picks the lowest index among ties: with all-zero initial estimates greedy then *never* tries most arms. Break ties randomly.
* **Using a constant step size with stationary arms** keeps the estimate noisy forever; using $1/n$ with *non-stationary* arms (e.g. a reward model that drifts) reacts too slowly.
* **Optimistic initialisation only helps at the start**: once the optimism is "used up" it explores no more, so it fails if the problem changes.
* **Forgetting the baseline in policy gradients**: still unbiased, but when all rewards are high (every answer gets a decent score) the update mostly reinforces whatever was sampled and learning is much slower (see the gradient-bandit GIF).
* **Comparing algorithms on one seed**: bandit curves are very noisy; always average over many runs.
* **Mis-scaled UCB bonus**: $\sqrt{2\ln t / N}$ assumes rewards in $[0, 1]$; with rewards in $[0, 100]$ you must scale $c$ accordingly. And a guarantee is not the same as good finite-horizon performance: the textbook constant over-explores (section 7).

## 11. How this connects to LLM/VLM training

* **Sequence-level LLM RL is a contextual bandit**: prompt = context, response = action, reward model / verifier = reward. RLOO, GRPO and DPO (notebooks 08-09) all treat the response as one action.
* The **gradient bandit with a baseline** is the core update of REINFORCE (04), PPO (06), RLOO and GRPO (09); GRPO's baseline is the mean reward of a *group* of responses to the same prompt, like our per-prompt baseline in section 8.
* **Temperature** is the LLM's exploration knob; RL can *collapse* it (entropy collapse, notebook 13), turning the policy greedy and stopping exploration.
* **Best-of-N** is the inference-time cousin of RL and is the basis of rejection-sampling fine-tuning (notebook 10).
* **Thompson-style uncertainty** shows up in active preference collection and ensembles of reward models for exploration.
* For **VLMs** nothing changes: the context now includes the image.

## 12. Cheat sheet

| Algorithm | Action selection | Update | Exploration | Regret |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| greedy | $\arg\max_a Q(a)$ | $Q \mathrel{+}= \frac{1}{N}(r - Q)$ | none (can lock in) | linear (worst case) |
| -greedy | random w.p. , else greedy | same | undirected, never stops | linear (slope  ) |
| optimistic init | greedy, $Q_0$ high | constant  | early only | depends |
| UCB1 | $\arg\max_a Q(a) + \sqrt{2\ln t / N(a)}$ | sample mean | directed by uncertainty | $O(\log T)$ (conservative early) |
| Thompson | $\arg\max_a \tilde\theta_a,\ \tilde\theta_a \sim \text{Beta}(\alpha_a,\beta_a)$ | $\alpha \mathrel{+}= r,\ \beta \mathrel{+}= 1-r$ | posterior sampling | $O(\log T)$ |
| gradient bandit | $a \sim \text{softmax}(H)$ | $H \mathrel{+}= \alpha(r - \bar r)(\mathbb{1}[b=a] - \pi_b)$ | stochastic policy | depends on  |
| contextual PG | $y \sim \pi_\theta(\cdot \mid x)$ | $\nabla_\theta\, (r - b(x)) \log\pi_\theta(y \mid x)$ | sampling / temperature | n/a |

**Next:** notebook 02 brings back the *states* and learns values from experience without a model: Monte
Carlo, TD learning, SARSA and Q-learning.

# Online ("self") reinforcement learning for LLMs, step by step 
### The model writes its own training data: sample  score  compare within the group  update (GRPO), for autoregressive *and* diffusion LLMs

Supervised fine-tuning copies answers someone else wrote. **Online RL** lets the model *practise*.
For each prompt it samples several answers **from its current self**. A reward says which were good,
and the model shifts probability toward those. Then it samples again from the improved model, and
repeats. The data are generated on-policy, *by the learner itself*, as training goes on.

"Self" covers two increasingly autonomous ideas, and this notebook builds and tests both:

| | who writes the training data | who judges it | example methods |
|---|---|---|---|
| SFT | humans / a teacher model | (labels are the target) | instruction tuning |
| offline preference learning | a fixed dataset of pairs | humans / a reward model, once | DPO |
| **online RL, verifiable reward** | **the policy itself, every step** | a checker (unit tests, exact answer) | PPO, **GRPO** (DeepSeek-R1), RLVR |
| **online RL, self-reward** | **the policy itself** | **the policy itself** (majority vote, its own judgement) | **TTRL**, self-rewarding LMs |

Everything below runs **for real on a CPU in about three minutes**. The models are this repo's
autoregressive and masked-diffusion LMs (`hale_llm.models`), trained by the RL package
`hale_llm.rl`, and every animation is rendered from the recorded run.

|  | Question |
|---|---|
| 1 | What does the online loop look like? |
| 2 | Setup, config (YAML with inheritance) and the registries |
| 3 | The task and the warm-start policy: what does it know before RL? |
| 4 | The maths: policy gradient  REINFORCE  group baseline (GRPO)  clipping  KL |
| 5 | Run online GRPO with a verifiable reward, and watch the policy change |
| 6 | Why exploration decides everything (temperature, over-confident SFT) |
| 7 | Self-reward without labels (majority vote): what it can and cannot do |
| 8 | Diffusion LLMs: no exact log-likelihood, so how do you do policy gradients? (d1) |
| 9 | Pitfalls checklist, summary, references |

## 1. The online loop

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  P["prompt batch<br/>(from the pool)"]:::io --> S["<b>sample</b> G answers per prompt<br/>from the CURRENT policy _<br/>(temperature > 1)"]:::pol
  S --> R["<b>score</b><br/>verifier r  {0,1}<br/>or self-reward (majority)"]:::rew
  R --> A["<b>normalise in the group</b><br/>A = (r  mean) / std"]:::adv
  A --> U["<b>update</b> <br/>clipped policy gradient<br/>+ KL(_  _ref)"]:::upd
  U -- "next step samples from the<br/>improved policy (on-policy)" --> S
  REF["_ref = warm-start copy<br/>(frozen)"]:::io -.-> U
  classDef io fill:#1e293b,stroke:#64748b,color:#fff
  classDef pol fill:#0c3b4a,stroke:#22d3ee,color:#fff
  classDef rew fill:#3b2f0b,stroke:#fbbf24,color:#fff
  classDef adv fill:#2e1f57,stroke:#a78bfa,color:#fff
  classDef upd fill:#0f3d2c,stroke:#34d399,color:#fff
```

The difference from supervised learning is the loop: the **data distribution moves with the model**.
Whatever the model cannot sample, it cannot learn from. Section 6 shows how decisive that is.

## 2. Setup

The cell below makes `src/` importable (and a sibling `HaleBlocks/src`, where the registries live),
quietens logging, and creates the output folder for the animations.

### The configuration: YAML with `inherits:`

Every experiment in `configs/rl/` inherits `base.yaml` and overrides only what differs, so the AR,
self-reward and diffusion runs below differ by **one or two lines each**. `OnlineRLConfig` resolves
chains of any depth (and rejects cycles), then validates every field with pydantic.

## 3. The task and the warm-start policy

The environment is deliberately tiny, so every number can be inspected:

* **prompt** `"a+b="` with `a, b  09` (100 facts), **answer** two digits (`"8+4="  "12"`);
* the **verifier** checks the answer exactly; that is the *verifiable reward* of RLVR;
* the **warm start** is a short supervised fine-tune on **40 %** of the facts, which teaches the
  format and some arithmetic. The other 60 facts were never shown with an answer: RL has to find them.

This mirrors the real recipe (DeepSeek-R1, Tlu 3, ): SFT gives a policy that *can* produce good
answers sometimes, and RL turns "sometimes" into "reliably".

## 4. The maths, one idea at a time

**Objective.** Maximise the expected reward of the policy's own samples:
$$J(\theta) = \mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{D}}\;\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid x)}\big[r(x,y)\big].$$

**Policy gradient (log-derivative trick).** The sampling distribution depends on $\theta$, and
$\nabla\pi = \pi\,\nabla\log\pi$ turns the gradient into an expectation we can sample:
$$\nabla_\theta J = \mathbb{E}\big[r(x,y)\,\nabla_\theta \log \pi_\theta(y\mid x)\big],
\qquad \log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)=\sum_k \log\pi_\theta(y_k\mid x, y_{<k}).$$
That is **REINFORCE** (Williams, 1992): sample, then push up the log-probability of each sample in
proportion to its reward.

**Baseline.** Subtracting any $b(x)$ that does not depend on $y$ leaves the gradient unbiased, since
$\mathbb{E}[\nabla\log\pi]=0$, but can cut its variance dramatically. PPO learns $b$ with a value
network (a critic). **GRPO** (DeepSeekMath; Shao et al., 2024) samples $G$ answers per prompt and uses
**their own mean**:
$$A_i = \frac{r_i - \operatorname{mean}(r_1..r_G)}{\operatorname{std}(r_1..r_G) + \varepsilon}.$$
No critic is needed, and the baseline is exactly on-policy. Answers *better than their siblings* go up;
worse ones go down.

**Clipping.** To reuse one batch of samples for a few gradient steps (`inner_epochs`), weight each
token by the ratio $\rho = \pi_\theta / \pi_{\text{old}}$, and **clip** it so that one batch cannot move
the policy too far (PPO; Schulman et al., 2017):
$$\mathcal{L}_{\text{clip}} = -\min\big(\rho A,\ \operatorname{clip}(\rho, 1-\epsilon, 1+\epsilon)\,A\big).$$

**KL anchor.** $\beta\,\mathrm{KL}(\pi_\theta\,\|\,\pi_{\text{ref}})$ keeps the policy near the warm
start. It is estimated per token with the non-negative "k3" estimator
$e^{\Delta}-\Delta-1$, $\Delta=\log\pi_{\text{ref}}-\log\pi_\theta$.

The cells below make each piece concrete, using the classes the trainer uses.

## 5. Run online GRPO with a verifiable reward

`OnlineRLExperiment.run` repeats the loop of 1 for 1000 steps. Each step samples 16 prompts  8 answers
from the current policy, scores them with the verifier, and runs 2 clipped updates. It records
accuracy every 50 steps and the answer distribution of three tracked *unseen* prompts.

**Reading the curves.**

* Accuracy on the **unseen** facts climbs from near zero. The model was never told those answers; it
  sampled them, the verifier said "yes", and GRPO made them more likely.
* **Zero-variance groups** rise as prompts get solved: when all 8 answers are right, $A = 0$ and the
  prompt stops producing a gradient. Late in training, the learning signal comes only from the prompts
  the model still gets *sometimes* right.
* **KL** stays small most of the time, with occasional **spikes**. The k3 estimator contains
  $e^{\Delta}$, so a token the reference liked but the policy now almost never produces
  ($\pi_\theta \ll \pi_{\text{ref}}$) gives a huge per-token value. Such spikes are a known
  property of per-token KL estimates; large-scale recipes clip the log-ratio, use a small , or drop the
  KL term once the reward is verifiable.

### One GRPO step, animated (real rollouts from step 0)

### The policy's beliefs over 1000 steps

Each bar is $\pi(\text{answer}\mid\text{prompt})$ for one *unseen* prompt, recorded every 50 steps.
The right panel shows greedy accuracy over the same steps. Probability mass that starts spread over
plausible guesses moves onto the answer the verifier accepted.

## 6. Exploration decides what can be learned

Policy gradients only reinforce what the policy **actually samples**. Two things kill exploration:

* a **cold sampling temperature** (1.0 instead of 1.5): the model keeps sampling its favourite answer;
* an **over-confident warm start** (150 SFT steps instead of 60): wrong answers on unseen facts become
  near-deterministic.

In both cases, most groups contain 8 copies of the *same* answer. With equal rewards the advantages are
0, and there is no gradient, however long you train. Each ablation below changes one line of config
and runs 400 steps.

## 7. Self-reward: online RL with **no labels at all**

Now remove the verifier. **Test-Time RL** (TTRL; Zuo et al., 2025) uses the policy's own consensus as
the label: sample $G$ answers, take the **majority vote** as a pseudo-label, and reward agreement with
it. Nothing external is read, so the model trains purely on its own outputs.

What should we expect? Majority voting is usually *more accurate than a single sample*, so rewarding
agreement can pull the policy toward the majority answer, and the method works when **the majority
is right**. When the majority is wrong, the same mechanism **locks the error in**, because consistency
is all the reward measures. The run below uses the same warm start and changes one line in the YAML
(`reward.type: majority_vote`).

**What happened.** The self-reward climbs toward 1 almost immediately: the model becomes
**self-consistent**, and the diversity of its answers collapses. Accuracy does **not** follow. The table
shows why. On this weak warm start the initial majority vote is wrong for most prompts, and which
answer ends up "consistent" is not tied to the truth: wrong majorities get entrenched, and even some
prompts with a right initial majority drift to a different, wrong consensus. The cell below prints the
exact counts. TTRL's positive results rely on the opposite regime: a base model whose majority vote is
already clearly better than its single samples.

The lesson, matching the TTRL paper's own analysis: **label-free self-reward amplifies the model's
existing beliefs**. It is a strong tool when the base model's majority vote is already better than its
single samples (large models, easy-ish problems), and a trap otherwise. In practice it is combined
with verifiers, held-out checks and early stopping on a labelled dev set.

Below, the same GRPO step animated with the pseudo-label, and the distribution of a prompt whose
initial majority was **wrong** (purple) collapsing onto it.

## 8. Online RL for diffusion LLMs

Everything above needed one number per rollout: $\log\pi_\theta(y\mid x)$. An autoregressive model gives
it **exactly**, in one teacher-forced pass. A **masked-diffusion LM** (this repo's `diffusion` variant)
generates by unmasking tokens in an order chosen at sampling time, so

$$\pi_\theta(y\mid x) = \sum_{\text{unmasking orders}} \;\prod_{\text{steps}} p_\theta(\text{revealed tokens}\mid\text{current mask}),$$

which is **intractable**. **d1 / diffu-GRPO** (Zhao et al., 2025) replaces it with a one-pass
**mean-field estimate**:

1. mask **every** completion token (the state at $t = 1$);
2. also mask a random ~15 % of the **prompt** tokens; this differs per inner epoch, acting as a
   regulariser so one rollout can be reused for several updates;
3. one forward pass; read each completion token's log-probability independently and sum.

The trainer is unchanged. The only new piece is the registered `MaskedDiffusionLogProb` for
`variant: diffusion`, which is the payoff of putting estimators behind an interface.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph AR["autoregressive: exact"]
    A1["[x ; y] teacher-forced"] --> A2["1 causal pass"] --> A3["_k log (y_k | x, y_<k)"]
  end
  subgraph DL["masked diffusion: d1 estimate"]
    D1["[x with ~15% masked ; y all masked]"] --> D2["1 bidirectional pass at t = 1"] --> D3["_k log p(y_k | masked view)"]
  end
```

The animation runs the estimator on the diffusion warm-start model, with three inner-epoch views of
one rollout.

The diffusion model improves with the very same loop, but more slowly. That is expected from the
mean-field estimate: it scores each answer digit as if the other were unknown, while the sampler can
reveal both digits **in the same step**, so the policy gradient sees a factorised approximation of the
joint it samples from. d1 pairs this estimator with SFT on reasoning traces and much larger models;
follow-ups improve the likelihood estimate (more masks or timesteps per rollout, variance-reduced
ELBO estimators) at extra compute.

## 9. Pitfalls checklist

| symptom | cause | what to do |
|---|---|---|
| reward flat, many **zero-variance groups** | policy never samples a correct (or an incorrect) answer | raise temperature, weaker or more diverse SFT, larger G, curriculum on difficulty |
| reward  but true accuracy  | **reward hacking**: self-reward rewards consistency, a learned RM has exploits | use verifiable rewards where possible; track a labelled dev set; early stop |
| diversity collapses, pass@k drops | entropy collapse from over-sharpening | KL anchor (), clip , entropy bonus, fewer inner epochs |
| unstable or exploding updates | stale ratios (too many inner epochs), too high lr | keep  near 1 (clip fraction < ~20 %), smaller lr |
| long answers favoured or penalised | per-sequence length normalisation, std scaling | Dr. GRPO (no std / length normalisation): `normalize_std: false` |
| dLLM learns slowly | noisy one-step log-prob estimate | more inner epochs with fresh prompt masks, better ELBO estimators |

## Summary

* **Online RL** = the model generates its training data from its *current* self, a reward judges it,
  and policy gradients shift probability toward better-than-average samples.
* **GRPO** replaces the critic with a **group baseline**; with clipping and a KL anchor it is the
  workhorse behind recent reasoning models.
* **Exploration is the bottleneck.** A group with no variance teaches nothing, so sampling temperature
  and the confidence of the warm start matter as much as the learning rate.
* **Self-reward** (majority vote) needs no labels but optimises *consistency*. It helps when the
  majority is right and entrenches errors otherwise.
* **Diffusion LLMs** need a log-likelihood estimate (d1's one-pass mean field). With that one plug-in,
  the same trainer works.

**Code:** `src/hale_llm/rl/` (one class per file; registries `REWARDS`, `LOG_PROB_ESTIMATORS`),
`configs/rl/*.yaml`, animations in `src/hale_llm/visualization/rl_films/`, tests in
`tests/unit/test_online_rl.py`.

## References

* J. Schulman et al. **Proximal Policy Optimization algorithms**, 2017. [arXiv:1707.06347](https://arxiv.org/abs/1707.06347)
* Z. Shao et al. **DeepSeekMath** (introduces GRPO), 2024. [arXiv:2402.03300](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.03300)
* DeepSeek-AI. **DeepSeek-R1**: incentivizing reasoning capability in LLMs via RL, 2025. [arXiv:2501.12948](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.12948)
* W. Yuan et al. **Self-Rewarding Language Models**, 2024. [arXiv:2401.10020](https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.10020)
* Y. Zuo et al. **TTRL: Test-Time Reinforcement Learning**, 2025. [arXiv:2504.16084](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.16084)
* S. Zhao et al. **d1: Scaling reasoning in diffusion LLMs via RL** (diffu-GRPO), 2025. [arXiv:2504.12216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12216)
* J. Schulman. **Approximating KL divergence** (the k1/k2/k3 estimators), blog post, 2020.

# 02  Learning from experience: Monte Carlo, TD, SARSA and Q-learning

In notebook 00 we solved an MDP with **dynamic programming** (value / policy iteration). That only works
if someone hands us the transition probabilities $P(s' \mid s, a)$ and the reward function. Real problems
almost never come with that manual: a robot does not know the physics of its gripper in closed form, and
an LLM certainly has no table of "probability that the user will be happy after this token".

This notebook is about **model-free** reinforcement learning: learning values and policies purely from
*sampled experience* $(s, a, r, s')$. Every algorithm here is tabular (one number per state or
state-action pair), so you can literally print the whole "brain" of the agent. Notebook 03 replaces the
table with a neural network (DQN), and from notebook 04 on we move to policy-gradient methods used for LLMs
- but the **TD error** you meet here is the same quantity that powers the PPO critic and GAE used in RLHF.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    N00[00 Foundations<br/>MDP, Bellman, DP] --> N01[01 Bandits<br/>exploration]
    N01 --> N02[02 Tabular TD<br/>MC, TD, SARSA, Q-learning]:::here
    N02 --> N03[03 DQN<br/>neural Q-functions]
    N02 --> N04[04 Policy gradient]
    N04 --> N05[05 Actor-critic & GAE<br/>uses the TD error!]
    N05 --> N06[06 PPO] --> N07[07 RLHF]
    classDef here fill:#2563eb,color:#fff,stroke:#1e3a8a,stroke-width:3px
```

**What you will learn**

| Section | Algorithm | Question it answers |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Monte Carlo (MC) prediction | "How good is a state?" - by averaging complete returns |
| 2 | TD(0) prediction | Same question, but learning *after every step* by bootstrapping |
| 3 | n-step TD | The dial between MC and TD |
| 4 | SARSA | Learn to *act* (control), on-policy |
| 5 | Q-learning | Learn to act optimally while behaving exploratorily (off-policy) |
| 6 | Double Q-learning | Fix the *maximization bias* of Q-learning (sets up Double DQN) |
| 7 | Connections to LLMs | TD errors in PPO critics, Q-functions over tokens (ILQL, "LLM logits are Q-values") |

## 0  Setup and notation recap

| Symbol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| $s_t, a_t, r_{t+1}$ | state, action, and the reward received *after* taking $a_t$ |
| $\gamma \in [0,1]$ | discount factor |
| $G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma r_{t+2} + \gamma^2 r_{t+3} + \dots$ | the **return**: total discounted future reward |
| $V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s]$ | state value under policy $\pi$ |
| $Q^\pi(s,a) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s, a_t = a]$ | action value |
| $\alpha$ | learning rate / step size |

The single most useful identity in this notebook is the **recursive definition of the return**:

$$G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma\, G_{t+1}.$$

MC methods use the left-hand side (wait for the true $G_t$). TD methods use the right-hand side and
replace the unknown $G_{t+1}$ with the current *estimate* $V(s_{t+1})$. That replacement is called
**bootstrapping** - "pulling yourself up by your own bootstraps", i.e. learning a guess from a guess.

### A picture first: three ways to "back up" a value

A **backup diagram** shows *which future information* is used to update the value of the state at the top.

* **Dynamic programming** looks one step ahead but over *all* possible next states (needs the model).
* **Monte Carlo** follows *one* sampled path all the way to the end of the episode.
* **TD(0)** follows *one* sampled step and then trusts the current estimate $V(s')$.

The figure below is generated with matplotlib (and saved to `assets/rl/course/02_backup_diagrams.png`).

## 1  The test-bed for prediction: the 5-state Random Walk

To compare *prediction* algorithms (estimating $V^\pi$ for a fixed policy) we use the classic Random Walk
from Sutton & Barto (Example 6.2):

```
 [0]  A  B  C  D  E  [+1]
              start
```

* every episode starts in the middle state **C**;
* at each step the agent moves left or right with probability 1/2 (the policy is fixed - we only *evaluate* it);
* reaching the right end gives reward **+1**, the left end gives **0**, every other reward is 0; $\gamma = 1$.

Because the dynamics are so simple, we know the **true values**: $V(s)$ is the probability of finishing on
the right, i.e. $V(A..E) = 1/6, 2/6, 3/6, 4/6, 5/6$. That lets us measure exactly how wrong each
algorithm's estimate is (root-mean-square error, RMS).

## 2  Monte Carlo prediction

### Intuition
You want to know how long your commute takes. The Monte Carlo way: drive to work many times, write down
the *total* time of each trip, and average. No theory of traffic needed - just complete experiences.

### Math, step by step
By definition $V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi[G_t \mid s_t = s]$. The law of large numbers says the sample mean
of returns observed from $s$ converges to that expectation:

$$V(s) \approx \frac{1}{N(s)} \sum_{i=1}^{N(s)} G^{(i)}(s).$$

Computing a running mean incrementally, after seeing the $N$-th return $G$:

$$\bar G_N = \bar G_{N-1} + \frac{1}{N}\big(G - \bar G_{N-1}\big).$$

Replace $1/N$ by a constant step size $\alpha$ (so that recent episodes weigh more - useful when the
policy changes) and you get **constant-$\alpha$ MC**:

$$\boxed{V(s_t) \leftarrow V(s_t) + \alpha\,\big[\,G_t - V(s_t)\,\big]}$$

Read it as *"move the estimate a little bit towards the target"*. The target $G_t$ is an **unbiased** sample
of $V^\pi(s_t)$, but it can have **high variance** because it sums many random rewards.

### Algorithm steps (every-visit, constant- MC)

1. **Initialise** $V(s)$ arbitrarily (here 0.5) for every non-terminal state.
2. **Generate an episode** $s_0, r_1, s_1, r_2, \dots, s_T$ by following $\pi$ until termination.
   *Why:* MC needs the complete return, so it cannot learn mid-episode.
3. **Compute returns backwards**: $G \leftarrow 0$; for $t = T-1, \dots, 0$: $G \leftarrow r_{t+1} + \gamma G$.
   *Why:* going backwards turns the sum into one multiply-add per step (the recursion $G_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma G_{t+1}$).
4. **Update** each visited state: $V(s_t) \leftarrow V(s_t) + \alpha [G - V(s_t)]$.
5. **Repeat** from step 2.

## 3  Temporal-Difference learning: TD(0)

### Intuition
Back to the commute. You leave the office; after 10 minutes you hit a traffic jam on the highway. You
*already* know the trip will be long - you do not need to wait until you get home to revise your estimate.
TD learning updates "how long from the office" using *"10 minutes so far + my current estimate of how long
from the highway"*. It learns from **every step**, before the outcome is known.

### Math, step by step
Start from the Bellman expectation equation for $V^\pi$ (notebook 00):

$$V^\pi(s) = \mathbb{E}_\pi\big[r_{t+1} + \gamma V^\pi(s_{t+1}) \mid s_t = s\big].$$

1. We cannot compute the expectation (no model), so take **one sample**: $r_{t+1} + \gamma V^\pi(s_{t+1})$.
2. We do not know $V^\pi(s_{t+1})$ either, so use our **current estimate** $V(s_{t+1})$ (bootstrapping).
3. This gives the **TD target** $\;y_t = r_{t+1} + \gamma V(s_{t+1})$, used exactly like $G_t$ in MC:

$$\boxed{V(s_t) \leftarrow V(s_t) + \alpha\,\underbrace{\big[r_{t+1} + \gamma V(s_{t+1}) - V(s_t)\big]}_{\delta_t\ =\ \text{TD error}}}$$

The **TD error** $\delta_t$ measures *surprise*: positive if things went better than expected, negative if
worse. (Neuroscience fun fact: the firing of dopamine neurons closely tracks a TD error - Schultz, Dayan &
Montague, 1997.)

**Bias vs variance.** The TD target depends on one random reward and one random next state, so it has much
lower variance than $G_t$. But it is **biased** while $V$ is still wrong. In practice the variance reduction
usually wins: TD typically learns faster than MC - we will measure this below. Remember this trade-off;
GAE in notebook 05 is literally a knob between the two.

| | target | bias | variance | can learn online / mid-episode | needs episode to end |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MC | $G_t$ | none | high | no | yes |
| TD(0) | $r + \gamma V(s')$ | yes (from $V$) | low | yes | no |

### Algorithm steps (TD(0) prediction)

1. **Initialise** $V(s)$ (0.5 here); terminal states have $V = 0$ forever.
2. **Start** an episode in $s$.
3. **Act** with $\pi$, observe $r$ and $s'$.
4. **Compute** the TD error $\delta = r + \gamma V(s') - V(s)$ (use $V(s')=0$ if $s'$ is terminal).
   *Why terminal = 0:* there is no future after the end, so nothing to bootstrap from.
5. **Update** $V(s) \leftarrow V(s) + \alpha\,\delta$, set $s \leftarrow s'$, and go to step 3 until the episode ends.

### Animation: watching MC and TD estimates converge

Both learners see **the same 60 episodes** (same random seed) with the same step size $\alpha = 0.1$. Bars are
the current estimates, the dashed line is the truth. Watch how:

* TD values change **smoothly** and even states never visited near the end get sensible values early;
* MC values **jump** - each update uses one whole noisy return, so a single lucky/unlucky episode moves them a lot;
* the right panel tracks the RMS error of each method episode by episode.

### Experiment: MC vs TD averaged over 100 runs

One run is anecdotal. Below we repeat the experiment 100 times for several step sizes and plot the average
RMS error over the first 100 episodes (this reproduces the shape of Sutton & Barto's Figure 6.2).

## 4  n-step TD: the dial between TD and MC

### Intuition
TD(0) looks 1 step ahead before trusting its estimate; MC looks all the way to the end. Why not look
$n$ steps ahead? **n-step TD** uses $n$ real rewards and then bootstraps.

### Math
The **n-step return** is

$$G_{t:t+n} = r_{t+1} + \gamma r_{t+2} + \dots + \gamma^{n-1} r_{t+n} + \gamma^n V(s_{t+n}),$$

(if the episode ends before $t+n$, it is just the ordinary return). The update is again
$V(s_t) \leftarrow V(s_t) + \alpha[G_{t:t+n} - V(s_t)]$. $n=1$ gives TD(0); $n=\infty$ gives MC.

Each extra real reward reduces bias (less reliance on $V$) and adds variance (more random terms). Usually an
intermediate $n$ is best. Averaging *all* n-step returns with weights $(1-\lambda)\lambda^{n-1}$ gives the
**-return**, TD() - and the same idea applied to advantages is **GAE()**, the advantage estimator inside
PPO for RLHF (notebook 05).

### Algorithm steps (n-step TD prediction)

1. Keep a buffer of the last $n+1$ states and rewards of the current episode.
2. At each time step $t$, once you have observed $r_{\tau+1..\tau+n}$ for the state visited at time $\tau = t - n + 1$,
   compute $G_{\tau:\tau+n}$ and update $V(s_\tau)$.
3. When the episode terminates at time $T$, flush the remaining states with truncated returns (no bootstrap).

Here we use the larger **19-state** random walk with rewards $-1$ (left) / $+1$ (right), whose true values are
$-0.9, -0.8, \dots, 0.9$ - more states make the advantage of larger $n$ visible (Sutton & Barto Figure 7.2).

Note: to keep the code short we compute all updates of an episode *after* it ends (an "offline" variant). The
online version from the book updates during the episode; the qualitative picture is the same.

## 5  From prediction to control: learning to *act*

So far the policy was fixed. Now we want the agent to **find a good policy**. The recipe is
**Generalised Policy Iteration (GPI)**: alternate between *evaluating* the current policy and *improving* it
by acting greedily with respect to the current estimates.

Two key changes compared to prediction:

1. **Learn $Q(s,a)$, not $V(s)$.** Acting greedily w.r.t. $V$ requires the model ($\arg\max_a \sum_{s'} P(s'|s,a)[\dots]$).
   Acting greedily w.r.t. $Q$ is just $\arg\max_a Q(s,a)$ - model-free.
2. **Keep exploring.** If we always pick the current best action, we never discover that another action is
   better (notebook 01's bandit lesson). The simplest fix is **-greedy**: with probability  pick a random
   action, otherwise the greedy one.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    Q["Q(s,a) table"] -->|"-greedy<br/>(improve)"| P["behaviour policy "]
    P -->|"act in env"| E["experience<br/>(s, a, r, s', a')"]
    E -->|"TD update<br/>(evaluate)"| Q
    style Q fill:#fde68a,stroke:#ea580c
    style P fill:#dbeafe,stroke:#2563eb
    style E fill:#dcfce7,stroke:#16a34a
```

### The test-bed: Cliff Walking (Sutton & Barto, Example 6.6)

A 412 grid. Start **S** bottom-left, goal **G** bottom-right. Every step costs 1. The bottom row between
S and G is a **cliff**: stepping into it costs 100 and sends you back to S. The shortest path runs right
along the cliff edge (13 steps, return 13). A path one row higher is safer but longer.

## 6  SARSA - on-policy TD control

### Intuition
SARSA learns *"how good is it to do $a$ in $s$, given that I will keep behaving the way I actually behave
(including my occasional random -moves)"*. Because it accounts for its own clumsiness, a SARSA agent near
a cliff learns to keep a safe distance.

### Math
Apply TD(0) to action values. The Bellman equation for $Q^\pi$ is
$Q^\pi(s,a) = \mathbb{E}[r + \gamma Q^\pi(s', a') ]$ with $a' \sim \pi(\cdot \mid s')$. Sampling it gives

$$\boxed{Q(s,a) \leftarrow Q(s,a) + \alpha\big[r + \gamma\,Q(s', a') - Q(s,a)\big]}$$

The update uses the quintuple $(s, a, r, s', a')$ - hence the name **S-A-R-S-A** (Rummery & Niranjan 1994;
named by Sutton 1996). It is **on-policy**: the $a'$ in the target is the action the agent will *actually* take.

### Algorithm steps

1. Initialise $Q(s,a) = 0$ for all pairs (terminal $Q = 0$).
2. Start an episode in $s$; choose $a$ -greedily from $Q$.
3. Take $a$; observe $r, s'$.
4. Choose $a'$ -greedily from $Q(s', \cdot)$ - *before* updating, because it is part of the target.
5. Update $Q(s,a)$ towards $r + \gamma Q(s',a')$ (target $= r$ if $s'$ is terminal).
6. $s \leftarrow s'$, $a \leftarrow a'$ (the action chosen in step 4 really is executed next). Repeat from 3.

## 7  Q-learning - off-policy TD control

### Intuition
Q-learning asks a bolder question: *"how good is $a$ in $s$ if, from then on, I behave **optimally**"* - even
though, while learning, it still explores randomly. It learns about the greedy (target) policy while
following an -greedy (behaviour) policy. That separation is called **off-policy** learning.

### Math
Sample the **Bellman optimality** equation $Q^*(s,a) = \mathbb{E}[r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q^*(s',a')]$:

$$\boxed{Q(s,a) \leftarrow Q(s,a) + \alpha\big[r + \gamma\,\max_{a'} Q(s', a') - Q(s,a)\big]}$$

(Watkins, 1989.) The only change from SARSA is $Q(s',a') \to \max_{a'} Q(s',a')$, but the consequences are big:

* Q-learning converges to $Q^*$ (under standard conditions: every pair visited infinitely often, decaying step sizes)
  **regardless of the behaviour policy** - so it can learn from old data, other agents, or human demonstrations.
* Because it learns the value of the *greedy* policy, it ignores the cost of its own exploration. On the cliff
  this makes it walk the edge - and fall in more often *while learning*.

### Algorithm steps

1. Initialise $Q(s,a) = 0$.
2. Start an episode in $s$.
3. Choose $a$ -greedily (behaviour policy) and take it; observe $r, s'$.
