Blue arrows indicate that this data is used to train one of our models. In Step 2, boxes A-D are samples from our models that get ranked by labelers."

### The one-line version of each method

| method | what the model is shown | how it learns | extra models needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| **SFT** | the *right answer* | maximise the likelihood of the demo | none |
| **RLHF (PPO)** | a score for *its own* answers, from a learned reward model | maximise reward, stay close to the starting model | reward model + critic + frozen reference |
| **DPO** | pairs: *better answer / worse answer* | raise the better one relative to the worse one, relative to a frozen reference | frozen reference |
| **GRPO** | a score for each of *G* answers to the same prompt | push up answers that beat their own group's average | frozen reference |
| **RLVR** | whether a checker says the answer is *correct* | RL (PPO, GRPO, ...) with that checker as the reward | none for the reward |

## 1. A language model is a policy

Strip away the engineering and a decoder-only LM does one thing: given the tokens so far, output a probability for the next token. To *answer* a prompt $x$ it samples one token at a time, feeding each choice back in:

$$\pi_\theta(y\mid x)\;=\;\prod_{t=1}^{|y|}\pi_\theta\big(y_t \mid x,\,y_{<t}\big).$$

Reinforcement-learning language calls this a **policy**: each next-token choice is an **action**, the text so far is the **state**, and the finished answer $y$ is the **trajectory**. Every method in this notebook only needs the model to do two things:

1. **`generate`** - sample an answer token by token (slow: one forward pass per token);
2. **`sequence_logprobs`** - score a *finished* answer: $\log\pi_\theta(y_t\mid x,y_{<t})$ for every token, in **one parallel pass** (this "teacher forcing" is why training on samples is affordable).

Our toy world: a character-level GPT (2 layers, 64 dims) answers prompts like `7+5=` with `12`. The *checker* is exact match, which is what makes the task **verifiable**.

**Scoring a finished answer in one pass - and a check that it is right.** `sequence_logprobs` should equal the slow way (feed the prefix, read one token's log-probability, repeat):

## 2. The base model: it "knows" the answer, but only says it 30% of the time

Real base models are trained on messy text, so they have *seen* the right answer but also plenty of wrong ones. We mimic that: pre-train the GPT on all 100 addition problems, but make **70% of the answers wrong** (off by a few). It then learns the *distribution of answers in its data*: the right answer is its single most likely guess, yet it is sampled less than a third of the time.

We keep 70 prompts for post-training (`TRAIN`) and 30 never-seen prompts for scoring (`TEST`). Post-training will never be shown a test prompt.

**Measuring.** Our headline number is **sampled accuracy**: draw an answer at temperature 1 (exactly how RL later samples) and check it against the gold answer - averaged over prompts and 16 samples each. We also track **KL** from the base model (how far training has moved the model) and the **greedy** accuracy (always pick the most likely token) for reference.

Look at what the base model actually produces. Each tile is one sampled answer: **green = the checker says correct, red = wrong**.

**Read it.** The correct answer is the most common single answer (green bar) but at only ~30% - the rest of the probability is smeared over near-misses. **Post-training will not teach the model arithmetic; it will teach it to *commit* to what it already half-knows.** Different methods do that with different signals - that is the whole story.

## 3. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT): learn by imitation

**Idea.** Show the model prompts with the *correct* answer and train it to predict those tokens. It is exactly the pre-training loss, with two differences: the data are *curated demonstrations*, and the loss is computed **only on the answer** (we do not want to train the model to predict the user's question):

$$\mathcal L_{\text{SFT}}(\theta)=-\frac{1}{|y|}\sum_{t=1}^{|y|}\log\pi_\theta\big(y_t^\star\mid x,\,y^\star_{<t}\big)\qquad\text{($y^\star$ = the demonstrated answer)}$$

**Check the loss is what the formula says.** `lm_loss` (cross-entropy on answer tokens) must equal "minus the mean log-probability of the demonstrated answer tokens", computed independently from `sequence_logprobs`:

### SFT in action - watch the probabilities move

We train on the 70 clean demonstrations and, every few steps, read the model's probability for the **first** and **second** answer digit of `7+5=` (gold: `1` then `2`). Gold in green.

**Read it.** Before training the digit probabilities are spread out; by step ~30 nearly all the mass sits on the demonstrated digits and the held-out accuracy (right panel, *never-seen prompts*) climbs with it. SFT is simple, stable and cheap - **but it needs the correct answer for every training prompt**. How much does it matter how many demonstrations you have?

**What SFT cannot do.** It only ever sees *positive* examples: it learns what to imitate, never what to avoid; it cannot exceed its demonstrations; and the demonstrations are costly. The next sections use **feedback on the model's own answers** instead.

## 4. Where does the learning signal come from? Two kinds of reward

SFT needs the right *answer*. Reinforcement learning needs only a **score for an answer the model produced itself**: a reward $R(x,y)$. Where that number comes from is the main design choice in modern post-training:

| | learned reward model (RLHF) | verifiable reward (RLVR) |
|---|---|---|
| source | a neural net trained on human **preferences** | a **program** that checks the answer |
| works for | anything people can compare ("which reply is better?") | things with a checkable answer: maths, code, formats |
| weakness | imperfect, and a strong optimiser *exploits* its mistakes | needs a checker; silent on style/safety |

### 4a. Preferences and the Bradley-Terry reward model

Asking a person to *write* a perfect answer is hard; asking which of two answers is **better** is easy. We collect pairs $(y_w \succ y_l)$ - winner and loser for the same prompt. To turn them into a number we assume the **Bradley-Terry** model: the probability that $y_w$ wins is

$$P(y_w\succ y_l\mid x)=\sigma\big(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l)\big),\qquad \sigma(z)=\tfrac{1}{1+e^{-z}},$$

and the reward model $r_\phi$ (a transformer with a scalar head) is trained by maximum likelihood:

$$\mathcal L_{\text{RM}}(\phi)=-\mathbb E\big[\log\sigma\big(r_\phi(x,y_w)-r_\phi(x,y_l)\big)\big].$$

Only the *difference* matters, so the reward has no absolute scale. Check the loss on hand numbers before trusting it:

**Collecting preference data.** For each prompt we sample 8 answers from the *base* model and pair one correct with one wrong answer. A human labeler is imperfect, so we **flip 10% of the labels** (noise a real pipeline always has):

**The reward model** is the base transformer with one extra linear layer that reads the hidden state at the **last token** of the answer (`<eos>`) and outputs a single number.

**Is the reward model any good?** Test it on **prompts it never saw**, with *clean* labels (sampled answers from the base model, scored by the true checker):

### 4b. The verifiable reward: a program

For maths, code or exact formats we can skip the learned judge entirely. The **verifier** just runs a check - here, exact match:

Setting **RLVR** (RL with verifiable rewards) apart from RLHF is exactly this swap: *no reward model to train, nothing to exploit*. Hold that thought - first we need the RL machinery itself.

## 5. The RL objective, in one page

Whatever the reward, post-training RL maximises

$$J(\theta)\;=\;\mathbb E_{x\sim\mathcal D,\;y\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot|x)}\big[R(x,y)\big]\;-\;\beta\;\mathrm{KL}\big(\pi_\theta(\cdot|x)\,\|\,\pi_{\text{ref}}(\cdot|x)\big).$$

- **First term:** get a high reward on your own samples.
- **Second term - the KL "leash":** do not drift far from the *reference* model $\pi_{\text{ref}}$ (the frozen starting point). Without it the model finds weird outputs that fool the reward. $\beta$ is the leash length.

**The policy-gradient trick.** We cannot differentiate through *sampling* a token, but we can use $\nabla_\theta\pi=\pi\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi$:

$$\nabla_\theta\,\mathbb E_{y\sim\pi_\theta}[R]=\mathbb E_{y\sim\pi_\theta}\big[\,R(y)\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y)\,\big]\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\nabla_\theta J\approx\frac1N\sum_i \big(R_i-b\big)\,\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(y_i).$$

"Raise the log-probability of answers in proportion to how good they were." Subtracting a **baseline** $b$ (anything not depending on the sampled answer) leaves the expected gradient unchanged but removes noise. **Every algorithm below (PPO, GRPO, ...) is this formula plus a different choice of baseline and a safety clip.** Verify on a case with an exact answer - four possible outputs:

**The leash has a closed-form answer.** For a single decision, maximising $\mathbb E_\pi[R]-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}(\pi\|\pi_{\text{ref}})$ has the exact solution

$$\boxed{\;\pi^\star(y)\;=\;\frac{1}{Z}\,\pi_{\text{ref}}(y)\,\exp\!\big(R(y)/\beta\big)\;}$$

("reweight the reference by $e^{R/\beta}$"). Gradient ascent on the objective should land on exactly this - and this identity is what makes **DPO** possible in section 7:

## 6. RLHF with PPO: the original recipe

**Proximal Policy Optimization** (PPO, Schulman et al., 2017) is the RL algorithm InstructGPT used. For language models it runs **four models**:

| model | role | trained? |
|---|---|---|
| **policy** $\pi_\theta$ | writes answers | yes |
| **reference** $\pi_{\text{ref}}$ | the frozen starting point for the KL leash | no |
| **reward model** | scores a finished answer | no |
| **critic** $V_\phi$ | predicts the reward-to-go from each token (reduces noise) | yes |

![PPO vs GRPO](../../assets/rl/course/paper_deepseekmath_fig4_ppo_vs_grpo.png)

**DeepSeekMath (Shao et al., 2024), Fig. 4 (PDF p. 13):** "Demonstration of PPO and our GRPO. GRPO foregoes the value model, instead estimating the baseline from group scores, significantly reducing training resources."

*What to look for:* the top row is what we build in this section (policy, reference, reward, value model, GAE); the bottom row is section 8.

### 6a. The reward the policy actually optimises

The reward model scores only the *finished* answer, but PPO wants credit for every *token*. So each answer token gets the KL penalty, and the final token also gets the reward-model score:

$$r_t \;=\; -\beta\,\big[\log\pi_\theta(y_t|x,y_{<t})-\log\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_t|x,y_{<t})\big]\;+\;\mathbb 1[t=\text{last}]\cdot r_\phi(x,y).$$

### 6b. Advantages with GAE

The **advantage** $A_t$ says "was this token better or worse than the critic expected?". **Generalised advantage estimation** blends one-step and full-return estimates with $\lambda$:

$$\delta_t=r_t+\gamma V_{t+1}-V_t,\qquad A_t=\sum_{l\ge0}(\gamma\lambda)^l\,\delta_{t+l}.$$

$\lambda=0$ trusts the critic (low noise, some bias); $\lambda=1$ ignores it (the plain reward-to-go). Check both ends:

### 6c. The clipped objective - "proximal" means "do not move too far"

Reusing the same sampled answers for several gradient steps is cheaper, but the policy moves away from the one that *produced* the samples. PPO measures the move with the probability ratio $\rho_t=\pi_\theta/\pi_{\theta_{\text{old}}}$ and **clips** it:

$$L^{\text{clip}}=\mathbb E_t\big[\min\big(\rho_t A_t,\;\operatorname{clip}(\rho_t,1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\,A_t\big)\big].$$

Read it case by case ($\epsilon=0.2$): a good token ($A>0$) stops earning extra credit once $\rho>1.2$; a bad token ($A<0$) stops being pushed down once $\rho<0.8$ - but a *wrong-direction* move is never clipped away.

### 6d. The whole loop

**A sanity check before running it.** Right after sampling, the policy has not moved, so $\rho=1$ and the clip is inactive: the PPO gradient must reduce to the plain "advantage times $\nabla\log\pi$" policy gradient. Then we run it with the *learned* reward model.

**Read the animation.** Tiles turn greener and held-out accuracy rises - but look at the second curve: the **reward-model score keeps climbing**. And look closely at the *tiles in the last frames*: for some prompts every sample is the **same wrong answer** - the policy found something the reward model likes and committed to it (section 10 counts how often). Does *true* accuracy follow the score?

**What you are seeing is reward-model over-optimisation (Goodhart's law).** The policy is optimised against the *reward model*, not against the truth. Once it has learned everything the reward model gets right, the remaining gains come from **its mistakes** - the score rises while real quality stalls. Two practical consequences: RLHF is only as good as the reward model, and the **KL leash $\beta$** exists to limit how far the policy can wander into the reward model's blind spots. Try two leash lengths:

## 7. DPO: RLHF without the reward model and without the RL loop

RLHF has two stages (fit a reward model, then run RL against it), which is where the cost and the instability come from. **Direct Preference Optimization** (Rafailov et al., 2023) observes that the pair of stages can be collapsed into **one supervised-style loss on the preference pairs**.

![DPO vs RLHF](../../assets/rl/course/paper_dpo_fig1.png)

**DPO (Rafailov et al., 2023), Fig. 1 (PDF p. 2):** "DPO optimizes for human preferences while avoiding reinforcement learning. Existing methods for fine-tuning language models with human feedback first fit a reward model to a dataset of prompts and human preferences over pairs of responses, and then use RL to find a policy that maximizes the learned reward. In contrast, DPO directly optimizes for the policy best satisfying the preferences with a simple classification objective [...]"

### The derivation in three lines (and each line is checked)

1. **The RLHF optimum** (section 5): $\;\pi^\star(y|x)=\pi_{\text{ref}}(y|x)\,e^{r(x,y)/\beta}/Z(x)$.
2. **Solve for the reward:** $\;r(x,y)=\beta\log\dfrac{\pi^\star(y|x)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y|x)}+\beta\log Z(x)$. *The policy itself encodes a reward.*
3. **Plug into Bradley-Terry.** The preference depends only on a *difference* of rewards, so the awkward $\beta\log Z(x)$ **cancels**, leaving the DPO loss with the policy $\pi_\theta$ in place of $\pi^\star$:

$$\mathcal L_{\text{DPO}}(\theta)=-\mathbb E\Big[\log\sigma\Big(\beta\big[\underbrace{\log\tfrac{\pi_\theta(y_w|x)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_w|x)}}_{\text{chosen: push up}}-\underbrace{\log\tfrac{\pi_\theta(y_l|x)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(y_l|x)}}_{\text{rejected: push down}}\big]\Big)\Big].$$

Steps 2-3 can be tested with the five-outcome example from section 5, where we know the true rewards:

### The loss in code

The only ingredients are the summed log-probabilities of the chosen and rejected answers under the policy and the frozen reference:

### Train it

Same base model, same kind of preference pairs as the reward model used (sampled from the base model, 10% label noise). **No reward model, no sampling during training, no critic** - just a loop over the pairs:

**Read it.** The loss falls from $\log 2$ and the model gets better. The right plot is the thing people find surprising: DPO optimises the **gap** between chosen and rejected - and in this run the chosen answer's log-probability **barely moves** (green, near 0) while the rejected one **plunges** (red). It is a *relative* objective that works mostly by pushing bad answers down, not "make the good answer much more likely" (the check above tests exactly this).

**DPO's honest limits.** (1) It is **offline**: the pairs came from the *base* model and are never refreshed, so the policy never gets feedback on *its own* new answers (the RL methods do). (2) It inherits the labeler's noise. (3) Because it never samples, it cannot tell you "this answer is wrong" for something outside the pairs.

## 8. GRPO: PPO without the critic

PPO's critic is a whole extra network to train and keep in memory. **Group Relative Policy Optimization** (DeepSeekMath, 2024) removes it with a simple idea: if a prompt has *several* answers, **each answer's baseline is the average reward of its own group**.

For each prompt sample $G$ answers $y_1..y_G$, score them, and use

$$\hat A_i=\frac{R_i-\operatorname{mean}(R_1..R_G)}{\operatorname{std}(R_1..R_G)+\varepsilon}\qquad\text{for every token of answer }i.$$

"Was this answer better than its siblings?" is exactly the question a baseline answers, so no learned value function is needed. The update is the *same* clipped PPO objective, plus a small KL term to the reference, estimated per token with the always-positive estimator $k_3=e^{\delta}-\delta-1,\ \delta=\log\pi_{\text{ref}}-\log\pi_\theta$:

$$\mathcal L_{\text{GRPO}}=-\mathbb E\big[\min(\rho_t\hat A_i,\ \operatorname{clip}(\rho_t,1\pm\epsilon)\hat A_i)\;-\;\beta\,k_3\big].$$

**Anatomy of one group** (from the base model, one training prompt, $G=8$):

**Check the maths** - group advantages must average to zero (the baseline is the group mean), have unit spread, and vanish when every answer got the same reward:

**The loop.** It is the PPO loop of section 6 with three deletions: no critic, no GAE, no reward-model-based reward (here the verifier scores each answer):

## 9. RLVR: when the reward is a program

**Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards** is not a new algorithm - it is a choice of reward: **skip the learned reward model and use a checker**. Tlu 3 named it and DeepSeek-R1 made it famous: a rule-based verifier (the answer matches, the code passes its tests) is the *only* feedback, and the optimiser is typically **GRPO**. We now run exactly that: GRPO + our `verifier_reward`.

**Read it.** The tiles flip from red to green with *no demonstrations and no reward model* - only a checker. And it generalises to prompts that were never trained on (the accuracy curve is held-out): RL taught the model to **commit to the answer it already preferred**. Compare the budget with the learned-reward run: same number of samples, much better reward signal.

**The R1 evidence at scale.** The same recipe, with a verifier for competition maths, drove DeepSeek-R1-Zero from 15.6% to 77.9% on AIME (pass@1), with answers growing longer as the model learned to reason:

![R1-Zero training curves](../../assets/rl/course/paper_r1_fig1_aime_length.png)

**DeepSeek-R1 (Guo et al., 2025), Fig. 1 (PDF p. 4):** "(a) AIME accuracy of DeepSeek-R1-Zero during training. [...] (b) The average response length of DeepSeek-R1-Zero on the training set during the RL process." The text on the same page: "the average pass@1 score on AIME 2024 shows a significant increase, jumping from an initial 15.6% to 77.9%."

### 9a. The catch: RL needs *some* signal to start with

GRPO learns from the *difference* between answers in a group. If every answer in a group is wrong, every advantage is **zero** and the gradient vanishes. Starting from a model that is never right, RLVR learns nothing:

That is why real pipelines do **SFT first** (a "cold start"): it lifts the model to the point where RL has something to amplify. Notice the blue curve too: as the model gets *good*, more groups become all-correct and the signal fades - RLVR is self-limiting on easy prompts, which is why practitioners filter for prompts of intermediate difficulty.

### 9b. Critic-based vs critic-free - same verifier, two optimisers

Is GRPO's advantage over the learned-reward run due to the *optimiser* or the *reward*? Run **PPO (with its critic) on the verifier reward** - only the reward changes relative to section 6:

**Read it.** The reward quality is what separated the runs. GRPO and critic-based PPO land in the same place; GRPO simply gets there **without training or storing a critic** (one network fewer), which is why it dominates large-scale reasoning RL.

### 9c. The usual real recipe: SFT, then RLVR

## 10. Head to head, on equal footing

Same base model, same train/test prompts, three seeds each. "Samples" counts the answers the *policy* generated while training; SFT and DPO generate none during training (DPO's pairs cost 8 samples per prompt up front).

The *shape* of learning also differs. Online RL methods improve as they collect more of their own samples:

### How do they *fail*? Not just how often

Accuracy hides *what kind* of wrong the model is. Two failure profiles on the 30 held-out prompts (16 samples each): how often the answer is **not even a number**, and on how many prompts the model has become **confidently wrong** (>= 90% of its samples are the *same wrong* answer):

**Two different failures, two different causes.**

- **PPO against the learned reward model** collapsed onto *one* answer per prompt - and for a good share of prompts it is a *wrong* one. This is 4's loophole made visible: a strong optimiser finds answers the judge loves and commits to them.
- **DPO** is the only method that sometimes emits something that is not even a number (a stray letter in the answer). It optimises a *gap*: making rejected answers less likely frees probability mass, and nothing in the loss says where that mass must go - including tokens that appear in no training data.
- **Verifier-based RL** (PPO or GRPO) and SFT stay clean: the checker rewards only answers that are exactly right, so there is no loophole to find.

**How to read this honestly.**

- **SFT on all 70 gold answers is the strongest method here (about 0.99)** - and it is the simplest and cheapest. Do not let the RL machinery fool you: *if you have a correct answer for every prompt and the model already contains the skill, imitation is very hard to beat.* Our toy is exactly that case (the base model already "knows" arithmetic).
- What RLVR buys is that it reaches nearly the same place with **no gold answers at all - only a checker** - and the SFT-25 row shows what happens when demonstrations are scarce (0.71).
- In real reasoning problems, demonstrations cannot cover the space, and SFT can never exceed its demonstrators, while RL on a checker can discover new strategies. A one-digit-sum toy cannot show that regime; the R1 curves in section 9 are the evidence for it.
- **Learned rewards are the weak link:** PPO+RM and DPO (both fed by preference labels) trail the verifier methods, and DPO has by far the largest seed-to-seed spread.

## 11. Pitfalls - each one you have now seen

| pitfall | where you saw it | the fix in practice |
|---|---|---|
| **Reward hacking / over-optimisation** of a learned reward | 6: reward-model score kept rising while true accuracy saturated | stronger/ensembled reward models, KL leash, early stopping, verifiable rewards where possible |
| **KL leash too loose / too tight** | 6: smaller $\beta$ drifted further from the base model | tune $\beta$; watch KL during training |
| **No signal at the start** | 9a: untrained model, zero-advantage groups | SFT cold start; curriculum of prompts the model sometimes solves |
| **Signal fades when the task is mastered** | 9a: share of informative groups falls | filter out too-easy prompts; harder data |
| **Offline data goes stale** (DPO) | 7: pairs come from the base model only | iterate: re-sample and re-label (online/iterative DPO) |
| **Noisy preferences** | 10% flipped labels in 4 | more data, cleaner labelling, noise-robust losses |

### Cheat sheet: which one when?

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    Q1{"Do you have an automatic checker<br/>(maths, code, format)?"} -- yes --> RLVR["RLVR: GRPO (or PPO) on the checker<br/>after an SFT cold start"]
    Q1 -- no --> Q2{"Do you have preference<br/>comparisons?"}
    Q2 -- yes --> Q3{"Need to keep optimising on the<br/>model's OWN new outputs?"}
    Q3 -- "no, keep it simple" --> DPO["DPO"]
    Q3 -- yes --> RLHF["Reward model + PPO (RLHF)"]
    Q2 -- no --> SFT["SFT on demonstrations"]
```

## 12. Recap

1. An LLM is a **policy**; training it means changing next-token probabilities. `generate` samples, `sequence_logprobs` scores.
2. **SFT** imitates demonstrations: simple, stable, but needs the right answer and only teaches what to copy.
3. A **reward** can be learned from preferences (Bradley-Terry) or computed by a **verifier**.
4. **RLHF/PPO** maximises reward minus a KL leash with four models; against a learned reward it over-optimises.
5. **DPO** uses the closed-form RLHF optimum to turn preferences into one supervised loss - no reward model, no RL loop.
6. **GRPO** replaces PPO's critic with a group baseline; with a verifier that is **RLVR**.
7. Real pipelines chain them: **SFT -> (DPO) -> RLVR** (Tlu 3's recipe).

### Exercises
- Change `p_correct` in `noisy_answer` to 0.05 (a much worse base model). Does GRPO still work from the base model? Which section predicts the answer?
- In `ppo_rlhf`, set `lam=0` and then `lam=1`. How does learning change?
- Run DPO with `flip=0.4` (very noisy labels). How does the reward model's AUC behave with the same noise?
- Make the verifier *partial credit* (reward the number of correct digits). Does RL learn faster? Could it also be hacked?

### Honest limits
A 2-layer GPT answering one-digit sums is a *microscope*, not a benchmark: it shows the mechanics and the relative behaviour of the methods, not their absolute performance at scale. The verifier here is perfect and the task is easy to check, which flatters RLVR; the preference labeler here is a noisy checker, not a human with taste, which the real RLHF settings add to. Numbers are means over three seeds and will move a little between machines.

Read it as: GRPO prefers the larger rate, PPO with the learned reward model the smaller, and PPO with the verifier is nearly indifferent:

# V-JEPA 2 from scratch 
### Self-supervised video model  action-conditioned world model  zero-shot robot planning

*Paper: **V-JEPA 2: Self-Supervised Video Models Enable Understanding, Prediction and Planning** - Assran, Bardes, Fan, Garrido, ... LeCun, Rabbat, Ballas (Meta FAIR, 2025), [arXiv:2506.09985](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.09985).*

This notebook builds V-JEPA 2 **piece by piece**, the way you would if you had to write it yourself. Every step has: **an intuition  the math (equation numbers are the paper's)  code you can run  a check that proves the code does what the text says.** Figures marked *"Fig. N (paper)"* are cropped from the paper itself; others come from the papers it builds on (ViT, MAE, I-JEPA, BYOL, RoPE, V-JEPA) or from Meta's MIT-licensed repo. All credit to the original authors.

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig1_overview.png" width="900"><br><sub>Fig. 1 (paper) - the whole story: pretrain on internet video, then either probe/align for understanding or post-train on 62 h of robot data for planning.</sub></p>

| step | question | what we build |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Why predict in *latent space* instead of pixels? | a measurable toy argument |
| 2 | How does a video become tokens? | 3D **tubelet** patch embedding (21616) |
| 3 | How does a transformer know *where/when* a token is? | **3D-RoPE** |
| 4 | What do we hide? | **multi-block masking** (full temporal extent, ~90 % in the paper) |
| 5 | The encoder | a ViT over *visible* tokens only; parameter counts of ViT-L/H/g vs the paper |
| 6 | The predictor | visible tokens + learnable **mask tokens**  predict masked features |
| 7 | The target and the loss | **EMA encoder, stop-grad, L1 (Eq. 1)**, and what happens without them |
| 8 | Train the whole thing | tiny V-JEPA on synthetic video, then *frozen* probes |
| 9 | What makes it scale | warmup-constant-cooldown LR, progressive resolution, data, model size |
| 10 | **V-JEPA 2-AC**: add actions | frozen encoder + block-causal action-conditioned predictor (Eqs. 2-4) |
| 11 | **Planning** | goal-image **energy** (Eq. 5) + Cross-Entropy Method + MPC |
| 12 | The paper's results, limits, and what came next | tables, figures, honest caveats |

**Two scales, kept honest.** *Paper scale* numbers (1 B parameters, 22 M videos, 62 h of Droid robot data) are quoted from the paper and marked as such. *Toy scale* experiments (0.5 M-parameter encoder, 4848 synthetic videos of a moving blob, CPU) are run here, so you can see each mechanism work end-to-end. Toy results demonstrate **mechanisms**, not the paper's accuracy numbers.

*Prerequisite:* what a transformer is. Plain PyTorch, CPU only.

## Step 1 - Why predict in latent space? 

**The goal (paper 1).** Learn a *world model* from video: something that can understand a scene, predict what happens next, and eventually help an agent plan. The obvious recipe is *generative*: predict the next frame's **pixels** (video diffusion, autoregressive pixel models, VideoMAE-style reconstruction).

**The problem.** A real video is full of detail that nobody - not even a perfect model - can predict: the exact sparkle on each leaf, sensor noise, the precise texture of grass. A pixel loss forces the model to spend capacity on it, and (with MSE) averages over it into blur. The paper says it directly: JEPA "focuses on learning representations for predictable aspects of a scene (e.g., the trajectory of an object in motion) while ignoring unpredictable details that generative objectives emphasize".

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_ijepa_fig2.png" width="880"><br><sub>Fig. 2 (I-JEPA, Assran et al. 2023) - (a) joint-embedding: compare two views; (b) generative: reconstruct y from x *in pixel space* using a decoder; (c) **joint-embedding predictive**: predict the *embedding* of y from x, with a latent variable z telling the predictor *which* part to predict. V-JEPA 2 is (c).</sub></p>

**Our toy world** (used for the whole notebook): a coloured Gaussian blob moves in a straight line (8 possible directions, random speed) over a textured background, with 2 static distractor blobs and **per-frame pixel noise**. The only *predictable, meaningful* thing is the blob's trajectory. Let's measure how little of the pixel signal that is.

**Reading this.** The learning signal that separates a model that understands the motion from one that doesn't is a *minority* of the pixel loss; the majority is noise floor that no model can remove (the numbers above). In real video the ratio is far worse (textures, lighting, compression). V-JEPA's answer: don't regress pixels; regress the **output of an encoder** that is itself being trained to keep what is predictable. We will *measure* in Step 8 that the trained encoder indeed throws the noise away.

> **V-JEPA vs V-JEPA 2.** V-JEPA (Bardes et al. 2024) introduced the video recipe. V-JEPA 2 keeps the objective and **scales it** (data 2 M22 M videos, model 300 M1 B params, longer training, higher resolution/longer clips - Step 9), then adds the action-conditioned stage (Steps 10-11).

## Step 2 - From video to tokens: tubelets 

A transformer eats a **sequence of vectors**. ViT (Dosovitskiy et al. 2020) cuts an image into square patches and linearly embeds each one:

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_vit_fig1.png" width="700"><br><sub>Fig. 1 (ViT) - split into fixed-size patches, embed linearly, add position information, run a transformer encoder.</sub></p>

For video, V-JEPA 2 does the same in 3D: it cuts the clip into **tubelets** of size `2  16  16` (T  H  W) - 2 consecutive frames  a 1616 pixel square - and embeds each with a *strided 3D convolution* (Table 12 in the paper: "Embedder: 2  16  16 strided conv"). A conv with kernel = stride = (2,16,16) is exactly "flatten each tubelet and apply one shared linear layer".

Why 2 frames per token? Neighbouring frames are nearly identical, so merging them halves the sequence length at almost no information cost; and the token itself already carries a little *motion* (difference between its two frames).

Our toy uses `2  8  8` tubelets on `8  48  48` clips  `4  6  6 = 144` tokens.

## Step 3 - Telling the transformer *where* and *when*: 3D-RoPE 

Self-attention is permutation-invariant: shuffle the tokens and nothing changes, so we must inject position. Older V-JEPA used absolute sin-cos embeddings **added** to the input. V-JEPA 2 uses **RoPE** (Su et al. 2021), which instead **rotates** the query and key vectors by an angle proportional to the position:

`q'_m = R(m) q_m`, `k'_n = R(n) k_n`    `q'_m  k'_n = q_m  R((nm)) k_n`

so the attention score depends only on the **relative offset** `n  m` - which is what you want for "the thing 2 patches to the left". The paper reports that 3D-RoPE "helps stabilize training for the largest models".

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_rope_fig1.png" width="760"><br><sub>Fig. 1 (RoFormer) - rotate each (x1,x2) pair of the query/key by m. Pairs use different  (frequencies).</sub></p>

**The 3D extension (paper 2.1):** split the feature dimension of each head into **three roughly equal segments** - one for time, one for height, one for width - and apply ordinary 1D RoPE to each segment using that axis' coordinate.

Our implementation: for head-dim `hd`, axis chunk `d = hd/3` (rounded to even), frequencies `_i = 10000^(2i/d)`.

## Step 4 - What to hide: multi-block masking 

The self-supervised task is **mask denoising in representation space** (paper 2.1): hide part of the video, and from what remains predict the *representation* of what was hidden.

Design question: *what* to hide? If you hide random single patches, a pixel in the next frame is trivially interpolated from its neighbours in space and time. V-JEPA uses **multi-block masks**: the *union of a few large rectangular blocks*, extended across **all frames** ("a large continuous block that covers the full temporal dimension limits information leakage due to the spatial and temporal redundancy of videos", Bardes et al.). The model must truly *infer* what happens inside the hole - including motion.

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_ijepa_fig4.png" width="520"><br><sub>Fig. 4 (I-JEPA) - the image version of the idea: a context block (2nd column) and several target blocks (right) whose representations must be predicted.</sub></p>

Paper hyper-parameters (Table 9): spatial block scale `[0.15, 0.7]`, aspect ratio `[0.75, 1.5]`, **temporal mask scale `[1.0, 1.0]`** (= full time), tubelet 2, patch 16. V-JEPA 1 (Bardes et al.) describes the two mask types: *short-range* = union of **8** blocks each covering ~15 % of the frame, *long-range* = union of **2** blocks covering ~70 %; together an average masking ratio of **~90 %**.

Our toy grid is only 66 patches, so we use a gentler `4` blocks  `20 %` (overlaps and the trimming below leave roughly a third of the tokens hidden). One more practical trick (also in the official code): every sample in a batch must have the **same number** of masked tokens so we can batch them - we randomly *un-mask* extra spatial cells (whole columns through time, so the hole stays identical in every frame).

## Step 5 - The encoder: a ViT that sees only what is visible 

Both the encoder `E_` and the predictor `P_` are plain **Vision Transformers**. A block is the standard pre-norm recipe:

`x  x + Attn(LN(x))` ; `x  x + MLP(LN(x))` with `MLP = Linear  GELU  Linear` (4 width) and **RoPE applied to q and k** inside attention.

Crucial efficiency trick (as in MAE): the encoder processes **only the visible tokens** (`Nv`), not the whole clip - with ~90 % masking in the paper that is ~10 cheaper per step. The positions of the visible tokens are carried by RoPE, so dropping tokens loses nothing about *where* the survivors are.

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_mae_fig1.png" width="560"><br><sub>Fig. 1 (MAE, He et al. 2021) - the same asymmetry: a big encoder sees only visible patches. (MAE reconstructs pixels with a decoder; V-JEPA regresses encoder features with a predictor.)</sub></p>

## Step 6 - The predictor: fill the hole with *mask tokens* 

The predictor `P_` receives
1. the encoder's outputs for the **visible** tokens (projected to the predictor's width),
2. one **learnable mask token** `y` for every **hidden** position - the same vector for all, so it carries *no content*, only a request: "tell me what is here".

Each token gets its RoPE position, so a mask token at `(t=2, h=3, w=1)` *knows where it is* and can attend to visible tokens around it in space and time. The predictor's outputs **at the mask positions** are projected back to the encoder's width and compared with targets. (Paper Fig. 2 left: "the outputs of the encoder are concatenated with a set of learnable mask tokens that specify the position of the masked patches".)

The predictor is much smaller than the encoder: **ViT-s (22 M)** next to a 300 M-1 B encoder (Table 12) - it only has to *route* information, the heavy lifting of "what is in this patch" is in the encoder.

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig2_training.png" width="820"><br><sub>Fig. 2 (paper), left half - V-JEPA 2 pre-training: encoder sees masked video; predictor gets encoder outputs + mask tokens z; target comes from an EMA copy of the encoder with stop-gradient; L1 loss. Right half (V-JEPA 2-AC) is Step 10.</sub></p>

## Step 7 - The target, the loss, and the collapse problem 

**Equation (1) of the paper:**

`minimize_{, , y}   P_(y, E_(x))  sg( E_(y) ) `

- `x` = the masked view, `y` = the full video; the loss is applied **only to the masked positions**.
- `sg()` = **stop-gradient**: the target is a constant for the optimiser.
- `` = an **exponential moving average** of the encoder weights: `  m + (1m)` (paper: `m = 0.99925`, fixed).
- **L1** rather than L2 (robust to outlier features; V-JEPA 1 noted it was more stable).
- *Not in the paper text but in the official code* (`app/vjepa/train.py`): the target features are passed through `F.layer_norm` (no affine) before the loss. We do the same.

**Why EMA + stop-grad? Collapse.** If target and prediction came from the *same network with gradients on both sides*, the trivial solution is `E(x) = constant`: the predictor then predicts the constant perfectly, loss = 0, and the encoder has learned nothing. The EMA teacher moves slowly and receives no gradient, so it cannot be dragged to a constant by the student; the student must chase a target that is a *lagged copy of itself*. (BYOL, Grill et al. 2020, found the same recipe; its architecture figure is below.)

<p align="center"><img src="images/r_byol_fig2.png" width="700"><br><sub>Fig. 2 (BYOL) - online network predicts the target network's projection; target = EMA of online weights, sg = stop-gradient. V-JEPA 2's Fig. 2 has the same shape.</sub></p>

## Step 8 - Train a tiny V-JEPA, then *freeze* it and test what it learned 

**Setup.** Encoder: 4 blocks, width 96 (0.48 M params). Predictor: 2 blocks, width 64. AdamW, cosine LR, EMA `m = 0.99` (the paper's 0.99925 is tuned for 252 K steps; with ~2 K steps it would barely move). A small fraction of batches (15 %) are **static "image" clips** (`T=2`, no motion), mirroring the paper's trick of treating images as repeated-frame clips so one model covers both - we will need this in Step 10 because V-JEPA 2-AC encodes single frames.

**Three arms.** (A) the real recipe: EMA teacher + stop-grad. (B) control *stop-grad only*: target = the current encoder, detached, no EMA. (C) control *neither*: target = the same encoder with gradients flowing through it. We track **feature spread** (`feat-std`: the standard deviation across tokens of the layer-normed features; 0 means all tokens look the same = **collapse**).

**Evaluation = the paper's protocol, miniaturised.** Freeze the encoder and train a small probe on its features to read out the blob's **direction of motion** (8 classes). Direction needs *several frames*, like Something-Something v2 in the paper ("motion understanding"). Baseline: a **randomly initialised** encoder with the same probe. Two probes: an *attentive probe* (learnable query cross-attending to tokens, as in the paper's 4-layer probe but 1 layer here) and a stricter *mean-pool + linear* probe.

### Did the encoder keep the *motion* and drop the *noise*? 

This is the claim from Step 1, measured. For the same scene we make two perturbations of the *input* and look at how far the representation moves:

- **noise re-draw**: identical scene and blob trajectory, fresh pixel noise (nothing meaningful changed);
- **motion change**: identical scene and noise, blob moves in the *opposite* direction (the meaningful change).

`sensitivity ratio = (change due to motion) / (change due to noise)`. In pixel space that ratio is small (noise dominates). A good representation makes it **larger**. We compare raw pixels, the random encoder, and the trained V-JEPA encoder (all after layer-norm so scales are comparable).

## Step 9 - What makes it *scale*: the V-JEPA 2 recipe 

The objective (Steps 4-7) is the same as V-JEPA 1. The paper's contribution in pre-training is a set of **four scaling ingredients** (2.1-2.4), each measured on the average of 6 frozen-probe tasks (SSv2, Diving-48, Jester, Kinetics, COIN, ImageNet) with a ViT-L baseline:

| ingredient | change | avg. accuracy (paper Fig. 3) |
|---|---|---|
| baseline V-JEPA 1 | ViT-L/16, 2 M videos, 90 K iters, 16 frames @ 256 | 84.2 |
| + **data** | 2 M  **22 M** videos (VideoMix22M) | 85.2 |
| + **model** | 300 M  **1 B** (ViT-L  ViT-g) | 86.7 |
| + **long training** | 90 K  **252 K** iterations | 87.5 |
| + **resolution/length** | 256  384 px and 16  **64 frames** | 88.2 |

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig3_scaling.png" width="430"><br><sub>Fig. 3 (paper) - each ingredient helps; total +4.0 points.</sub></p>

Two *engineering* ideas make the last two affordable:

1. **Warmup-constant-cooldown LR** instead of cosine. Train at a constant LR as long as you like; when you want a final model, branch off a *short cooldown*. Many cooldowns can start from checkpoints of one long run.
2. **Progressive resolution.** Do 95 % of training on cheap clips (16 frames, 256 px) and only raise frames/resolution *during the final 12 K-step cooldown*. The paper estimates training ViT-g at 64384384 throughout would need ~60 GPU-years.

Hyper-parameters straight from **Table 9**: warmup 12 000 steps `1e-4  5.25e-4`; constant `5.25e-4`; cooldown 12 000 steps `5.25e-4  1e-6`; weight decay 0.04; EMA 0.99925; global batch 3072.

**Data curation (paper 2.3).** The 1.4 M-hour YT-Temporal-1B is noisy, so they cluster scene embeddings (DINOv2) and *retrieve* scenes close to the target datasets (Kinetics, SSv2, COIN, Epic-Kitchens): **+1.4 points** over uncurated YT1B at ViT-L. **Images** (ImageNet) are added by repeating the image into a 16-frame static clip - exactly the `p_image` trick in our toy pre-training.

Now we have a strong *action-free* video encoder. Steps 10-11 turn it into something a robot can **plan** with.

## Step 10 - V-JEPA 2-AC: bolting actions onto a frozen world model 

V-JEPA 2 can "fill in" missing parts of a video but doesn't know **what an agent's action does**. Stage 2 (paper 3) learns exactly that, from only **~62 hours** of Droid robot video (23 k trajectories, success *and* failure, no rewards, no task labels):

- **Freeze** the V-JEPA 2 encoder `E`. Encode each frame independently: `z_k = E(x_k)` - for ViT-g a `16  16  1408` feature map.
- Train a **new predictor** `P_` (~300 M params: 24 layers, 16 heads, width 1024, GELU) that, given the history of *(action, end-effector state, frame features)*, predicts the **next frame's features** `_{k+1}`.
- Tokens are **interleaved** per time step: `[a_k, s_k, patches of z_k]` and the attention is **block-causal** - a token at time `k` can attend to everything at times ` k`, never the future.
- Action `a_k` = the *change* in 7-D end-effector state (3 position, 3 Euler angles, 1 gripper) between frames k and k+1; state `s_k` = the 7-D end-effector pose itself. 4-second clips at 4 fps = 16 frames.
- RoPE: patches get full 3D-RoPE; **action and state tokens only get the temporal rotation**.

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig6_ac_training.png" width="880"><br><sub>Fig. 6 (paper) - left: teacher-forcing loss (predict every next frame from the *true* history). Right: rollout loss (feed the predictor's own output back in, to reduce error accumulation).</sub></p>

**Losses (Eqs. 2-4).**

`L_teacher-forcing() = (1/T) _{k=1..T}  P_((a_t, s_t, E(x_t))_{tk})  E(x_{k+1}) `, `T = 15`

`L_rollout() =  P_(a_{1:T}, s_1, z_1)  z_{T+1} `, `T = 2` (gradient only through one recurrent step)

`L() = L_teacher-forcing + L_rollout`

**Our toy version.** The "robot" is the *same blob*, now moved by an action `a = (dx, dy)` (L1-norm  5 px) instead of by a hidden constant velocity; the state is its `(x, y)` position; the scene has the same noisy background and two static distractors. The encoder is the **frozen toy V-JEPA from Step 8**, applied to a *single* frame duplicated into a 2-frame clip - precisely why we mixed static clips into pre-training. Everything else (interleaving, block-causal attention, temporal-only RoPE for the action/state tokens, both losses) follows the paper.

**Does the world model actually use the action?** Held-out episodes, four comparisons of the error `  z` against the *true* next-frame features:

1. **ours**: predictor with the true actions;
2. **shuffled actions**: same inputs but actions permuted across the batch - if the model ignores actions this changes nothing;
3. **copy-last**: predict `z_{k+1} = z_k` ("nothing moves");
4. **noise floor**: re-encode the *same* scene and position with a fresh draw of pixel noise - the error nobody can avoid.

Then **autoregressive rollouts** for 1-4 steps (feeding predictions back in, states updated by `s  s + a`), because planning needs multi-step predictions and the paper warns that error accumulates.

## Step 11 - Planning: actions from a goal *image* 

Now the payoff. Given a **goal image** `x_g`, we don't train a policy; we **search** for actions by imagining. With current features `z_k`, state `s_k`, goal features `z_g = E(x_g)` and horizon `T`, define the **energy** (Eq. 5):

`E(_{1:T}; z_k, s_k, z_g) =  P_(_{1:T}; s_k, z_k)  z_g `

and solve `a* = argmin E` with the **Cross-Entropy Method** (Rubinstein 1997):

1. keep an independent Gaussian per action coordinate per time step, initialised **mean 0, variance 1**;
2. sample many action sequences, project each action into the **L1-ball** (paper: radius 0.075   13 cm per step, since larger actions are out-of-distribution);
3. score each by the energy (an imagined rollout of the world model);
4. refit the Gaussians to the **top-k** sequences; repeat a few iterations; return the **mean**.

Then **execute only the first action**, observe the new frame, and **re-plan** - *model-predictive control* with a receding horizon (so imperfect predictions get corrected by feedback).

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig7_planning.png" width="900"><br><sub>Fig. 7 (paper) - current frame and goal frame are encoded by the frozen encoder; the action-conditioned predictor imagines the future from sampled action sequences; the L1 distance between the imagined and goal features is the cost.</sub></p>

**Why latent planning is fast.** The world model predicts *features*, not pixels. The paper's Table 3: with 800 samples, 10 CEM iterations and horizon 1, V-JEPA 2-AC needs **16 s per action** vs **4 min** for the Cosmos video-diffusion world model (80 samples, 10 iterations, horizon 1) - and succeeds more often.

## Step 12 - What the paper reports (paper scale), limits, and what came next 

Everything above is a miniature. These are the **paper's own numbers** (all quoted from the paper's tables; nothing here is re-measured).

**Understanding** (frozen encoder + attentive probe, Table 4):

| model | params | SSv2 | Diving-48 | Jester | K400 | COIN | IN1K | avg |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DINOv2 | 1.1 B | 50.7 | 82.5 | 93.4 | 83.6 | 90.7 | 86.1 | 81.1 |
| PE-core G | 1.9 B | 55.4 | 76.9 | 90.0 | 88.5 | 95.3 | 87.6 | 82.3 |
| V-JEPA (v1) ViT-H | 0.6 B | 74.3 | 87.9 | 97.7 | 84.5 | 87.1 | 80.0 | 85.2 |
| V-JEPA 2 ViT-g | 1 B | 75.3 | 90.1 | 97.7 | 86.6 | 90.7 | 84.6 | 87.5 |
| V-JEPA 2 ViT-g384 | 1 B | **77.3** | **90.2** | **97.8** | 87.3 | 91.1 | 85.1 | **88.2** |

Strongest on **motion** (SSv2, Diving, Jester), competitive on **appearance** - exactly what a model trained to predict *dynamics* should be.

**Prediction** - Epic-Kitchens-100 action anticipation (recall@5, Table 5): V-JEPA 2 ViT-g384 reaches **39.7** on *action* (verb 63.6, noun 57.1) vs PlausiVL 27.6 and InAViT 25.8 - a 44 % relative improvement - using a frozen encoder+predictor and a probe.

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig11_ek100.png" width="820"><br><sub>Fig. 11 (paper) - EK100 anticipation: context frames  top-5 predicted actions  what happened 1 s later. Two successes, one failure.</sub></p>

**Video QA** (encoder aligned to an 8 B LLM, no language supervision in the encoder): PerceptionTest **84.0**, MVP **44.5**, TempCompass **76.9**, TemporalBench **36.7**, TOMATO **40.3**.

**Planning** (zero-shot, two labs not in Droid, Table 2): 

| task | Octo (avg of 2 labs) | V-JEPA 2-AC (avg of 2 labs) |
|---|---|---|
| Reach | 100 % | 100 % |
| Grasp cup / box | 15 % / 0 % | **65 % / 25 %** |
| Reach with object, cup / box | 15 % / 70 % | **75 % / 75 %** |
| Pick-and-place cup / box | 15 % / 10 % | **80 % / 65 %** |

<p align="center"><img src="images/p_fig10_pickplace.png" width="820"><br><sub>Fig. 10 (paper) - closed-loop pick-and-place with three goal images; highlighted frames mark when a sub-goal is reached and the next goal takes over.</sub></p>

**Limits the authors state (4.3):** (1) **camera sensitivity** - the model must infer the action axes from a monocular image; when the robot base isn't visible this is ill-posed, and they hand-tuned the camera position; (2) **long-horizon planning** - autoregressive errors accumulate and the search space grows exponentially with horizon, so pick-and-place needed *image sub-goals*; (3) **goals are images** - language goals would need aligning the latent world model with a language model.

**What came next.** The official repository's README (accessed while preparing this notebook) lists **V-JEPA 2.1** (dated 2026-03-16): a recipe with a *dense predictive loss* (all tokens, visible and masked, contribute), *deep self-supervision* at intermediate layers, and multi-modal tokenizers, aimed at higher-quality, temporally consistent **dense** features. We did not read that paper; the image below is the repo's own PCA teaser.

<p align="center"><img src="images/web_vjepa21_pca.png" width="820"><br><sub>V-JEPA 2.1 PCA feature visualisation, from facebookresearch/vjepa2 (MIT licence). Not covered by this notebook's experiments.</sub></p>

## The whole thing in one page

```
STAGE 1  pre-train (no actions, no labels)                          STAGE 2  post-train (62 h robot video, encoder frozen)
video tubelets 2x16x16 tokens mask 90 % ENCODER(visible)       frames  frozen ENCODER  z_k
                                                                     [a_k, s_k, z_k] interleaved  PREDICTOR (block-causal)  _{k+1}
        PREDICTOR(visible feats + mask tokens)  _masked              loss = |_{k+1}-z_{k+1}|  (teacher forcing) + 2-step rollout
        target = sg( LN( EMA-ENCODER(full video) ) )                  PLAN: argmin_a |P(a; z, s) - z_goal|  by CEM, execute first action, repeat
        loss = L1 on masked tokens only
```

### Key takeaways
1. **Predict in representation space.** The target is another encoder's output, so the model is free to ignore unpredictable pixel detail (Step 1, and the noise-vs-motion measurement in Step 8).
2. **Collapse is the central failure mode**; EMA teacher + stop-gradient (+ layer-normed targets) is what keeps it away (Step 7-8).
3. **Mask big, mask all frames.** Large multi-block holes through the whole clip force real inference about motion (Step 4).
4. **Scaling = data  model  schedule  resolution**, made affordable by warmup-constant-cooldown and a *late* resolution increase (Step 9).
5. **A world model is a predictor you can search through.** Freeze the encoder, learn `P(next features | history, action)` from little data, then plan by minimising a feature-space distance to a goal image (Steps 10-11).

### Exercises
1. Replace L1 with L2 in `jepa_loss` and compare `feat-std` and the probe accuracy.
2. Raise the mask ratio (more/larger blocks) and lower it (random tubes: mask individual tokens). Which gives the better frozen probe?
3. Remove layer-norm from the target (Step 7). Does the loss scale matter? Does collapse come back?
4. In Step 10 drop the rollout loss (Eq. 3). Does the 4-step error grow faster?
5. Planning: increase the horizon to 6 with the same CEM budget. How does success change? Relate it to the paper's "long-horizon planning" limitation.
6. Replace the blob-position action with a *noisy* action effect (random slippage) and see how CEM+MPC's closed loop compensates.

### Honest limits of this notebook
- **Toy scale.** A 0.5 M-parameter encoder on synthetic 4848 videos of one moving blob. It demonstrates mechanisms (masking, EMA, collapse, block-causal AC predictor, CEM+MPC), **not** the paper's accuracy, and none of the paper's results are reproduced here.
- Hyper-parameters differ from the paper where scale demands it: tubelet `288`, mask  one third (paper  90 %), EMA 0.99 (paper 0.99925), equal-size mask trimming across the batch, batch 32 (paper 3072).
- The 7-D end-effector state/action is replaced by a 2-D position/displacement; the encoder sees a single frame duplicated into a 2-frame clip, and planning uses a fixed small L1 radius in pixels.
- Probe results here use a **single seed** and small test sets; differences of a few points are within noise. Collapse/no-collapse is the robust effect.
- The FLOP comparison in Step 9 is a back-of-envelope estimate (no masking, no hardware effects); the paper's own GPU-time figures should be preferred.
- The target layer-norm and the equal-size-mask trick are taken from the official code, not from the paper text. The V-JEPA 2.1 paragraph is based only on the repository README.

- Dosovitskiy et al. 2020, *ViT*, arXiv:2010.11929  He et al. 2021, *MAE*, arXiv:2111.06377  Su et al. 2021, *RoFormer (RoPE)*, arXiv:2104.09864  Grill et al. 2020, *BYOL*, arXiv:2006.07733  Rubinstein 1997, *Cross-Entropy Method*.
- Code: github.com/facebookresearch/vjepa2 (MIT). Figures credited in their captions.

## 1. Shared backbone  LGT transformer

All four generation paradigms run on the same Local-Global Transformer (LGT) or standard GPT backbone. Autoregressive uses GPT; all diffusion/flow variants use LGT because they need bidirectional context.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph LGT["LGT layer (Local-Global Transformer)"]
    H_IN["hidden states h  [B, S, D]"]

    subgraph LOCAL["Local attention heads  (nl heads)"]
      LNorm["RMSNorm"]
      LATTN["MHA within a window of size w\n(e.g. w = 32 tokens)\nRoPE with local frequency _local"]
      LOUT["local context  [B, S, D_l]"]
      LNorm --> LATTN --> LOUT
    end

    subgraph GLOBAL["Global attention heads  (ng heads)"]
      GNorm["RMSNorm"]
      GATTN["MHA over full sequence\nRoPE with global frequency _global\n(nl + ng = n_heads total)"]
      GOUT["global context  [B, S, D_g]"]
      GNorm --> GATTN --> GOUT
    end

    MERGE["concat(local, global)  Linear  [B, S, D]"]
    ADD1["residual add"]

    subgraph FFN_L["FFN sub-layer"]
      FNorm["RMSNorm"]
      FFNMOD["GeGLU: gate(x)  up(x)  down\nor MoE (configurable)"]
      FOUT["[B, S, D]"]
      FNorm --> FFNMOD --> FOUT
    end

    ADD2["residual add"]
    H_OUT["h_out  [B, S, D]"]

    H_IN --> LOCAL & GLOBAL
    LOUT & GOUT --> MERGE --> ADD1
    ADD1 --> FNorm
    FOUT --> ADD2 --> H_OUT
  end
```

---

## 2. Four generation paradigms in detail

### 2a. Autoregressive (reference baseline)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph AR_TRAIN["Training"]
    TOKENS["token sequence x x  x"]
    CAUSAL["causal mask\n(each position attends\nonly to previous tokens)"]
    LOGITS["logits p(x | xx)\nfor all positions in parallel"]
    CE_LOSS["cross-entropy loss\naverage over non-padding tokens"]
    TOKENS --> CAUSAL --> LOGITS --> CE_LOSS
  end

  subgraph AR_SAMPLE["Sampling"]
    START["prefix tokens"]
    STEP["sample x ~ softmax(logits[-1] / )\n(temperature , top-k, top-p)"]
    APPEND["append x to sequence"]
    START --> STEP --> APPEND
    APPEND -->|"repeat until EOS or max_len"| STEP
  end
```

### 2b. Masked diffusion (absorbing-state)

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph MD_TRAIN["Training"]
    X1["clean tokens xx"]
    T_SAMP["sample noise level t ~ U(0,1)"]
    MASK_PROB["mask probability m(t)\n(e.g. cosine schedule)"]
    XT["x_t: replace each token\nwith [MASK] with prob m(t)"]
    BIDIR["bidirectional attention\n(all positions see all others)"]
    UNMASK_LOGITS["p(x_i | x_t)  for every masked i"]
    MASK_LOSS["cross-entropy on masked positions only\nwith masking weight 1/m(t)"]
    X1 & T_SAMP --> MASK_PROB --> XT --> BIDIR --> UNMASK_LOGITS --> MASK_LOSS
  end

  subgraph MD_SAMPLE["Sampling (MDLM schedule)"]
    ALLMASKED["x = [MASK][MASK]\n(all positions masked)"]
    SCORE["score all masked positions\nvia one forward pass"]
    SELECT["unmask fraction 1/T tokens\n(highest-confidence predictions)"]
    ALLMASKED --> SCORE --> SELECT
    SELECT -->|"repeat T steps"| SCORE
  end
```

### 2c. Block diffusion

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph BD_TRAIN["Training"]
    SEQBD["xx split into blocks\nof size block_size B"]
    BLOCK_K["select block k at random"]
    PREV["xx_{kB-1} committed\n(prefix, visible via block-causal mask)"]
    MASKED_BLK["x_{kB}x_{(k+1)B-1} fully masked"]
    BLKCAUSAL["block-causal attention:\nprefix tokens see each other\nmasked block sees prefix + itself"]
    PRED["p(x_i | prefix, x_t_block)\nfor i in masked block"]
    BLKLOSS["cross-entropy on masked block tokens"]
    SEQBD --> BLOCK_K
    PREV & MASKED_BLK --> BLKCAUSAL --> PRED --> BLKLOSS
  end

  subgraph BD_SAMPLE["Sampling (left to right over blocks)"]
    COMMIT["committed = []"]
    NEXT_BLK["denoise next block via T diffusion steps\n(bidirectional within block,\nblock-causal to committed prefix)"]
    APPEND_BLK["append denoised block to committed"]
    COMMIT --> NEXT_BLK --> APPEND_BLK
    APPEND_BLK -->|"repeat over blocks"| NEXT_BLK
  end
```

### 2d. Discrete flow matching

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph FM_TRAIN["Training"]
    X0_FM["x ~ uniform over vocab\n(noisy distribution)"]
    X1_FM["x = ground truth tokens\n(target distribution)"]
    T_FM["t ~ U(0, 1)"]
    XT_FM["x_t: straight-line interpolation\np_t(y) = (1-t)p(y) + tp(y)\n(per-token token mixture)"]
    TARGET_FM["target velocity\nv*(y) = p(y) - p(y)"]
    PRED_FM["model predicts v_(x_t, t)\nvia full-sequence forward pass"]
    FLOW_LOSS["MSE or KL(v_, v*)"]
    X0_FM & X1_FM & T_FM --> XT_FM --> TARGET_FM & PRED_FM --> FLOW_LOSS
  end

  subgraph FM_SAMPLE["Sampling (Euler integration)"]
    P0["p = uniform distribution"]
    EULER["for i in 0T:\n  dt = 1/T\n  v = model(p_t, t_i)\n  p_{t+dt} = p_t + v  dt\n  (re-normalise to simplex)"]
    P1_OUT["p  data distribution\n sample token from p"]
    P0 --> EULER --> P1_OUT
  end
```

---

## 3. Token backbone  ids to logits

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  IDS["token ids  [B, S]"]
  EMB["Token embedding\n50,257  D_model\n(GPT-2 tokenizer)"]
  STACK["LGT or GPT transformer stack\nN layers, D_model=384, 8 heads, D_ff=1536"]
  LM_HEAD["LM head\nLinear D_model  50,257\n(often tied with embedding weights)"]
  LOGITS["logits  [B, S, 50,257]"]

  IDS --> EMB --> STACK --> LM_HEAD --> LOGITS
```

---

## 4. Loss functions

```mermaid
flowchart TB
  subgraph AR_LOSS["Autoregressive loss"]
    AR_L["CE( logits[:, :-1], tokens[:, 1:] )\nshift by 1  predict next token\naverage over non-padding positions"]
  end
  subgraph MD_LOSS_F["Masked diffusion loss"]
    MD_L["CE( p(x_i | x_t) , x_i ) for masked i only\n reweighting 1 / m(t)\n(upweights highly masked steps)"]
  end
  subgraph BD_LOSS_F["Block diffusion loss"]
    BD_L["CE( p(x_i | prefix, x_t_block) , x_i )\nfor tokens i in the masked block only"]
  end
  subgraph FM_LOSS_F["Flow matching loss"]
    FM_L["MSE( v_(x_t, t) , p  p )\nor token-level KL(v_ || v*)\nover all positions"]
  end
```

## 5. Visualiser  block diffusion GIF

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  CKPT["checkpoint (latest or named run)"]
  PROMPT["prompt text (prefix)"]
  MODEL_VIZ["load model + config"]
  SAMPLE_VIZ["run block diffusion sampling\nrecord partial sequences at each\ndenoising step of each block"]
  FRAMES["list of partial sequences"]
  RENDER["render each frame:\nprompt = steelblue\ncommitted tokens = orange\nactive block = black on light overlay\nmasked positions = dimmed"]
  GIF["output.gif\n(one frame per denoising step)"]

  CKPT & PROMPT --> MODEL_VIZ --> SAMPLE_VIZ --> FRAMES --> RENDER --> GIF
```

## 6. Config inheritance and variant switching

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph BASE["configs/base.yaml"]
    B1["d_model: 384\nn_layers: 16\nn_heads: 8\nd_ff: 1536\nmax_length: 128\narch: lgt\ntokenizer: gpt2\ndataset: wikitext2\nstride: 10"]
  end

  subgraph VARIANTS["Variant configs (one field change each)"]
    V1["autoregressive/small.yaml\n  inherits: base.yaml\n  variant: autoregressive\n  arch: transformer\n  attention_mask: causal"]
    V2["diffusion/small.yaml\n  inherits: base.yaml\n  variant: diffusion\n  attention_mask: bidirectional"]
    V3["block_diffusion/small.yaml\n  inherits: base.yaml\n  variant: block_diffusion\n  attention_mask: block_causal\n  block_size: 16"]
    V4["flow_matching/small.yaml\n  inherits: base.yaml\n  variant: flow_matching\n  attention_mask: bidirectional"]
  end

  BASE --> V1 & V2 & V3 & V4
```
